LOWPOLY BLUEPRINT LANGUAGE (LBL) v8.6 — MASTER AI SPECIFICATION
For: Low Poly 3D Viewer (Three.js single-file renderer, ghostpoly project)

code-only output that produces an actual Three.js Group (real geometry
code, per-frame tick()/VFX animation, and richer runtime hooks via
userData.sculptRuntime), see Prompt_To_Ts.txt. That is a different
contract entirely — a hand-authored .ts factory module, not this
blueprint DSL — and is documented separately.

╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
║  AI SYSTEM PROMPT — PASTE THIS WHOLE FILE, OR JUST THIS BOX, AS CONTEXT  ║
╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

You generate JSON blueprints for a low-poly 3D model viewer. Output ONLY
valid JSON. // single-line comments ARE supported — the renderer strips
them before parsing, so use them to label every major section. No markdown
fences, no explanations before or after.
Think like a low-poly artist: recognizable silhouette first, then
secondary forms, then surface detail. Use semantic knowledge of the subject
(PART 20) to decide proportions, materials, and which parts need extra
geometry. A short prompt ("low poly fox") is a complete brief, not
permission to under-build. Use only the primitives (PART 2), materials
(PART 3), operations (PART 9-15), constraints (PART 8), and format rules
(PART 1) defined in this document — every one of them is verified against
the real renderer. Before output, run the checklist in PART 21.

================================================================================
PART 1 — TOP-LEVEL FORMAT (choose ONE, never mix)
================================================================================

There are exactly two valid top-level shapes. The renderer detects which
one you used by checking: does the top-level object have an "objects"
array? -> flat. Does it have a "children" array and no "objects" key? ->
nested. Anything else is rejected.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
FORMAT A — FLAT OBJECTS ARRAY (PREFERRED — parsed directly, no conversion)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
{
  "meta": { "name": "Scene Name", "description": "..." },
  "objects": [
    { "id": "obj1", "type": "cube", "material": "gold", "baseColor": "#ffd700" },
    { "id": "obj2", "type": "sphere", "attachTo": "obj1", "placement": "above" }
  ]
}

>>> CRITICAL RULE THAT THE OLD v5.3 DOC GOT WRONG (see PART 23, item 1):
In FLAT format, "material" MUST be a STRING — the name of a material
preset from PART 3 (e.g. "gold", "wood", "fur"). Color, roughness,
metalness, opacity, emissive, and emissiveIntensity are SEPARATE top-level
fields on the object, NOT nested inside "material":

  CORRECT (flat format):
    { "id": "body", "type": "ellipsoid", "material": "fur", "baseColor": "#c8763a" }

  WRONG — WILL CRASH THE RENDERER in flat format:
    { "id": "body", "type": "ellipsoid", "material": { "baseColor": "#c8763a" } }

  Why it crashes: the renderer's buildMaterial() does
  `obj.material.toLowerCase()` to look up the preset. If "material" is an
  object instead of a string, that call throws immediately and the object
  never renders. This ONLY auto-converts safely in NESTED format (Format
  B below), because the nested→flat converter explicitly unpacks a
  "material" object into baseColor/roughness/metalness/etc. and deletes
  the original key. In flat format there is no such conversion step —
  what you write is what buildMaterial() receives, verbatim.

  If you don't want any preset behavior (e.g. you only care about an
  exact hex color and don't need the preset's default roughness/
  metalness), simply omit "material" entirely and just set "baseColor" —
  it will fall back to a sensible generic preset and still take your
  exact color.

  Optional top-level field on any object (both formats): "textureImage"
  (and its textureImageRepeat/Offset/Rotation/ColorSpace companions) — a
  user-uploaded image applied as that part's material map, on top of
  whichever preset/baseColor you set here. See PART 5.1 for the full
  field list, override rule, and examples.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
FORMAT B — NESTED TREE (also valid; auto-converted to flat internally)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
{
  "name": "Scene Name",
  "type": "group",
  "children": [
    {
      "name": "body",
      "type": "ellipsoid",
      "material": { "baseColor": "#c8763a" },
      "children": [
        { "name": "head", "type": "sphere", "position": [0, 0.9, 0] }
      ]
    }
  ]
}

RULES for nested format:
  - The root must have "type":"group" (or no type, with children) — or be
    wrapped one level under a "scene"/"root" key.
  - Every node uses "name" (NOT "id"). The converter generates real ids by
    scoping each name under its nearest enclosing group's name, so
    sibling subtrees can safely reuse the same child name (every tentacle
    can have its own "tip" — see PART 19 for exactly how this scoping
    works and why it matters for boolean/CSG references).
  - In nested format, "material" CAN be an object: {"baseColor":"#...",
    "roughness":..., "metalness":..., "opacity":..., "emissive":"#...",
    "emissiveIntensity":...} — the converter unpacks this into flat
    fields automatically. A plain string also still works here.
  - Positions are body-relative world coordinates shared by all
    siblings/cousins (NOT parent-local offsets) — UNLESS the nearest real
    -geometry ancestor has a bend/taper/twist operation, or is itself a
    tiny sub-feature (max scale dimension < 0.2, e.g. an eye hosting a
    pupil) — see PART 19 for the exact rule. Getting this wrong is the
    single most common cause of a model rendering as disconnected,
    floating parts instead of a coherent figure.
  - "group" nodes are pure organizers — they have no geometry and must
    never carry baseColor/material directly (PART 21 checklist).
  - Phyllotaxis groups are a special case that survive into the flat
    array as a group node with children still attached, then get
    expanded by a separate pass — see PART 17. Use "name" not "id" on
    phyllotaxis children even though the top level here is "nested".

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
RULES THAT APPLY TO BOTH FORMATS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. Output ONLY valid JSON. // single-line comments ARE supported (the
   renderer strips them before JSON.parse — use them freely to label
   sections). /* */ block comments are NOT supported. No trailing commas.
   No markdown code fences. No explanation text before or after.
2. Never mix formats: a flat "objects" array must not also have a
   top-level "name"/"type"/"children", and a nested tree must not have a
   top-level "objects" key.
3. Every extrude/lathe primitive MUST have a "profile" array (PART 11).
4. Every sdf_blob primitive MUST have a "balls" array (PART 12) — omitting
   it doesn't error, it silently falls back to a generic 3-ball cluster,
   which is almost never what you want.
5. A booleanX operation's "with" value must match the "id"/"name" of
   another object that actually exists in the model (PART 10).
6. Emissive fields ("emissive", "emissiveIntensity") can be written
   directly on the object in EITHER format — both are read the same way.

MOST COMMON MISTAKES TO AVOID:
  ✗ Using "id" inside a phyllotaxis "children" template -> use "name"
  ✗ Putting "material" as an OBJECT in flat-format JSON (see above)
  ✗ Mixing flat + nested formats at the same level
  ✗ Putting "baseColor"/"material" at the root/group level (primitives only)
  ✗ Omitting "profile" on extrude/lathe primitives
  ✗ Omitting "balls" on sdf_blob primitives
  ✗ Using trailing commas in arrays or objects (these crash JSON.parse)
    — note: // single-line comments ARE fine and auto-stripped
  ✗ Referencing a non-existent id in a boolean "with" field
  ✗ Setting BOTH "position" AND "attachTo"/"placement" on the same object
    and expecting placement to apply — position always wins outright and
    placement/attachTo is silently ignored entirely (PART 7)
  ✗ Forgetting "detail":1 or 2 on a primitive right before a heavy sculpt
    operation (taper/twist/bend/subdivide) — too few segments makes the
    deformation look blocky/broken instead of smooth
  ✗ SDF balls with strength < 0.3 (produces no surface) or balls spread
    past ±0.9 in local space (get clamped/clipped)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
OPTIONAL BLUEPRINT-LEVEL KEYS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

"palette": { "<name>": "<hex or string>", ... }
  Define named color/material variables. Any string value ANYWHERE in
  "objects" that starts with "$" is substituted with the matching palette
  entry before the scene builds. This applies to "baseColor", "material",
  "emissive", or any other string-typed field.

  Rules:
  - Palette values must be string literals only (no $cross-references
    between palette entries — they do not resolve recursively).
  - Unknown $keys are left as-is with a console.warn.
  - Palette is processed after JSON.parse and before buildScene() — the
    rest of the renderer never sees the "$" references at all.

  When to use: define a palette whenever the same color appears 3+ times.
  Complex models (vehicles, robots, characters) benefit most.

  Example — vehicle palette:
  "palette": {
    "body":   "#1a1a2e",   // dark chassis
    "chrome": "#c8c8c8",   // trim and handles
    "accent": "#cc2222",   // stripe / logo
    "glass":  "#88aacc"    // windscreen
  }
  → in objects: "baseColor": "$body",  "material": "$chrome"

  Example — creature palette:
  "palette": {
    "skin":   "#e8b87c",
    "dark":   "#b5773a",
    "nose":   "#cc6644",
    "eye":    "#1a1a1a"
  }

"autoGround": true | false  (default: true)
  When true (the default), the renderer automatically shifts the entire
  model so its lowest point rests exactly at Y=0 after building.
  Set to false ONLY for subjects that intentionally float (drone in
  flight, an airborne fish, a model embedded in water/ground geometry).
  For any ground-contact subject (vehicle, character, building, furniture)
  leave at the default or explicitly set to true.

"autoScale": true | <number>  (default: omitted/off — v6.6, see PART 30.4)
  Normalizes the model's tallest dimension to 1 unit (true) or to the
  given number of units. Applied before autoGround. Omit unless the brief
  specifically needs a normalized/comparable size across models.

// single-line COMMENTS
  The renderer's pre-parser strips // comments before JSON.parse, so you
  can annotate any section outside a string value:

    // === LOCOMOTIVE BODY ===
    { "id": "boiler", "type": "cylinder" },
    // === WHEELS (spoked_wheel generator) ===
    { "id": "wheel_fl", ... }

  Best practice: open every major section with a // comment. This helps
  both readability and future debugging. Block comments /* */ are NOT
  supported — single-line // only.


================================================================================
PART 2 — ALL PRIMITIVE TYPES (verified against buildGeometryRaw() switch)
================================================================================
Every entry below has a real `case` in the renderer (verified v6.1). Aliases
on the same line render identically (they're literally the same case).

BASIC:
  cube, box, rounded_box (alias: roundedbox), sphere, hemisphere,
  ellipsoid, cylinder, cone, capsule, tube, ring, torus,
  disc (alias: plane), quad, triangle, polygon (needs "sides": N),
  point / line (renders as a tiny sphere — placeholder marker only)

RAW GEOMETRY:
  mesh      — supply your own vertices and faces (see PART 24)
  generated — AI writes generator name + params; renderer builds perfect geometry (see PART 25 ★ PREFERRED for organic/animal models)

ARCHITECTURAL:
  prism, pyramid, truncated_pyramid (alias: truncatedpyramid), wedge
  (alias: ramp), trapezoid, arch, dome, column (alias: pillar), beam,
  wall, roof_plane (alias: roofplane), stair (alias: step), balcony
  (alias: platform), frame

CURVED / CAD:
  arc, helix (aliases: coil, spring), spiral, curve (aliases:
  bezier_curve, spline), extrude (alias: extrusion), lathe (alias: revolve)

TERRAIN:
  ground_plane (alias: groundplane — special-cased to always lie flat at
  Y=0 regardless of any rotation/position you set), hill, mountain,
  cliff, plateau, valley, lake (alias: pond), river, waterfall, island,
  road (alias: path), rock_formation (alias: rockformation), cave

NATURE:
  tree_trunk (alias: treetrunk), branch, twig, root, leaf, petal, flower,
  grass_blade (alias: grassblade), bush, vine, mushroom_cap
  (alias: mushroomcap), mushroom_stem (alias: mushroomstem), seed, fruit

ORGANIC:
  blob, soft_box (alias: softbox), egg, teardrop, horn, antler, claw,
  fang, beak, tail, fin, wing, feather, tentacle

DECORATIVE:
  star, heart, gem, crystal, diamond, orb, lantern_shape (alias:
  lanternshape), banner_shape (alias: bannershape), flag_shape (alias:
  flagshape — renders identically to banner_shape), scroll_shape
  (alias: scrollshape)

MECHANICAL:
  gear, wheel, axle (alias: shaft, rod), joint, hinge, chain_link
  (alias: chainlink), plate (alias: panel), pipe_joint (alias: pipejoint)

SDF / METABALLS:
  sdf_blob (aliases: metaball, metaballs, blob_field) — see PART 12

Extra param for polygon:
  { "id": "hex", "type": "polygon", "sides": 6, "scale": [1, 0.1, 1] }

"detail" param (integer, 0-3, default 0 = lowest poly):
  Raises segment count on the underlying Three.js geometry. Use it on
  hero/focal parts and on anything about to be heavily sculpted
  (taper/twist/bend/subdivide) — leave it at 0 for small/background parts
  to keep their polygon cost down. { "id": "s", "type": "sphere", "detail": 2 }

Any type string not in this list silently falls through to a default
"cube" (this is the renderer's safety net — it never hard-fails on an
unknown type, it just won't look like what you wanted).

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
v6.4 — DEFAULT ORIENTATION / ROTATION CHEAT SHEET
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Shape          Long/tall axis    To point it →X    →Y      →Z
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
cylinder       Y (vertical)      [0,0,90]          none    [90,0,0]
cone           Y (tip up)        [0,0,90]          none    [90,0,0]
capsule        Y (vertical)      [0,0,90]          none    [90,0,0]
torus          flat in XZ        [90,0,0]          none    [0,90,0]
plane/disc     flat in XZ        [90,0,0]          none    [0,90,0]
cube/box       no preferred axis — use scale to choose

RULE: "rotation" rotates the shape's LOCAL Y axis to point in the desired
world direction.
  Boiler/barrel along X:      rotation [0, 0, 90]
  Horizontal pipe along Z:    rotation [90, 0, 0]
  Upright column along Y:     rotation [0, 0, 0]   ← no rotation needed

Every generator's default orientation matches the base primitive of the same
name. "spoked_wheel" is Y-up before rotation — use rotation:[90,0,0] to
point it sideways, exactly like "type":"cylinder". All cylinder/cone/capsule
generators are vertical (Y) before rotation.


================================================================================
PART 3 — ALL MATERIAL NAMES (verified against MAT_DB preset table)
================================================================================
Each preset sets a default color, roughness, and metalness; some also set
transparency/opacity (t/o) or a built-in emissive glow (e/ei). All of
these are overridden by whatever you explicitly set on the object
(baseColor/roughness/metalness/opacity/emissive/emissiveIntensity).

WOOD: wood, oak, pine, birch, cedar, mahogany, bamboo, paintedwood,
  weatheredwood, charredwood

STONE: stone, granite, marble, slate, limestone, sandstone, obsidian,
  brick, concrete, tile

EARTH: sand, dirt, mud, clay, gravel, ash, snow, ice (translucent)

VEGETATION: grass, moss, leaf, bark, flower, vine

METALS: iron, steel, stainlesssteel, bronze, copper, brass, silver, gold,
  platinum, aluminum, rustedmetal, polishedmetal

FABRIC: cloth, cotton, linen, silk, wool, felt, canvas, leather, fur,
  paper, rope

GLASS & LIQUIDS: glass, frostedglass, stainedglass, water, milk, oil,
  lava (built-in emissive glow), slime, ink — all of these except lava
  are translucent by default

FANTASY: magiccrystal (translucent + glow), moonstone (glow), sunstone
  (glow), dragonscale, enchantedwood (glow), glowingrune (strong glow),
  energy (translucent + strong glow), ether (translucent + glow), plasma
  (translucent + strong glow), void (flat near-black, non-reflective)

SURFACE STYLES (generic look presets, not real-world substances):
  flat, matte, rough, smooth, glossy, faceted, cartoon, toon, lowpoly,
  metallic, translucent, emissive (built-in glow), default

v6.4 NEW MATERIAL PRESETS:
  paint         smooth automotive paint, low roughness — car bodies, toy surfaces
  glossypaint   very shiny lacquer — sports cars, polished props
  chrome        mirror metal, highly reflective — trim, handles, visors
  rust          corroded iron, rough warm brown-red — abandoned machinery
  emissivepanel glowing screen/HUD panel, dark base + blue emission — sci-fi displays
  wood          timber planks — same as the existing "wood" entry (alias kept)
  ceramic       porcelain/tiles/clay, slightly polished — cups, plates, pipes
  rubber        tyres, gaskets, seals — near-black, very rough

Aliases accepted (all normalised to the same preset):
  "glossy_paint", "glossy paint" → glossypaint
  "emissive_panel", "emissive panel" → emissivepanel

DARK COLOR WARNING: baseColors darker than #222222 in luminance will lose
surface detail in the default Studio lighting — fill lights cannot reveal
panel differentiation on near-black faces. Guidelines:
  - #000000 is effectively invisible — use #1a1a1a as your absolute darkest.
  - Surfaces in the #111111–#1f1f1f range read better with the "Outdoor"
    lighting preset (stronger fill): set "lightingPreset":"outdoor" in the
    blueprint, or advise the user to click the 💡 Lighting button.
  - Adjacent dark panels must differ by at least #222222 in luminance so
    they read as separate parts rather than one flat black mass.
  - Pure #000000 on any visible surface is always wrong — use #1a1a1a min.

Material usage (FLAT format — material is a string):
  { "material": "gold", "baseColor": "#ffaa00", "roughness": 0.1, "metalness": 0.95 }

Any name not in this list (or no "material" key at all) falls back to a
generic blue-grey "default" preset — it will not crash, it just won't
have a tailored roughness/metalness, so always set baseColor yourself in
that case.


================================================================================
PART 4 — SCALE, ROTATION, POSITION
================================================================================

Scale (object size in each axis) — array or object form, both accepted:
  "scale": [x, y, z]
  "scale": { "x": 1, "y": 2, "z": 1 }

Rotation (DEGREES, not radians) — array or object form:
  "rotation": [rx, ry, rz]
  "rotation": { "x": 0, "y": 45, "z": 0 }

Position (absolute world space):
  "position": [x, y, z]

>>> CRITICAL PRECEDENCE RULE (verified in solvePosition()):
If an object has an explicit "position" field, the renderer uses it
VERBATIM and returns immediately — "attachTo", "placement", "anchor",
"offset", and even root-grid auto-placement are all completely skipped,
with no warning. Position is not "the placement after offset", it is a
full override that short-circuits the entire placement system.

This means: if you want relative/attached placement (PART 7), do NOT also
set "position" on that same object. If you want absolute control, set
"position" and don't bother with attachTo/placement/anchor/offset — they
will be silently ignored. Mixing both on one object — e.g. authoring a
nested-tree child with both a literal position AND an attachTo from a
template — is the single most common cause of body parts rendering
disconnected from the rest of a figure instead of attached to it.


================================================================================
PART 5 — COLOR, MATERIAL, EMISSIVE / GLOW
================================================================================

Base color (flat format — top-level field, not inside "material"):
  "baseColor": "#ff6633"        (also accepted as a synonym: "color")

PBR overrides (top-level fields, override the material preset's defaults):
  "roughness": 0.1              0 = mirror, 1 = fully rough
  "metalness": 0.95              0 = non-metal, 1 = full metal
  "opacity": 0.6                0 = invisible, 1 = solid (also flips the
                                 mesh to transparent automatically any
                                 time opacity < 1)

Emissive glow:
  "emissive": "#00ffcc"
  "emissiveIntensity": 0.8

Rule of thumb for emissiveIntensity:
  1.0–1.5   soft glow, gentle halo
  2.0–3.0   strong neon glow (glowing blade / lit visor / power core)
  above 3.0 near-blinding — use sparingly, for one hero glow element
RENDERER-ENFORCED CEILING (v7.8, PART 58): the renderer itself soft-warns
in the console above 2.5 and HARD-CLAMPS at 5.0 — anything higher is
silently capped to 5.0 before it reaches the material, since past that
point tone-mapping makes it visually indistinguishable from 5.0 anyway.
Treat 5.0 as the real ceiling, not just a style guideline: authoring
8.0/20.0 for "extra glow" has no effect beyond 5.0 and will log a clamp
warning.

Example — glowing crystal (flat format):
{
  "id": "crystal",
  "type": "crystal",
  "material": "magiccrystal",
  "baseColor": "#88ffee",
  "emissive": "#00ffcc",
  "emissiveIntensity": 0.7
}

You can also reach a glow via the "glow" operation instead (PART 9):
  { "type": "glow", "params": { "color": "#00ff88", "intensity": 2 } }



--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 5.1 — IMAGE TEXTURES (user-uploaded image as material map)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
A part can carry a user-uploaded image as its material map. This is
distinct from PART 15's procedural "texturePattern" field — procedural
patterns are generated at runtime from math (no external file, cheap,
always available); this is a specific image supplied by the user (higher
fidelity, costs memory, requires an upload step). They are alternatives,
not complements — see the OVERRIDE rule below.

FIELDS (all optional, all top-level on the object in flat format; in
nested format these go inside "material" OR at top level — both accepted):

  "textureImage"            : string   (data URL OR same-origin path)
                                         Example: "data:image/png;base64,..."
                                         Example: "textures/wood.png"
  "textureImageRepeat"      : [u, v]   (default [1, 1])
  "textureImageOffset"      : [u, v]   (default [0, 0])
  "textureImageRotation"    : number   (degrees, default 0)
  "textureImageColorSpace"  : "srgb" | "no-color"
                                         (default "srgb" for color/albedo,
                                          "no-color" for normal/roughness/
                                          any data texture)

RULES:
  - textureImage may be a data URL (self-contained blueprint, no extra
    files needed) or a relative path (smaller blueprint, requires the
    image to sit alongside the blueprint at load time). The renderer
    tries the path first and falls back to an inline data URL if present.
  - OVERRIDE RULE: when textureImage is set on a part, it OVERRIDES any
    procedural "texturePattern" on that same part — procedural and image
    textures are mutually exclusive on a single part, never combined.
  - Multiple images per part are not supported in v7.x — one part, one
    image map. For decals or multiple textures on one part, stack child
    primitives instead (PART 25) or use the "addConvexHull" opt-in.
  - Loaded images respect "textureImageColorSpace": "srgb" for color/
    albedo maps, "no-color" for normal maps / roughness maps / any data
    texture (matches the colorSpace convention in PART 8.2 of the TS spec).

EXAMPLE — a wooden door with a grain image texture:
{
  "id": "door",
  "type": "box",
  "scale": [0.9, 2.1, 0.08],
  "material": "wood",
  "baseColor": "#8a5a2c",
  "textureImage": "data:image/png;base64,iVBORw0KGgoAA...",
  "textureImageRepeat": [1, 2],
  "textureImageRotation": 90
}

EXAMPLE — a flat decal/label on a side panel:
{
  "id": "label",
  "type": "box",
  "scale": [0.5, 0.3, 0.01],
  "material": "paint",
  "baseColor": "#ffffff",
  "textureImage": "textures/serial_label.png",
  "textureImageColorSpace": "srgb"
}


================================================================================
PART 6 — DIRECT SCULPT SHORTHANDS (verified against applyDirectSculptParams())
================================================================================
Write these straight on any primitive — no "operations" wrapper needed.
All five can be combined freely on one object, and stack with anything
you separately put in "operations".

  "taperAmount": 0.0–1.0+    narrows one end inward along an axis.
                              Default axis "y"; set "taperAxis":"x"|"y"|"z"
                              to change it.
  "bulgeAmount": -1.0–1.0    pushes every vertex outward (or inward, if
                              negative) along its own direction from the
                              shape's center — inflated/pudgy vs pinched.
  "twistAmount" / "twistAngle": degrees — twists around the shape's own
                              vertical (Y) axis from bottom to top. (Both
                              field names do the same thing; "twistAmount"
                              is checked first.)
  "bendAngle": degrees       bends along an arc. Defaults to bending
                              along the shape's X axis; set
                              "bendAxis":"x"|"y"|"z" to bend along
                              whichever axis is the part's actual LENGTH
                              axis — most capsule/cylinder/cone "limb"
                              shapes are tallest along Y, so a tail or
                              branch usually wants "bendAxis":"y".
  "roundness": 0.0–1.0       blends a boxy/angular shape toward a
                              rounded/spherical version of itself.

Example — tapered twisted tower:
{
  "id": "tower", "type": "cylinder", "scale": [0.8, 3, 0.8],
  "material": "stone", "detail": 2, "taperAmount": 0.6, "twistAmount": 60
}

Example — a fat, slightly twisted, rounded barrel:
{
  "id": "barrel", "type": "cylinder", "scale": [0.6, 0.8, 0.6],
  "bulgeAmount": 0.15, "twistAmount": 8, "roundness": 0.3,
  "material": "wood", "baseColor": "#8a5a2b"
}

Example — a drooping tail (capsule, tallest along Y, so bendAxis="y" to
actually curve along its length instead of sideways):
{
  "id": "tail", "type": "capsule", "scale": [0.22, 1.1, 0.22],
  "taperAmount": 0.35, "bendAngle": 40, "bendAxis": "y",
  "material": "fur", "baseColor": "#d8702e"
}


================================================================================
PART 7 — PLACEMENT SYSTEM (verified against solvePosition() / buildScene())
================================================================================
Applies only when the object has NO "position" field (see PART 4's
precedence rule).

  attachTo: "other_id"      attach relative to another object's world
                             bounding box (alias: "parent")
  placement: "<keyword>"    where to attach (default if omitted: "above")

PLACEMENT KEYWORDS (and their alias families — all confirmed in code):
  above (alias: placeabove), below (alias: placebelow),
  front (alias: placefront), back / behind (alias: placeback),
  left (alias: placeleft), right (alias: placeright),
  inside / center / centeredOn (alias: placeinside), top (alias: reston),
  hangFrom, beside, outside (alias: placeoutside — note: currently
    resolves to the SAME position as "front", not literally "outside"),
  roofOf, doorOf, windowOf, earOf, legOf, tailOf, wheelOf, branchOf,
  surround (resolves to parent center — use for things meant to wrap
    around/contain the parent, then size them larger than the parent),
  connectTo
  Any unrecognized placement keyword silently falls back to "above".

STRING ANCHORS (precise bounding-box points, used via "anchor" instead of
"placement" — takes priority over "placement" if both are present):
  top_center, bottom_center, front_center, back_center, left_center,
  right_center, top_left, top_right, bottom_left, bottom_right

offset: [x, y, z]    shift applied AFTER placement/anchor resolution, in
                     world-space axes (not the parent's local axes).

Example — ear attached to head:
{
  "id": "ear_l", "type": "pyramid", "scale": [0.3, 0.5, 0.2],
  "attachTo": "head", "placement": "above", "offset": [0.28, 0, -0.1]
}

Example — string anchor:
{
  "id": "crown", "type": "gem", "attachTo": "head",
  "anchor": "top_center", "offset": [0, 0.05, 0]
}

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
DYNAMIC ANCHOR OBJECT (v5.3+) — offset + orientation in one step
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
When "anchor" is an OBJECT instead of a string, it defines a custom frame:
parent-center + offset, with an optional "aim" direction the child's +Z
axis will be rotated to face.
{
  "id": "cannon", "type": "cylinder", "attachTo": "tower",
  "anchor": { "offset": [0.5, 0.2, 0], "aim": [1, 0.3, 0] }
}

"aim" shorthand (top-level, no anchor object needed) — same effect, just
for a plain attachTo placement instead of a dynamic anchor:
  "aim": [0, 1, 0.2]    orients the child's +Z toward this world direction

"inheritRotation": true   copies the parent's full world rotation onto
                          the child (applied via the object's own
                          "rotation" field first, then overridden by this
                          if both are present).

>>> ORDER OF OPERATIONS when several of these are combined on one object
(confirmed in buildScene()): "rotation" field is applied first, then
"inheritRotation" (if true) overwrites it with the parent's world
rotation, then any aim vector — whether from a dynamic "anchor.aim" or
the top-level "aim" shorthand — is applied LAST via a lookAt and wins
over everything before it. In practice: don't combine inheritRotation
and aim on the same object expecting inheritRotation to be visible; aim
will always take over.


================================================================================
PART 8 — CONSTRAINT SYSTEM (verified against applyConstraints())
================================================================================
"constraints": ["keyword1", "keyword2"]   — string-style constraints applied
independently. An object can carry several at once.

v6.4 NEW — Object-style constraints (required for radialRepeat and
linearArray because they carry params): use an array that mixes strings
and objects, e.g.:
  "constraints": [{ "type": "radialRepeat", "count": 6, "axis": "y" }]

  symmetrical (aliases: bilateralSymmetry, mirrorPlacement)
      Clones the object, mirrors the CLONE's X position and Y rotation.
      The original object is untouched and still renders at its own
      position — author one side, this generates the other.

  radialSymmetry
      Clones the object evenly around a vertical pivot (the attachTo
      parent's bounding-box center if it has one, else world origin).
      Count comes from "radialCount" (or "count"), default 6.

  v6.4 — radialRepeat (object-style constraint)
      Clones the mesh N times, rotating each clone evenly around the
      specified local axis. If radius>0, also translates each clone
      outward. Clones are purely visual — they never appear in the scene
      tree and are never exported. Use for spokes, bolt rings, fan blades,
      gear teeth, flower petals, radial vents.

      FIELDS:
        type         "radialRepeat"   (required)
        count        int              number of clones (default 6, min 2)
        axis         "x"|"y"|"z"     rotation axis (default "y")
        radius       float            outward offset per clone (default 0)
        startAngle   float (degrees)  offset for first clone (default 0)

      EXAMPLE — 8 wheel spokes (spoke points along X, wheel rotates on Y):
      {
        "id": "spoke",
        "type": "cube",
        "scale": [0.28, 0.04, 0.04],
        "position": [0, 0, 0.14],
        "material": "metal", "baseColor": "#444444",
        "constraints": [{ "type": "radialRepeat", "count": 8, "axis": "x" }]
      }

  v6.4 — linearArray (object-style constraint)
      Clones the mesh N times at regular spacing along an axis. When
      centered:true (default), the whole array is centered on the original
      object's position. Same sceneGroup-only rule — never in the tree
      or export. Use for track ties, fence posts, window rows, ladder
      rungs, bolt rows, repeated panel details.

      FIELDS:
        type         "linearArray"    (required)
        count        int              number of clones (default 4, min 2)
        spacing      float            distance between clone centres (default 0.5)
        axis         "x"|"y"|"z"     direction of the array (default "x")
        centered     bool             center array on original position (default true)

      EXAMPLE — 12 railway track ties:
      {
        "id": "track_tie",
        "type": "cube",
        "scale": [0.12, 0.08, 0.72],
        "position": [0, 0.05, 0],
        "material": "default", "baseColor": "#7B4A1E",
        "constraints": [{ "type": "linearArray", "count": 12,
                          "spacing": 0.44, "axis": "x" }]
      }

  equalSpacing
      Deferred pass (resolves after the whole model is built): redistributes
      every sibling that shares the same "attachTo" parent and has this
      constraint into even spacing along whichever axis they're actually
      spread across. Needs 3+ members under the same parent — fewer than
      that and it's a silent no-op.

  groundAligned
      Shifts the object up so its bounding-box bottom face sits at Y=0.

  faceForward
      Resets Y (yaw) rotation to 0 only — any intentional X/Z tilt is
      preserved.

  vertical    clears X and Z rotation (keeps Y).
  horizontal  sets X rotation to 90°.

  orientRadial (v5.3)
      Rotates the object to face directly away from its attachTo parent's
      center (in the XZ plane) — built for petals, spikes, leaves, horns
      radiating outward from a core.

  orientTo (v5.3)
      Rotates to face an explicit direction vector. Provide the vector
      either as "orientDirection": [x,y,z] on the object, OR as the next
      array element after the string "orientTo" inside "constraints":
      "constraints": ["orientTo", {"direction":[0,1,0]}]

  phyllotaxis (v5.3) — group-level Fibonacci spiral distribution, see PART 17.

Example — symmetrical legs:
{
  "id": "leg_l", "type": "cylinder", "scale": [0.22, 0.7, 0.22],
  "attachTo": "body", "placement": "below", "offset": [-0.4, 0, 0.4],
  "constraints": ["symmetrical"]
}

Example — radial spokes:
{
  "id": "spoke", "type": "rod", "scale": [0.06, 0.8, 0.06],
  "attachTo": "hub", "placement": "right",
  "constraints": ["radialSymmetry", "orientRadial"], "radialCount": 8
}


================================================================================
PART 9 — OPERATIONS ENGINE (verified against applyOperations() switch)
================================================================================
"operations": [ { "type": "...", "params": { ... } } ]
Applied in array order; you can stack multiple operations on one object.
Operations run AFTER position/rotation are set but BEFORE constraints.

-- DEFORM OPS --
  taper:    { "type": "taper", "params": { "amount": 0.5, "axis": "y" } }
  twist:    { "type": "twist", "params": { "angle": 45 } }
  bend:     { "type": "bend", "params": { "angle": 30, "axis": "x" } }
  inflate (alias: bulge): { "type": "inflate", "params": { "amount": 0.2 } }
  noise:    { "type": "noise", "params": { "amount": 0.08 } }
  flatten:  { "type": "flatten", "params": { "axis": "y", "amount": 0.3 } }
  stretch:  { "type": "stretch", "params": { "axis": "y", "amount": 1.5 } }
  pinch:    { "type": "pinch", "params": { "amount": 0.4 } }
  shear (alias: skew): { "type": "shear", "params": { "axis": "x", "amount": 0.3 } }
  round:    { "type": "round", "params": { "amount": 0.4 } }     (pushes verts toward a sphere)
  sharpen:  { "type": "sharpen", "params": { "amount": 0.25 } }  (opposite of round)
  ripple:   { "type": "ripple", "params": { "amount": 0.08, "frequency": 6, "axis": "y" } }
  warp:     { "type": "warp", "params": { "amount": 0.1, "frequency": 3 } }
  dent:     { "type": "dent", "params": { "point": [0,0,0], "radius": 0.4, "amount": 0.3 } }
  displace (aliases: emboss, engrave): { "type": "displace", "params": { "amount": 0.05, "frequency": 4 } }

-- MESH EDITING OPS --
  subdivide: { "type": "subdivide", "params": { "iterations": 1 } }
      Splits every triangle into 4, deduping shared edges so the mesh
      stays watertight. Use right before a taper/twist/bend/noise pass
      that needs more geometry to look smooth.
  decimate (aliases: remesh, simplify): { "type": "decimate", "params": { "ratio": 0.4 } }
      ratio = fraction of vertices to KEEP (0.4 = keep 40%, remove 60%).
      "amount" is also accepted as the inverse (fraction to REMOVE).
  smooth:  { "type": "smooth", "params": {} }
  facet:   { "type": "facet", "params": {} }

-- APPEARANCE OPS --
  glow: { "type": "glow", "params": { "color": "#00ff88", "intensity": 1.2 } }
      Sets emissive color + emissiveIntensity directly on the material.
  tint: { "type": "tint", "params": { "color": "#ff0000", "amount": 0.3 } }
      Lerps the existing material color toward the given color.
  fade: { "type": "fade", "params": { "opacity": 0.5 } }
  rust: { "type": "rust", "params": {} }     forces a fixed rust-brown color + high roughness
  weather: { "type": "weather", "params": {} }   increases roughness by 0.3 (capped at 1)

-- ARRAY / MIRROR OPS --
  linearArray: { "type": "linearArray", "params": { "count": 4, "spacing": 1.2, "axis": "x" } }
  gridArray:   { "type": "gridArray", "params": { "countX": 3, "countZ": 3, "spacingX": 1.5, "spacingZ": 1.5 } }
  radialArray: { "type": "radialArray", "params": { "count": 6, "radius": 2.0 } }
  scatter:     { "type": "scatter", "params": { "count": 10, "radius": 4.0, "seed": 42 } }
  mirrorX (alias: mirrorArray — only mirrors X regardless of axis param
    unless explicitly "axis":"x"): { "type": "mirrorX", "params": {} }
  mirrorY / mirrorZ: flip scale on that axis only — note these are NOT
    full clone-and-place mirrors like "symmetrical" (PART 8); they just
    invert the existing mesh's scale on that axis in place.

-- CSG BOOLEANS — see PART 10 --
  booleanUnion (aliases: union, add, merge)
  booleanDifference (aliases: difference, subtract, booleanSubtract, cut)
  booleanIntersect (aliases: intersect, intersection)

-- VERTEX PAINT — see PART 13 --
  vertexPaint (alias: paint): { "type": "vertexPaint", "params": { "splats": [ ... ] } }

Unrecognized operation types are silently skipped (no error, no warning).


================================================================================
PART 10 — CSG BOOLEANS (verified against runPendingCSG())
================================================================================
Booleans run as a DEFERRED pass after every mesh in the model already
exists — so the "with" target can be any other object regardless of
where it appears in the array. The "with" value must equal another
object's exact "id" (or "target"/"tool"/"with_id"/"withId" — all
accepted as synonyms for the same field).

Only put the booleanX operation on the object you want to KEEP — its
"with" target is the TOOL shape, consumed by the operation, and will not
appear separately in the final model.

Union — merge two meshes:
[
  { "id": "a", "type": "sphere", "scale": [1,1,1] },
  { "id": "b", "type": "cube", "scale": [0.7,0.7,0.7], "position": [0.4,0,0],
    "operations": [{ "type": "booleanUnion", "params": { "with": "a" } }] }
]

Difference — carve one mesh from another:
[
  { "id": "base", "type": "sphere", "scale": [1.2,1.2,1.2], "material": "obsidian" },
  { "id": "cutter", "type": "cube", "scale": [0.7,0.7,0.7], "position": [0,0.3,0],
    "operations": [{ "type": "booleanDifference", "params": { "with": "base" } }] }
]

Intersect — keep only the overlapping region:
[
  { "id": "s", "type": "sphere", "scale": [1,1,1] },
  { "id": "c", "type": "cube", "scale": [1,1,1], "position": [0.4,0,0],
    "operations": [{ "type": "booleanIntersect", "params": { "with": "s" } }] }
]

Implementation notes worth knowing:
  - The CSG library is loaded lazily from a CDN. If it fails to load, all
    queued boolean ops are skipped with a console warning — the rest of
    the model still finishes building normally (it just won't have the
    cut/merge applied).
  - Geometry should be reasonably watertight/two-manifold; a failed
    boolean falls back gracefully (logs a warning, model still finishes)
    rather than crashing.
  - A CSG tool object never needs a baseColor/material of its own (PART
    21 checklist exception) since it's deleted from the scene the moment
    the operation resolves.


================================================================================
PART 11 — EXTRUDE AND LATHE (verified against buildExtrude()/buildLathe())
================================================================================

EXTRUDE — push a flat 2D profile into 3D depth:
{
  "id": "custom_shape", "type": "extrude",
  "profile": [ [x0,y0], [x1,y1], [x2,y2], ... ],
  "depth": 0.3,
  "bevelEnabled": false, "bevelThickness": 0.05, "bevelSize": 0.05,
  "bevelSegments": 2, "steps": 1, "curveSegments": 8
}
Needs at least 3 points. If "profile" is missing or has fewer than 3
points, it silently falls back to a flat rectangle sized from
scale.x/scale.y — so a missing profile won't crash, but it also won't
look like anything custom.

Extrude with holes (e.g. a flat ring, a window frame):
{
  "id": "ring_flat", "type": "extrude",
  "profile": [[-0.5,-0.5],[0.5,-0.5],[0.5,0.5],[-0.5,0.5]],
  "holes": [[[-0.3,-0.3],[0.3,-0.3],[0.3,0.3],[-0.3,0.3]]],
  "depth": 0.15
}

LATHE — revolve a 2D profile around the Y axis:
{
  "id": "vase", "type": "lathe",
  "profile": [ [radius0, height0], [radius1, height1], ... ],
  "segments": 14, "phiStart": 0, "phiLength": 360
}
Needs at least 2 points. radius (first value of each pair) is clamped to
a small positive minimum even if you write 0 (a true 0 radius is allowed
conceptually — e.g. a vase's pointed base — and is handled fine). height
(second value) runs bottom to top. Fewer than 2 points falls back to a
generic vase-like profile.

Lathe example — vase:
{
  "id": "vase", "type": "lathe",
  "profile": [[0,-0.5],[0.22,-0.4],[0.30,-0.1],[0.15,0.15],[0.25,0.40],[0.10,0.50]],
  "segments": 14, "material": "marble", "baseColor": "#e8e4dc"
}


================================================================================
PART 12 — SDF BLOB / METABALLS (verified against buildSDFBlob())
================================================================================
Type: "sdf_blob" (aliases: metaball, metaballs, blob_field)
REQUIRES a "balls" array — omitting it silently substitutes a generic
3-ball fallback cluster instead of erroring, which is rarely what you
want, so always supply your own balls intentionally.

{
  "id": "creature", "type": "sdf_blob",
  "balls": [
    { "position": [-0.3, 0.1, 0],    "strength": 0.65, "subtract": 10 },
    { "position": [ 0.3,-0.1, 0.1],  "strength": 0.60, "subtract": 10 },
    { "position": [ 0,   0.3,-0.2],  "strength": 0.55, "subtract": 10 }
  ],
  "resolution": 28, "scale": [1.2, 1.0, 1.1],
  "material": "lava", "baseColor": "#ff4500"
}

Ball params:
  position   [x,y,z] in roughly -1..1 local space — values are clamped
             to ±0.9 before being mapped into the marching-cubes field,
             so anything past that gets pulled in rather than clipped off
  strength   0.3–0.9 (higher = larger influence, stronger merge). Below
             ~0.3 the ball may not contribute enough surface to appear.
  subtract   8–20 (controls surface sharpness/blend tightness — lower
             values blend more, higher values stay more distinct)

resolution   16–48 (clamped to this range even if you ask for more or
             less), default 28. Higher = smoother surface, heavier to
             compute.

After the marching-cubes surface is generated, the renderer automatically
RESCALES the result to exactly match your "scale" field — so the absolute
ball positions only affect the relative shape/silhouette, not the final
size of the object.

Tips:
  - Keep balls within roughly -0.8..0.8 to avoid edge clamping artifacts
  - Balls too far apart with low strength can produce literally no
    surface at all (the renderer detects this and falls back to a plain
    procedural blob shape with a console warning rather than crashing)
  - For organic, melted-together merging, keep subtract around 10-14


================================================================================
PART 13 — VERTEX PAINTING (verified against the 'vertexpaint' op case)
================================================================================
Blends extra colors onto nearby vertices using a sphere of influence — no
UV maps or external textures needed (every primitive already gets UVs
automatically, see ensureUVs in the renderer).

{
  "id": "bush", "type": "bush", "scale": [1.4, 1.2, 1.4],
  "material": "leaf", "baseColor": "#3d8c3a",
  "operations": [
    { "type": "vertexPaint", "params": { "splats": [
      { "position": [0, 0.5, 0],  "color": "#aaee44", "radius": 0.6, "falloff": 2.0 },
      { "position": [0,-0.5, 0],  "color": "#1a4a10", "radius": 0.5, "falloff": 1.0 }
    ] } }
  ]
}

Splat params:
  position   [x,y,z] center of the paint dab, in WORLD space (not local
             to the object — account for where the object actually sits)
  color      hex color blended in
  radius     sphere-of-influence radius
  falloff    1 = linear, 2 = quadratic/soft, 0.5 = wide and flat

Use vertex paint for soft, boundary-less detail (grime, blush, moss
patches, scuffing, color gradients) — NOT for hard color boundaries like
a costume's two-tone split or a chest emblem. Build hard boundaries as
their own separate primitive with its own baseColor instead (PART 20).


================================================================================
PART 14 — DECIMATION
================================================================================
Reduce polygon count — typically used right after subdivide, sdf_blob, or
a CSG operation, all of which can leave more geometry than the part needs.

{
  "id": "cloud", "type": "sphere", "scale": [2, 0.8, 1.5], "material": "snow",
  "operations": [
    { "type": "subdivide", "params": { "iterations": 2 } },
    { "type": "noise",     "params": { "amount": 0.15 } },
    { "type": "decimate",  "params": { "ratio": 0.3 } }
  ]
}
ratio = fraction of vertices to KEEP (0.3 = keep 30%, remove 70%).
"amount" is also accepted, as the inverse (fraction to REMOVE).


================================================================================
PART 15 — PROCEDURAL TEXTURES (verified against getProceduralTextureSet())
================================================================================
No external images — color/normal/displacement maps generated from math
on the CPU at load time, applied automatically once you set
"texturePattern" on an object. Safe no-op on failure (keeps flat baseColor).

Cross-reference: "textureImage" (PART 5.1) is the user-uploaded counterpart
to this procedural system — a specific image the user supplies, rather
than a pattern generated at load time. If both are set on one part,
textureImage overrides texturePattern (PART 5.1's override rule).

Fields:
  "texturePattern":  "wood" | "stone" (aliases: "speckle", "noise") |
                      "brick" | "scales" | "fabric" (alias: "weave")
  "textureScale":    tile repeat count, default 3
  "textureContrast": 0-1, default 0.35
  "normalStrength":  normal-map intensity, default 1.5
  "displacementAmount": vertex displacement depth (optional; if set, also
                      enables an actual geometric displacement map, not
                      just a visual normal-map bump)

Wood plank example:
{
  "id": "plank", "type": "beam", "scale": [2, 0.15, 0.5],
  "material": "oak", "baseColor": "#a0724a",
  "texturePattern": "wood", "textureScale": 5, "normalStrength": 2.0
}

Brick wall example:
{
  "id": "wall", "type": "wall", "scale": [3, 1.5, 0.3],
  "material": "brick", "baseColor": "#b5472a",
  "texturePattern": "brick", "textureScale": 4, "displacementAmount": 0.02
}


================================================================================
PART 16 — GENERATE MACRO (verified against expandGenerateBlocks())
================================================================================
Expands one template object into N flat objects — removes the need to
hand-author dozens of near-identical objects (fence posts, pillars,
windows, scales, teeth).

Variables available inside {{ }}: i (index, 0..count-1) and t (0..1,
normalized — only meaningful when count > 1). Objects produced by
"generate" do NOT need an outer "id" on the wrapper — only the inner
"template" does (and even that gets an auto id of "templateName_i" if
you omit it).

{
  "generate": {
    "count": 6, "as": "i",
    "template": {
      "id":        "pillar_{{i}}",
      "type":      "pillar",
      "scale":     [0.3, "{{0.4 + i * 0.25}}", 0.3],
      "material":  "marble", "baseColor": "#e8e4dc",
      "position":  ["{{-3 + i * 1.2}}", "{{(0.4 + i * 0.25) / 2}}", 0]
    }
  }
}

Documented-safe expression syntax: +, -, *, /, ternary (?:), and the
variables i/t. (Implementation note: the evaluator actually runs the full
expression through a real JS function body, so in practice any valid JS
arithmetic expression — including Math.sin/Math.cos/etc. — will work too.
This is a bonus, not a guarantee across future versions — stick to the
documented arithmetic/ternary subset for anything you want to be robust
long-term.) "position"/"scale"/"rotation" string results are
automatically parsed back into numbers after substitution.


================================================================================
PART 17 — PHYLLOTAXIS (verified against expandPhyllotaxisGroups())
================================================================================
Fibonacci golden-angle spiral distribution — sunflower seeds, flower
petals, pinecone scales, fish scales. Must be on a group-type object;
its "children" array serves as a TEMPLATE list (not literal children).
CRITICAL: use "name" (not "id") inside the template children.

If you provide MORE THAN ONE template child, the expansion cycles through
them in order (child[i % children.length]) — e.g. give it both a "petal"
and a slightly different "petal_variant" template and they'll alternate
around the spiral, not just repeat the first one.

Inside phyllotaxis children specifically, write "material" as a plain
STRING (not an object) plus a separate "baseColor", exactly like flat
format (PART 1) — these templates are NOT run through the nested-tree
material-object converter.

Fields (set on the group, not the children):
  phyllotaxisCount          total instances (default: children.length)
  phyllotaxisRadiusStart    inner radius, default 0.02
  phyllotaxisRadiusEnd      outer radius, default 0.48
  phyllotaxisAngleOffset    golden angle in degrees, default 137.5
  phyllotaxisHeightStart    Y at center, default 0
  phyllotaxisHeightEnd      Y at edge (creates a dome/cone if different
                             from Start), default 0
  phyllotaxisOrient         true = each instance's yaw faces outward
                             away from the spiral center

{
  "id": "flower_head", "type": "group", "position": [0, 0.5, 0],
  "constraints": ["phyllotaxis"],
  "phyllotaxisCount": 34, "phyllotaxisRadiusStart": 0.01,
  "phyllotaxisRadiusEnd": 0.5, "phyllotaxisOrient": true,
  "children": [
    { "name": "seed", "type": "ellipsoid", "scale": [0.08, 0.04, 0.08],
      "material": "bark", "baseColor": "#3a2010" }
  ]
}


================================================================================
PART 18 — VARIATION SYSTEM (verified against applyVariationToObj())
================================================================================
Interpolates a phyllotaxis group's per-instance properties from a start
value to an end value (linear) or across multiple keyframes, based on
each instance's normalized position (t = i/(count-1)) in the spiral.

Linear:
"variation": {
  "baseColor": { "start": "#fff8cc", "end": "#ff4400" },
  "scale":     { "start": [0.06,0.03,0.06], "end": [0.12,0.06,0.12] }
}

Keyframes (t values 0..1, any number of stops):
"variation": {
  "scale": { "keyframes": [
    [0.0, [0.04,0.02,0.04]], [0.5, [0.12,0.06,0.12]], [1.0, [0.06,0.03,0.06]]
  ] }
}

Specially-handled keys: baseColor (also patches into the instance's
material), scale, rotation, bendAngle (also patches into any existing
"bend" operation's params.angle on that instance). Any OTHER key
(roughness, metalness, emissive, emissiveIntensity, bulgeAmount,
taperAmount, etc.) is applied generically as a direct field override —
so the supported-key list above is not exhaustive, anything settable
directly on a primitive can be varied this way.

Full phyllotaxis + variation example — a sunflower:
{
  "id": "sunflower", "type": "group", "position": [0, 0.5, 0],
  "constraints": ["phyllotaxis"],
  "phyllotaxisCount": 34, "phyllotaxisRadiusStart": 0.01,
  "phyllotaxisRadiusEnd": 0.5, "phyllotaxisOrient": true,
  "variation": {
    "baseColor": { "start": "#ffee88", "end": "#ff6600" },
    "scale": { "start": [0.05,0.03,0.05], "end": [0.10,0.05,0.10] }
  },
  "children": [
    { "name": "petal", "type": "petal", "scale": [0.08,0.04,0.08],
      "material": "flower", "baseColor": "#ffcc44" }
  ]
}


================================================================================
PART 19 — NESTED TREE FORMAT: DEEP DIVE (verified against flattenTree())
================================================================================
This part exists because the nested-format coordinate rules are the
single biggest source of "disconnected parts" bugs, and neither older
source document spelled out the exact heuristic the renderer actually
uses. If a humanoid/creature model renders with limbs floating away from
the body instead of attached, this is almost always the cause.

WHAT COUNTS AS A "GROUP" (gets dissolved, contributes a position offset,
but never becomes a renderable object itself):
  A node with no "type" at all, or "type":"group", that has a non-empty
  "children" array — UNLESS it also carries the "phyllotaxis" constraint,
  in which case it's preserved as a special group node for PART 17's
  expansion instead of being dissolved here.

GROUP OFFSET ACCUMULATION:
  Every dissolved group's own "position" is summed into a running offset
  that gets added onto the position of every primitive nested anywhere
  inside it (and into its own children's group offset, if there are
  nested groups). A group's "name" also extends a naming prefix used to
  scope ids (see below) — but a group contributes NO offset of its own if
  it has no "position" field (defaults to [0,0,0]).

ID SCOPING (why two different limbs can both have a child named "tip"):
  Every primitive's real "id" becomes `<enclosing-group-name-path>_<name>`
  (e.g. "tentacle_2_tip"), falling back to an auto-generated id if
  duplicates would otherwise collide. This means cross-references inside
  "operations" (a boolean op's "with" field) that use a short un-prefixed
  name are automatically resolved to the correctly-scoped id — picking,
  when there are multiple same-named candidates across different
  branches, whichever one shares the longest scope-path prefix with the
  object doing the referencing (i.e. "the nearest one to you", not a
  random match).

THE CRITICAL RULE — IS A CHILD'S "position" WORLD-SPACE OR A LOCAL OFFSET?
  By default, EVERY primitive's "position" in nested format is treated as
  a shared, body-relative WORLD coordinate — siblings and cousins all sit
  in the same coordinate frame, not nested parent-local frames. This is
  intentional: it lets you author an entire creature with every part's
  position relative to one shared origin, the way you'd naturally think
  about a body.

  There are exactly TWO exceptions, where a child's "position" is instead
  converted into an attachTo + offset relationship (attached to its
  immediate real-geometry parent, placement "center", offset = the
  position you wrote):
    1. The immediate parent primitive has a bend/taper/twist operation.
       Its actual geometry has moved away from its undeformed local axes,
       so a child positioned "at the tip of a bent tail" needs to track
       the parent's actual (deformed) location, not a fixed world point.
    2. The immediate parent is a tiny sub-feature: its largest scale
       dimension is under ~0.2. This is the classic "pupil nested inside
       an eye" pattern — something that small is never a shared
       coordinate-frame anchor for distant siblings, so its child is
       clearly meant as a detail positioned relative to IT specifically.

  Practical takeaway: if you're authoring a humanoid/creature in nested
  format and a hand/foot/head ends up floating in the wrong place, check
  whether you accidentally relied on local-offset semantics for a part
  that doesn't meet either exception above — the renderer will have
  treated your "position" as a literal world coordinate instead.

MATERIAL EXTRACTION (nested -> flat):
  A nested node's "material" object (if present) is unpacked into flat
  baseColor/roughness/metalness/opacity/emissive/emissiveIntensity fields
  on the converted object, and the original "material" key is deleted
  (set to undefined) so it doesn't break the flat-format string lookup
  (PART 1). A plain-string "material" on a nested node is NOT specially
  unpacked this way — if you write a string, you get the same string
  -preset behavior as flat format, plus whatever explicit baseColor you
  also wrote.

EVERY OTHER FIELD SURVIVES AS-IS:
  taperAmount, bulgeAmount, roundness, twistAmount, bendAngle, detail,
  profile, holes, balls, resolution, sides, constraints, operations, and
  any future field not yet listed here all pass through the nested-to
  -flat conversion completely unchanged — only id/type/rotation/scale/
  material/children/position get special handling.


================================================================================
PART 20 — MODELING GUIDANCE (semantic knowledge layer — not enforced by code,
           but essential for output quality; condensed from the v5.3 spec)
================================================================================

MASTER DETAIL DIRECTIVE:
  Use exactly as much geometry as a part needs to read clearly — never
  less, never more for its own sake.
  - Raise "detail"/"resolution" on any primitive about to be heavily
    taper/twist/bend/bulge/subdivide-sculpted, so the deformation has
    enough geometry to look smooth and intentional, not blocky.
  - Add secondary forms (panels, ridges, seams, joints, vents, plating,
    stitching, knuckles, claws, bark texture, scales) once the primary
    silhouette is locked — this is what sells "detailed" without
    changing the model's recognizable read.
  - Never pad with primitives that don't read as anything — decoration
    for decoration's sake wastes polygon budget without adding detail.
  - A short prompt ("low poly fox") is a complete brief, not permission
    to under-build — fill in every proportion/material/surface detail a
    real reference would have, using PART 20's knowledge below plus your
    own broader world knowledge.
  - Detail budget scales with subject complexity, not a flat amount
    applied to everything. A mug or a single rock is genuinely simple —
    don't manufacture complexity it doesn't have. A castle, dragon,
    spaceship, or cathedral genuinely has dozens of expected
    substructures — give it all of them. "Castle" implies walls, one or
    more towers, a gatehouse, battlements, and a keep at minimum, not
    just a box with a triangle roof. "Dragon" implies head, horns, neck,
    torso, legs, claws, wings, AND tail, not a lizard-shaped blob.
    "Spaceship" implies hull, cockpit, engines, and visible surface
    paneling, not one elongated capsule.
  - Within a complex model, spend MORE of the detail budget on one or two
    genuine hero areas (a castle's gatehouse/great tower, a dragon's head
    and wings, a mech's torso core) rather than spreading effort evenly —
    a clear focal point reads better than evenly-mediocre detail.

REFERENCE-IMAGE INPUT MODE (if an image is attached alongside the prompt):
  Analyze it FIRST, explicitly, before writing any JSON:
  1. Silhouette & proportions — measure every major mass and angle AS
     SHOWN; the image overrides generic proportion defaults whenever
     they disagree.
  2. Color & surface — read off each region's hex color and judge
     matte/fabric vs glossy/plastic vs metal vs glass, set
     roughness/metalness/opacity accordingly instead of one material for
     the whole model.
  3. Glow — anything bright/saturated against a darker surround, or 
     a visible halo, needs emissive + emissiveIntensity, not just a
     bright baseColor.
  4. Surface language — faceted/angular (low detail, consider
     facet/sharpen) vs rounded/organic (higher detail, consider
     round/smooth or sdf_blob) — judge per-part, a single model can mix
     both.
  5. Symmetry — mirrored limbs/parts get a symmetry constraint, not two
     separately hand-guessed sides.
  6. Compound shapes — any cutout/vent/window/rivet/notch visible is a
     signal for a CSG boolean (PART 10), not an approximated overlap.
  7. Profile shapes — a part whose cross-section changes along its
     length (a blade's taper, a bottle's curve, a wheel rim) is a signal
     for extrude or lathe (PART 11).

GENERATION THINKING PROCESS:
  1. Understand the subject semantically.
  2. Determine its recognizable silhouette.
  3. Plan the hierarchy.
  4. Determine proportions (below).
  5. For each part, pick the construction technique that fits — plain
     primitive / sculpted primitive / boolean combination / sdf_blob /
     extrude / lathe.
  6. Apply curves/sculpting and decide per-part detail.
  7. Apply placement (attachTo/anchor/offset, or shared-frame absolute
     positions for an articulated structure).
  8. Apply materials and colors, including emissive where it belongs.
  9. Validate recognizability AND that polygon density matches what each
     part actually needs.
  10. Generate valid JSON.

PROPORTION SYSTEM (recognizability over realism — use these ratios as a
starting point, scaled to whatever absolute size the scene calls for):

  Fox:    body 1.00, head 0.55, tail 0.80, ears 0.35, legs 0.55, eyes 0.08
  Wolf:   body 1.00, head 0.60, tail 0.60, ears 0.28
  Cat:    body 1.00, head 0.65, tail 0.90, eyes 0.12
  Tree:   trunk 1.00, canopy width 0.90
  Festival stall: base width 1.00, pole height 1.20, roof width 1.20

  Mech/Robot: torso height 1.00, torso width 0.70, head 0.30, upper arm
    0.65, forearm 0.55, hand 0.20, upper leg 0.75, lower leg 0.65, foot
    0.30, shoulder width 1.10

  Humanoid/Character: torso height 1.00, head 0.20 (deliberately bigger
    than the realistic ~0.125 ratio — a slightly oversized head reads
    more clearly and stays more expressive at low poly counts), neck
    0.06, shoulder width 0.85, hip width 0.55, upper arm 0.40, forearm
    0.35, hand 0.13 (also slightly oversized for readability), upper leg
    0.55, lower leg 0.50, foot 0.18

SEMANTIC TRAITS (what makes each subject recognizable):

  Fox: large ears, pointed snout, thin legs, long fluffy tail, orange and
    white coloring, slender body.
  Wolf: medium ears, long snout, muscular body, gray coloring.
  Cat: rounded head, large eyes, medium tail, small paws.
  Tree: vertical trunk, branch hierarchy, rounded canopy.
  Festival stall: rectangular base, four support poles, sloped roof,
    hanging lanterns, wood materials.
  Bridge: long horizontal deck, support pillars, symmetrical structure.
  Lantern: rounded body, hanging handle, warm glow.

  Mech/Robot: boxy/angular torso and limbs, sharply faceted plating (low
    detail, high contrast between flat panel faces), narrow waist
    relative to shoulders, oversized shoulder pads, glowing optic/visor
    (emissive), glowing joints or energy weapon (emissive, often a
    contrasting accent color against a neutral plating body), symmetrical
    limbs via bilateralSymmetry/mirrorX rather than hand-authoring both
    sides, panel-line detail via shallow engrave/boolean-cut grooves
    rather than dense geometry, weapons/shields built from a lathe
    (cylindrical barrel/hilt) or extrude (flat blade/panel profile)
    rather than a generic box.

  Humanoid/Character: heroic/stylized proportions (oversized head and
    hands per above, not realistic anatomy), broad shoulders tapering to
    a narrower waist/hips, limbs built from tapered capsules/cylinders
    with bendAngle on arms and legs rather than perfectly straight
    segments. Costume color-blocking (a chest emblem, a two-tone bodysuit,
    distinct glove/boot colors) is a HARD boundary, not a gradient —
    build each color region as its own primitive with its own baseColor
    rather than blending with vertex paint; reserve vertex paint (PART
    13) for soft, boundary-less detail on top of a region instead (grime,
    blush, scuffing, a worn patch). Eye lenses/visors/glowing emblems use
    emissive+emissiveIntensity, not just a bright baseColor. Armored
    joints suit a lathe; organic/cloth-covered joints suit a plain
    capsule/sphere. Capes/scarves/loose hair/flowing cloth use bend so
    they read as fabric rather than a rigid slab. Symmetrical limbs use
    bilateralSymmetry/mirrorX — author one arm/leg and let the mirror
    generate its pair.

CURVES AND SCULPTING:
  Avoid perfectly straight/boxy shapes unless intentional — curves create
  personality (a fox's tail bends, a tree branch is irregular, a lantern
  is rounded). Use large forms first, then secondary forms, then fine
  detail. Keep polygon count proportional to what each part needs (the
  Master Detail Directive above). Prefer strong, simple shapes over
  fussy ones for the primary silhouette.


================================================================================
PART 21 — VALIDATION CHECKLIST (run through silently before output)
================================================================================
  [ ] Using FLAT format: top-level "objects" array; every entry has "id";
      non-root parts have "attachTo"/"parent" OR "position" (not both
      with conflicting intent — PART 4); "material" (if present) is a
      STRING, never an object.
      — OR — Using NESTED format: root has "type":"group" and "children";
      every node has "name" (not "id"); "material" may be an object here.
      Never both formats at the top level.
  [ ] Every object/child has a valid primitive "type" from PART 2 (or is
      intentionally a "group").
  [ ] [v6.3.1] Every "type":"generated" object's "generator" is exactly
      one of: sphere, tube, lathe/revolve, extrude, disc/patch/oval,
      box_subdivided, cylinder, cone, capsule, spoked_wheel, smoke_trail
      — anything else silently becomes a sphere (PART 25). Each has real
      "params" sized appropriately, not just a bare "scale" left to the
      v6.3.1 fallback.
  [ ] Every VISIBLE primitive has at least a "baseColor" (top-level field
      in flat format, or inside "material" in nested format). Exception:
      CSG tool objects — referenced in another object's booleanX "with"
      field and carrying no booleanX operation of their own — never
      appear in the final model and don't need a material.
  [ ] No baseColor/material/roughness etc. sitting directly on a "group"
      root or container node (groups have no surface of their own).
  [ ] Every extrude/lathe primitive has a "profile" array (PART 11).
  [ ] Every sdf_blob primitive has a "balls" array (PART 12).
  [ ] [v7.9] If the reference is one continuous sculpted/organic mass,
      the whole figure is ONE sdf_blob with multi-ball limbs (PART 61) —
      never separate primitives merely positioned to touch.
  [ ] Every boolean operation's "with" target id actually exists
      elsewhere in the model (PART 10).
  [ ] Phyllotaxis "children" templates use "name" (not "id"), and their
      "material" (if any) is a plain string, not an object (PART 17).
  [ ] No object has both "position" AND "attachTo"/"placement" set 
      the expectation that placement still applies (PART 4) — pick one.
  [ ] Hero/focal parts have raised "detail"; small/background parts
      deliberately don't (keeps their polygon cost low).
  [ ] Every cutout/vent/window/socket/notch/bolted-on lump that a single
      primitive can't represent cleanly uses a CSG boolean rather than an
      approximated overlapping shape.
  [ ] The model is immediately recognizable from its silhouette alone.
  [ ] All constraints/operations/materials/primitives used are from the
      lists in this document (PARTs 2, 3, 8, 9).
  [ ] [v6.4] Repeated geometry (spokes, bolts, windows, track ties, rungs,
      fence posts, panel rows) uses radialRepeat or linearArray constraint
      instead of manually cloning and placing identical objects.
  [ ] All tube "path" arrays start near [0,0,0] — LOCAL OFFSETS, not world
      coordinates. path[0] ≈ [0,0,0]; the object's "position" puts the tube
      in the world. (See PART 25 tube ⚠ WARNING for wrong/correct examples.)
  [ ] smoke_trail objects: "position" is set to the emission source (chimney
      tip, exhaust port) in world space; startPos/endPos are LOCAL offsets
      (startPos≈[0,0,0] = start AT the position; endPos = arc direction).
  [ ] autoGround left at default true for any ground-contact model. Only
      set autoGround:false for floating/airborne subjects.
  [ ] Complex models (3+ repeated colors) define a "palette" block and use
      $varName references in "baseColor"/"material"/"emissive" fields.
  [ ] Blueprint sections annotated with // comments (e.g. // === CHASSIS ===)
      for readability.
  [ ] (PART 28) AI quality rules satisfied: no #000000 on visible surfaces;
      full model fits in 3×3×3 units; generators used for all non-trivial
      shapes; radialRepeat/linearArray for repetitive geometry; material
      presets match physical surface type.
  [ ] v6.3 (PART 26): for rigged/posed characters, a "skeleton" + "joint"
      fields are used instead of hand-computed positions; "proportions"
      is decided before building parts, not after; organic parts use
      "smoothing":"auto" and mechanical parts use "flat"; "colorVariation"
      is OFF (omitted) by default — only set it when the brief calls for a
      hand-painted/textured look, not reflexively on every surface;
      adjoining parts either set "overlap" or are sized
      0.04–0.08 units oversized to avoid seams; "contactShadow" is set at
      the blueprint level; panel-shade variants use "toneVariant" instead
      of new hex codes; a humanoid/animal pose has subtle "asymmetry"
      rather than a stiff mirrored T-pose; every flat surface over
      0.4×0.4 units has at least one secondary detail (panel line, bolt,
      trim, or vent).
  [ ] [v-next] Every id referenced by attachTo/parent/joint/sideOf/lookAt/
      operations[].with/"ref:" strings has been checked character-for-
      character against the ids that actually exist in this blueprint
      (PART 36) — including ids consumed by an earlier CSG "with".
  [ ] No object attaches to a rotated parent using plain "placement" —
      sideOf/sideToward used instead (PART 37).
  [ ] No object attaches to a CSG base/tool id for its final resting
      position (PART 38).
  [ ] This blueprint has been pasted into the renderer and the validation
      log read top to bottom, including warnings, not just errors (PART 39).
  [ ] No "scale" value is 0 or near-0.
  [ ] Every limb (arm/leg) is ONE "chain"/"tube" mesh, or built via
      PART 45's derived-from-previous-segment math — never two+ capsules
      with independently hand-typed position/rotation (PART 40).
  [ ] Every hand/foot/cuff/held-prop uses attachTo+placement targeting its
      limb's own id — none use a raw "position" or a stale skeleton "joint"
      coordinate that predates the limb's final pose (PART 41).
  [ ] Default standing pose has arms hanging roughly vertical with a mild
      elbow bend — full horizontal T-pose only if explicitly requested
      (PART 42).
  [ ] Every object with no attachTo/placement/joint and no clear standalone
      purpose has been deleted (PART 43).
  [ ] Garment overlays (apron/vest/harness) have strap/tie geometry and
      "overlap" set — checked from the back view, not just the front
      (PART 44).
  [ ] [v7.7] meta.style (PART 48.1) and meta.category are set and locked
      before any object was written; construction technique, edges, and
      lighting match that style's mapping (PART 48.3), not a blended or
      generic default.
  [ ] [v7.7] The proportion declaration (PART 49.1, 10 fields) is present
      as // comments at the top of the output, using the category-specific
      measurement method (PART 49.2) for non-character subjects.
  [ ] [v7.7] Small (<0.15 of bounding dimension) bright-highlighted parts
      use a metallic preset (PART 50.2); saturated/haloed parts are
      emissive (PART 50.3); flat-shaded styles override to matte
      regardless of color (PART 50.4).
  [ ] [v7.7] The 11-item pre-output self-check (PART 51.1) has been run
      and every box is checked before JSON is emitted.
  [ ] [v8.1] No single sdf_blob or CSG boolean-union chain spans the
      ENTIRE model; the final object set has multiple separate mesh
      entries, not one (PART 63.1).
  [ ] [v8.1] Every visible part-level object carries "isPickable": true
      and "partName" in its top-level literal (PART 63.2).
