While auditing #2 above, every other lookup table with the same drift risk
(KNOWN_GENERATORS, KNOWN_OPERATION_TYPES, KNOWN_CONSTRAINT_TYPES) was
re-diffed programmatically against its real switch statement. All three
are currently fully in sync — no further silent gaps found by this pass.

If you're an AI reading this to author a blueprint: nothing above changes
what you should write. "seed" and "lightingPreset" already worked exactly
as documented in every section above the first time you read them — these
two entries exist so the changelog matches the renderer's real history, and
so a future audit doesn't have to re-derive that these two were checked.

v7.7 ADDITIONS — LBL v7.2 UNIVERSAL FIDELITY IMPROVEMENT PATCH
Applied to: Prompt_To_Json.txt   |   Date applied: 2026-07-17
Source: "LBL v7.2 SYSTEM — UNIVERSAL FIDELITY IMPROVEMENT PATCH"
(Mavis / MiniMax Agent). Adds PARTs 48-53 below, plus cross-reference
amendments folded into PART 21 (validation checklist) and, for the
image-input forks, PART 27 (image analysis protocol) above. Every
PART number in this patch was renumbered from the source document's
47-52 to 48-53 to avoid colliding with this file's existing PART 47
(v7.6 upgrade-guide audit). Scope is universal — it applies to every
subject category this system produces, not just the chef-with-sword
example used to illustrate failure modes in the source patch.

================================================================================
PART 48 — v7.7 ADDITIONS: UNIVERSAL STYLE LOCK (fires on every render)
================================================================================
This is the single biggest source of mismatch between reference
and output across ALL subjects, not just one. A voxel reference
and a smooth low-poly reference of the SAME subject (a car, a
tree, a person, anything) have nothing in common visually — same
subject, completely different geometric vocabulary, edge
treatment, material behavior, lighting response. Without an
explicit style-lock step, the AI falls back on whichever style
it has the strongest generic prior for, which is almost NEVER
the reference's actual style.

This applies equally to characters, vehicles, buildings,
creatures, plants, furniture, weapons, props — the style of the
reference is the dominant signal, NOT the subject category.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
48.1 — UNIVERSAL STYLE VOCABULARY (canonical list)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The renderer does NOT enforce these as enum values, but using
the same string across the project lets you filter, style-switch,
and benchmark later. Use these exact strings in meta.style:

  "smooth-low-poly"     — clean faceted forms, subtle bevels,
                          PBR-ish shading, ~5-15 facets per major
                          mass. Common for character art, vehicles,
                          organic props.

  "flat-low-poly"       — visible facets, hard edges, flat-shaded,
                          no specular gradient. Very common for
                          stylized character renders, game assets,
                          mobile-game art.

  "voxel"               — Minecraft-style cubes only, axis-aligned
                          blocks, no curves. Common for retro /
                          sandbox / blocky art.

  "chibi"               — oversized head, small body, ~2-3 head
                          heights total, simple geometry. Common
                          for mascot characters, anime-style
                          collectibles, cute creatures.

  "anime-cel"           — flat color regions, painted shadows,
                          black outlines, no realistic shading.
                          Common for anime character art, JRPG
                          characters, stylized portraits.

  "pbr-realistic"       — physically based, full PBR pipeline,
                          normal maps, roughness variation. Common
                          for product shots, vehicles, architecture,
                          realistic creatures.

  "papercraft"          — flat polygonal planes joined at edges,
                          zero thickness visible. Common for
                          origami, paper toys, flat illustration
                          translated to 3D.

  "toon"                — ramped toon shading, 2-3 tone steps,
                          black outlines. Common for cel-shaded
                          games, Borderlands-style art, stylized
                          animation.

  "photogrammetry"      — noisy, high-poly, irregular surface.
                          Common for scanned real-world objects,
                          terrain, ruins.

  "isometric-flat"      — 2-3 tone stepped flat colors, no outline,
                          classic isometric game art. Common for
                          mobile games, illustration, icons.

  "low-poly-painterly"  — low-poly geometry with painted texture
                          variation (colorVariation, painted
                          highlights). Common for stylized indie
                          games, character portraits.

  "wireframe" / "x-ray" — geometry visible, often transparent or
                          backface-visible. Common for technical
                          illustration, blueprints, schematics.

If the reference is ambiguous between two styles, PICK ONE and
stay consistent — do not blend. Blended styles produce output
that matches no reference.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
48.2 — UNIVERSAL STYLE LOCK PROCEDURE (fires on every render)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Step 1 (do this BEFORE any measurement, on every render): Identify
the reference's style from 48.1 by asking:
  - Are the edges hard or soft?
    hard = voxel/flat-low-poly/isometric-flat
    soft = smooth-low-poly/pbr-realistic/photogrammetry
  - Is shading flat or gradient?
    flat = flat-low-poly/voxel/anime-cel/toon/isometric-flat
    gradient = smooth-low-poly/pbr-realistic
  - Are forms faceted or curved?
    faceted = all low-poly variants
    curved = pbr-realistic/photogrammetry
  - Are there black outlines?
    yes = anime-cel/toon
  - Is the surface noisy?
    yes = photogrammetry
  - Are there painted texture details on otherwise flat geometry?
    yes = low-poly-painterly

Step 2: Write the style as `meta.style` in EVERY output, for
EVERY subject:
  "meta": {
    "name": "<Subject Name>",
    "style": "<style from 48.1>",
    "description": "..."
  }

Step 3: Lock every subsequent choice to that style. Subject
category is irrelevant to style — a "voxel car" and a "voxel
character" both use the same voxel rules. The following mismatches
are equally bad across all subjects:
  - voxel reference → smooth-low-poly output (curves where there
    should be cubes — happens for cars, trees, furniture, anything)
  - flat-low-poly reference → PBR output (specular highlights
    where the reference is matte — happens for weapons, gems,
    fruits, anything shiny-looking in a flat-shaded art style)
  - anime-cel reference → smooth-low-poly output (shading gradient
    where the reference has flat color regions — happens for any
    anime-styled reference)
  - chibi reference → realistic proportions output (head:body
    ratio snap to realism — happens for mascots, kawaii creatures,
    any reference with exaggerated head size)
  - pbr-realistic reference → flat-low-poly output (geometry too
    low-poly for the smooth-shaded reference — happens for product
    shots, vehicles, real-world scans)

Step 4: Render a 5-second mental "would this look right next to
the reference" check. If not, the style is wrong — go back to
Step 1.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
48.3 — UNIVERSAL STYLE → CONSTRUCTION TECHNIQUE MAPPING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Once the style is locked, default construction choices apply to
EVERY subject in that style:

  smooth-low-poly (any subject):
    - Primitives: ellipsoid, capsule, rounded forms preferred
    - Operations: sculpt, sdf_blob for organic forms
    - Materials: matte-fabric, skin, metal with high roughness
    - Edges: implicit (no explicit edge geometry)
    - Lighting: 3-point or PBR

  flat-low-poly (any subject):
    - Primitives: cube, tetrahedron, low-poly icosphere
    - Operations: sculpt only for clearly faceted forms
    - Materials: ALL matte regardless of declared color/preset — see
      PART 50.4, which supersedes this line as originally written
      ("metal, low roughness OK"); flat-shaded geometry has no specular
      response in this renderer, so that line contradicted 50.4 and has
      been corrected here to match it [fixed 2026-07-22]
    - Edges: visible (the renderer draws them by default for
      flat-shaded materials)
    - Lighting: directional, single hard shadow

  voxel (any subject — including organic-looking references):
    - Primitives: cube ONLY (single biggest mistake is using
      sphere/cylinder for a voxel reference)
    - Operations: NONE beyond placement
    - Materials: matte only — no metallic, no glossy
    - Edges: explicit black outline on every face
    - Lighting: ambient only, no cast shadows

  chibi (any subject):
    - Primitives: sphere for head/eyes, capsule for limbs
    - Operations: minimal
    - Materials: skin (head/hands), matte-fabric (clothes)
    - Proportions: head:body = 1:2 to 1:2.5 (NOT realistic 1:7)
    - Lighting: soft, no harsh shadows

  anime-cel (any subject):
    - Primitives: simple cube/sphere for forms
    - Operations: extrude for hair/clothing planes
    - Materials: matte only with explicit outline
    - Edges: ALWAYS explicit black outline
    - Lighting: flat, no gradient

  pbr-realistic (any subject):
    - Primitives: all
    - Operations: sculpt, sdf_blob heavily
    - Materials: full PBR with proper metalness/roughness maps
    - Edges: invisible
    - Lighting: HDR environment + 3-point

  papercraft (any subject):
    - Primitives: extrude ONLY (single flat polygon pushed to
      its silhouette)
    - Operations: extrude (PART 12)
    - Materials: matte paper preset, both sides
    - Edges: visible fold creases
    - Lighting: flat, no cast shadow

  toon (any subject):
    - Primitives: simple
    - Operations: minimal
    - Materials: toon-shaded preset (2-3 tone steps)
    - Edges: black outline
    - Lighting: directional with hard ramp

  photogrammetry (any subject):
    - Primitives: high-poly icosphere subdivision
    - Operations: sculpt with high noise
    - Materials: full PBR
    - Edges: invisible
    - Lighting: HDR environment

  isometric-flat (any subject — common for buildings, props,
  characters in 2.5D games):
    - Primitives: cube, simple shapes
    - Operations: minimal
    - Materials: matte with 2-3 explicit color tones
    - Edges: invisible
    - Lighting: implied (no actual lighting — colors do the work)

  low-poly-painterly (any subject):
    - Primitives: low-poly base
    - Operations: sculpt
    - Materials: matte-fabric with heavy colorVariation
    - Edges: implicit
    - Lighting: 3-point, soft

  wireframe / x-ray (any subject):
    - Primitives: any
    - Operations: any
    - Materials: wireframe or transparent preset
    - Edges: visible (this IS the subject)
    - Lighting: none

[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22 — what the renderer actually enforces
from this section, vs. what it only warns about]:
  - FORCED (real behavior change, not just a message): voxel/anime-cel/
    toon auto-enable the existing outline toggle if it wasn't already on
    (never auto-disables it, so a deliberate "no outline" choice for a
    non-outline style is left alone); anime-cel/toon auto-switch every
    object to toon-shaded material unless that object explicitly asked
    for a different special shading mode; voxel defaults the lighting
    preset to "Flat" (ambient/hemi only, matching "ambient only, no cast
    shadows" above) when the blueprint didn't request a specific preset
    of its own; flat-low-poly/voxel/isometric-flat/anime-cel force every
    material to matte per PART 50.4 (metalness/roughness/emissive/
    transmission/clearcoat/sheen all zeroed, regardless of preset).
  - RECOMMENDED, not forced: pbr-realistic pushes a validator warning
    suggesting the 🌐 HDRI toggle if it isn't already on — this one
    stays a nudge, not an automatic toggle, since "HDR environment"
    above reads as a recommendation rather than a hard requirement the
    way the outline/matte rules do.
  - WARNING ONLY (no forced behavior change): voxel's "cube ONLY"
    primitive rule — a non-cube object under voxel style gets flagged,
    not silently replaced with a cube, since auto-substituting geometry
    the author explicitly authored felt riskier than telling them about
    it. Existing blueprints authored before this patch existed are never
    retroactively broken by any of the above — every check here is
    additive to what already rendered, not a new precondition for
    rendering at all.


================================================================================
PART 49 — v7.7 ADDITIONS: MEASURED PROPORTIONS GATE (fires on every render)
================================================================================
The current PART 27.2 says "measure proportions" but gives no
enforcement mechanism. The AI measures nothing and outputs generic
ratios. This gate makes measurement MANDATORY and the output
VERIFIABLE for every subject, every render.

NOTE FOR THE TEXT-PROMPT (NO-IMAGE) PIPELINE: this fork of the spec
has no reference image to measure pixels from. Apply this gate anyway —
every 'image' or 'reference' mention below means "the subject as
described in the text prompt, reasoned about with real-world/genre
knowledge of that subject" instead of pixel measurement. The 10-line
declaration and category-specific ratios (49.2) are still mandatory;
you are estimating them from description + semantic knowledge (PART 20)
rather than reading them off an image. If the user's prompt itself
supplies an actual reference image, defer instead to the image-input
fork of this spec (Image_To_JSON.txt / Image_To_Js.txt), which measures
for real.

Generic proportion priors are wrong for:
  - Chibi characters (head:body = 1:2, not realistic 1:7)
  - Vehicles (cars, planes, ships have very different ratios
    than anything in PART 20 tables)
  - Architecture (towers, halls, bridges — see PART 20 for
    specific guidance but always override with image)
  - Weapons (swords, guns, staves are NOT human-shaped)
  - Furniture, props, plants, animals, environments, abstract
    forms — ALL have subject-specific proportions that generic
    tables get wrong.
Image-measured ratios ALWAYS win.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
49.1 — THE PROPORTION DECLARATION (mandatory, every render)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Before any object is written, the AI MUST output this block as
// single-line comments (the renderer strips them, so it's free
to emit but required to exist). It must appear in EVERY output,
for every subject:

  // STYLE: <style from 48.1>
  // CAMERA: <front | 3-4-front | profile | 3-4-back | top-down | 
  //         worm-eye | multi-panel>
  // SUBJECT_CATEGORY: <character | vehicle | architecture | 
  //         weapon | creature | plant | furniture | prop | 
  //         environment | abstract | other>
  // HEAD_TO_BODY: <ratio, e.g. 0.32 — OR N/A if non-character>
  // PRIMARY_ASPECT: <ratio, e.g. 2.4:1 for a car, 1:3 for a 
  //         tower, 1:1.5 for a sword, 0.4:1 for a wide building>
  // LIMB_TO_TORSO: <ratio, e.g. 0.85 — OR N/A if non-character>
  // SUBJECT_HEIGHT: <approximate world units, e.g. 2.0>
  // DOMINANT_COLORS: <hex1, hex2, hex3 sampled from image midtones>
  // BACKGROUND: <hex or "transparent" or "studio-dark" or 
  //         "studio-light" or "sky" or "ground">
  // DISTINCTIVE_DETAIL: <one specific thing the reference has 
  //         that generic priors would miss — e.g. "gold crown-
  //         pommel on sword hilt" or "chrome bumper on car" or
  //         "weathered stone texture on tower">

If ANY of these 10 lines is missing, the output is INCOMPLETE
and should be rejected by your validator (see PART 52).

[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22]: this section and PART 51.1/53.7
below all originally called this the "11-line declaration," but only 10
distinct fields are named above (STYLE/CAMERA/SUBJECT_CATEGORY/
HEAD_TO_BODY/PRIMARY_ASPECT/LIMB_TO_TORSO/SUBJECT_HEIGHT/DOMINANT_COLORS/
BACKGROUND/DISTINCTIVE_DETAIL) — the wrapped continuation lines shown
above (e.g. CAMERA's option list spanning two physical lines) aren't
separate fields, and were likely what inflated the original count. The
renderer's parser (parseProportionDeclaration(), low_poly_3d.html)
concretely checks for these same 10 named fields and reports any of them
missing by name; every "11-line"/"11-item" reference to this specific
declaration elsewhere in this document has been corrected to "10" to
match. (PART 51.1's own checklist is a genuinely separate 11-item list —
that count was correct and is untouched.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
49.2 — UNIVERSAL MEASUREMENT METHOD
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
For a single image of ANY subject:
  1. Mentally overlay a bounding box around the subject (ignore
     background, ground shadow, props outside the silhouette).
  2. Mark the top and bottom on the vertical axis, leftmost
     and rightmost on the horizontal axis.
  3. Identify the subject category (48.1 line 4).
  4. Apply the category-specific measurement checklist:

  CHARACTERS / CREATURES:
    - Head top to chin = head height
    - Chin to waist = neck + torso
    - Waist to crotch = pelvis
    - Crotch to floor = legs
    - Shoulders left to right = shoulder width
    - Waist left to right = hip width
    - Plus: limb segment ratios (upper arm / lower arm, etc.)

  VEHICLES (cars, planes, ships, trains):
    - Front bumper to rear bumper = length
    - Ground to roof = height
    - Left tire to right tire = width
    - Wheelbase, track, overhang
    - Hood to cabin ratio
    - Plus: any distinctive features (spoiler, antenna, etc.)

  ARCHITECTURE (buildings, towers, bridges, walls):
    - Foundation width to total height
    - Floor count and floor height ratio
    - Window:wall ratio
    - Roof style and pitch
    - Plus: architectural style (Gothic, modern, etc.)

  WEAPONS (swords, guns, bows, staves):
    - Blade length to handle length (swords)
    - Barrel length to grip (guns)
    - Total length vs. subject height (for scale reference)
    - Plus: pommel, guard, grip wrap details

  FURNITURE (chairs, tables, beds, shelves):
    - Width to depth to height ratios
    - Leg height to body height
    - Plus: style (modern, ornate, industrial)

  PLANTS (trees, flowers, grass, mushrooms):
    - Trunk diameter to height
    - Canopy spread to trunk height
    - Branch count and angle
    - Plus: leaf/needle style

  PROPS (everyday objects — vases, books, food, tools):
    - Use a bounding box + 1-2 dominant dimensions
    - Plus: identifying features (handle, spout, label, etc.)

  ENVIRONMENTS (rooms, landscapes, scenes):
    - Foreground to background depth
    - Sky to ground ratio
    - Plus: focal-point identification

  ABSTRACT / OTHER:
    - Use bounding box + distinctive feature list
    - Document what makes the form recognizable

  5. These measurements go into the "proportions" object
     (PART 26.2) and the actual "scale" arrays on each object.
  6. NEVER apply generic PART 20 proportion tables when an image
     is present — image-measured ratios ALWAYS win.

For multi-image (front + side / turnaround sheet / contact
sheet of any subject):
  - Measure from whichever view is most face-on for each
    dimension. Length from side, width from front, height
    from either.
  - Cross-check: side-view depth should be consistent with
    front-view width. If not, the reference itself is
    inconsistent (concept art often is) — prioritize
    whichever view shows the feature more directly.
  - For vehicles especially, a top-down view is gold — most
    AI systems default to a side view and lose 3D form.

[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22]: this entire section is AI-facing
process guidance for HOW to measure a reference during generation — the
renderer never sees the reference image, only the final numbers the AI
already decided on, so nothing above is (or could be) directly checked
by low_poly_3d.html. What the renderer DOES check is a separate,
complementary thing: a per-category STRUCTURAL spot-check on the
finished blueprint (e.g. "does a 'vehicle' have a wheel-like part
anywhere") — see PART 53.4's as-built note for exactly what's checked
per category. That check can catch "the measurement process clearly
wasn't followed" (no wheels at all) but can't confirm "the measurement
process WAS followed correctly" (correct wheelbase-to-length ratio) —
the two are complementary, not the same guarantee, and this section's
guidance remains fully in force for the AI regardless of what the
renderer can verify afterward.


================================================================================
PART 50 — v7.7 ADDITIONS: MATERIAL MAPPING TABLE (fires on every render)
================================================================================
The single most common fidelity loss across ALL subjects is
incorrect material assignment. Examples by subject category:

  - Sword hilt in a chef reference → defaulted to brown wood
    instead of gold metal
  - Car bumper → defaulted to body color instead of chrome
  - Tree trunk → defaulted to uniform brown instead of bark
    texture
  - Glass window on a building → defaulted to opaque instead
    of translucent
  - Glowing lamp / screen / eyes → defaulted to bright color
    instead of emissive
  - Fruit / food → defaulted to plastic-looking color instead
    of skin/flesh
  - Water → defaulted to blue solid instead of glass/translucent
  - Snow / ice → defaulted to white solid instead of translucent

This table makes the mapping explicit and applies to every
subject.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
50.1 — UNIVERSAL VISUAL CUE → MATERIAL PRESET MAPPING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Visual cue in reference → PART 3 preset to use:

  Sharp small highlight + dark reflections + saturated color
    → gold / silver / copper / metal (with metalness ≥ 0.8,
      roughness ≤ 0.3)
    Applies to: jewelry, sword hilts, buckles, badges, coins,
    polished armor, car bumpers, chrome trim, faucets,
    doorknobs, cutlery, polished weapons, belt buckles,
    musical instrument strings, eyeglass frames, watch faces,
    scissors, blades, drills, tools with metal parts

  Soft wide highlight + no reflection + uniform color
    → matte-fabric / cotton / leather / skin
    Applies to: cloth clothing, skin, soft armor, paper,
    rubber, unpolished wood, soft food (bread, cake, fruit
    skin), cushions, upholstery, hair, fur, fabric flags,
    leaves, grass, leather bags, paper books

  No highlight at all + flat uniform color (typical of
  flat-shaded illustrations and stylized art)
    → matte / flat-color (NOT metal, NOT glossy — even if
      the color is bright)
    Applies to: low-poly flat-shaded 3D, cel-shaded art,
    voxel Minecraft, papercraft, isometric flat art, any
    "illustration style" reference

  Visible subsurface / translucency
    → translucent / glass / wax / jade
    Applies to: stained glass, jade, wax candles, leaves in
    backlight, jelly, skin in backlight, ice, frosted glass,
    gemstones, soap, skin showing veins, onion slices, some
    plastic, resin

  Emissive halo / glow effect / lens flare
    → emissive preset with emissiveIntensity 1.0-3.0
    Applies to: lamps, screens, fire, magic, glowing eyes in
    dark, neon signs, traffic lights, vehicle headlights,
    TV/monitor displays, fairy lights, hot coals, lava,
    bioluminescence, lasers, energy beams

  Specular rainbow / iridescence
    → iridescent preset (if available) or metal with
      colorVariation; otherwise closest metallic preset
    Applies to: soap bubbles, oil slicks, beetle shells,
    peacock feathers, CDs/DVDs, abalone, mother of pearl,
    opals, some butterfly wings, holographic foil

  Rough / pitted / irregular surface
    → concrete / stone / bark with high roughness (≥ 0.8)
    Applies to: rock, brick, bark, concrete, asphalt, rusted
    metal (oxidized), rough plaster, unglazed pottery,
    sandstone, granite, dirt, gravel, cork, pumice, rough
    tree trunks, weathered wood, old stone walls

  Wood (smoothed, finished, furniture-grade)
    → wood preset with appropriate baseColor
    Applies to: furniture, finished floors, musical
    instruments, picture frames, weapon handles, tool
    handles, decorative trim, boats, barrels

  Wood (rough, bark-like, natural)
    → stone / bark with brown baseColor
    Applies to: tree trunks (natural), branches, driftwood,
    logs, rustic fences, raw lumber

  Fabric / cloth with visible weave or pattern
    → fabric preset, optionally with colorVariation for
      pattern
    Applies to: clothing, upholstery, banners, flags, tents,
    sails, curtains, tablecloths, bedding, towels

  Fur / hair / feathers
    → fur / hair preset (NOT matte-fabric — fur is visually
      distinct)
    Applies to: animal fur, human hair, bird feathers,
    down/fluff, beards, mustaches, manes, tails, plush
    toys, animal costumes

  Water (clear)
    → glass / water preset, high transparency
    Applies to: drinking glasses, water in pools, clean
    streams, ice cubes, bottles, jars

  Water (murky / ocean)
    → water preset, low transparency
    Applies to: ocean, muddy water, pond water, dirty
    puddles

  Food (soft, organic)
    → skin / food preset
    Applies to: fruit, bread, cake, vegetables, meat,
    cheese, cooked dishes

  Food (hard, crystalline)
    → stone / translucent preset
    Applies to: sugar, salt, hard candy, ice cream cones,
    crackers, biscuits, dry pasta

[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22]: this whole table is AI-facing —
the renderer has no reference image to read a highlight/reflection/
translucency cue FROM at validation time, so it can't check most of this
table at all (wood-vs-bark, fur-vs-fabric, water clarity, food type,
etc. are all genuinely unchecked). What it DOES check is a narrow,
purely-numeric subset where a declared material keyword contradicts its
own declared numeric properties, or where a color signal suggests a
likely-missed glow:
  - A material name from the "soft/organic" family (wood, cloth, skin,
    fur, leaves, etc.) combined with a declared metalness > 0.5 →
    warning ("likely mis-mapped").
  - A metallic-preset material name combined with a declared
    roughness > 0.7 → warning ("reads as dull/plastic").
  - A saturated base color (HSV saturation > 0.7) with no "emissive"
    field set, on a part smaller than 30% of the whole blueprint's
    bounding diagonal → warning (possible missed glow — see 50.3 for
    the firmer version of this same idea).
All three are warnings, never build-blocking, and only cover
CONTRADICTIONS the renderer can see in the numbers themselves — this
table's full guidance remains what the AI should actually follow during
generation; the renderer's checks are a spot-check on the output, not a
restatement of the whole table.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
50.2 — UNIVERSAL METALLIC SMALL-PARTS RULE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Small metallic details are systematically under-detected across
ALL subjects. This rule fires for every render:

  RULE: If a part is < 0.15 of the subject's total bounding
  dimension AND has a sharp small highlight in the reference,
  it is METALLIC by default. Use the closest matching metallic
  preset from PART 3 (gold, silver, copper, bronze, chrome,
  steel, etc.) with:
    - metalness: 0.9
    - roughness: 0.25
    - colorVariation: 0.05

This catches (non-exhaustive — apply to any small bright part):
  - Characters: belt buckles, sword hilts, jewelry, badges,
    buttons, rivets, armor trim, eyeglasses, pocket watches
  - Vehicles: door handles, mirrors, badges/logos, hubcaps,
    exhaust pipes, license plate frames, antenna tips
  - Architecture: doorknobs, mailboxes, light fixtures,
    house numbers, weather vanes, drainpipes
  - Weapons: crossguards, pommels, blade highlights, trigger
    guards, sight beads
  - Furniture: drawer pulls, hinges, decorative metal inlay
  - Props: zippers, buckles, latches, hinges, decorative trim
  - Electronics: screens, indicators, USB ports, buttons
  - Tools: blades, drill bits, polished metal parts, fittings

[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22]: the "< 0.15 of the subject's total
bounding dimension" threshold is implemented exactly (from an
approximate whole-blueprint bounding diagonal, computed from authored
position/scale — no real geometry exists yet at validation time, so
this is a deliberately rough estimate, not pixel-exact). "Has a sharp
small highlight in the reference" can't be checked (no reference image
at validation time) — the renderer substitutes "uses a plain/generic
material name (default/flat/matte/lowpoly/rough) instead of any real
preset" as the observable proxy, and only fires this warning under
smooth-low-poly or pbr-realistic style, since flat-low-poly/voxel/
isometric-flat/anime-cel already force everything to matte under PART
50.4 regardless — recommending "make this metallic" would be moot for a
style that can't render metal at all. Warning only, never build-blocking.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
50.3 — UNIVERSAL GLOW / EMISSIVE RULE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Bright colored parts in dark surroundings are systematically
missed as "glow" — they're painted as just-bright base color.

  RULE: If a part has a saturated color, a soft halo, or is
  clearly brighter than the lighting would justify, it is
  EMISSIVE. Add:
    - material: closest matching preset
    - emissive: same color as baseColor
    - emissiveIntensity: 1.0-3.0 depending on halo strength

This catches:
  - Lamps, lanterns, candles, flashlights, headlights, taillights
  - Screens, monitors, displays, indicators, LEDs
  - Magical glows, energy effects, fire, lava, plasma
  - Neon signs, fairy lights, string lights
  - Glowing eyes, bioluminescent creatures
  - Hot coals, embers, sparks
  - Reflective warning strips on safety gear / vehicles
  - Lit windows in night scenes

[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22]: "clearly brighter than the lighting
would justify" can't be checked against actual scene lighting at
validation time (no render exists yet), so the renderer uses a plain
HSV-based proxy: baseColor with saturation > 0.7 AND value > 0.8 AND no
"emissive" field set → warning, regardless of scene lighting context.
This will occasionally flag a legitimately bright-but-non-glowing part
(and occasionally miss a glow that isn't especially saturated) — it's a
heuristic nudge, not a certain diagnosis, and never blocks the build.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
50.4 — UNIVERSAL FLAT-LOW-POLY MATERIAL OVERRIDE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Important style-specific override: when style (PART 48) is
"flat-low-poly", "voxel", "isometric-flat", or "anime-cel",
ALL materials default to matte regardless of color, because
flat-shaded geometry has no specular response in this renderer.
If you set a sword hilt to "gold" in a flat-low-poly style, it
will render as a flat yellow shape — which is CORRECT for the
style, but means the material is being used for its COLOR, not
its physical behavior. Document this in meta.materialsNote if
you want humans reviewing the JSON to understand why a "gold"
hilt isn't shiny.

This override is style-specific, not subject-specific. A flat-
low-poly CAR and a flat-low-poly CHARACTER both get the same
override treatment.


================================================================================
PART 51 — v7.7 ADDITIONS: PRE-OUTPUT SELF-CHECK + POST-RENDER FIDELITY
          GATE (fires on every render)
================================================================================
PART 21 already has a checklist. This adds an IMAGE-SPECIFIC +
UNIVERSAL checklist that must run before JSON is emitted, and a
FIDELITY-GATE that must run AFTER rendering (in the validator),
on every render, regardless of subject.

[CONTRADICTION FIX, v7.8/PART 54] This entire PART was written assuming a
reference IMAGE exists to sample pixels/silhouettes from (51.1's "measure
proportions from the image", 51.2's "sample the rendered output's dominant
colors and compare" against a photographed reference, silhouette overlap,
hand-counted detail from "the reference"). That's true for the image-input
fork of this spec (Image_To_JSON.txt) but this fork (Prompt_To_Json.txt) has
no image at all — PART 49 already carries the equivalent fix for exactly
this problem; this PART was simply missed when that note was added. Same
translation applies here: every "image"/"reference" mention below means
"the subject as described in the text prompt, reasoned about with real-
world/genre knowledge (PART 20)" instead of actual pixel measurement.
Checks that need a real photographed image with no text-only equivalent —
51.2's item 3 (COLOR MATCH by pixel-sampling the render) and item 4
(SILHOUETTE MATCH by image overlap) — can't run literally; treat them as
"does the rendered output match what I described/declared in meta and the
PART 49.1 proportion declaration" instead of a pixel-level comparison. If
the user's prompt itself supplies an actual reference image, defer instead
to the image-input fork of this spec (Image_To_JSON.txt), which runs this
gate for real.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
51.1 — UNIVERSAL PRE-OUTPUT CHECK (the AI runs this on itself)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Before writing the first object, the AI answers these out loud
(in // comments at the top of the JSON) for EVERY subject:

  □ Did I identify the style from PART 48 and write it to 
    meta.style?
  □ Did I identify the subject category and write it to
    meta.category?
  □ Did I measure proportions from the image, NOT use generic
    PART 20 tables?
  □ Did I write the proportion declaration from 
    PART 49.1?
  □ For every small metallic-looking detail, did I apply the
    metallic small-parts rule (PART 50.2)?
  □ For every bright/glowing-looking detail, did I apply the
    emissive rule (PART 50.3)?
  □ Did I sample midtone colors (not highlights or core 
    shadows)?
  □ Did I infer occluded geometry from symmetry + visible
    materials, not leave it hollow?
  □ Did I include at least one distinctive detail from
    PART 27.7 (something only visible if you actually looked
    at the image)?
  □ Does my mental render at the reference's camera angle look
    like the reference, not a generic "<subject category>"?
  □ If my subject is non-character (vehicle, building, prop,
    etc.), did I use the category-specific measurement method
    from PART 49.2 instead of forcing character-style ratios?

If any box is unchecked, the AI is not ready to emit JSON.

[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22]: this section is written as something
the AI does to itself before emitting output. low_poly_3d.html's
postSchemaCheck() runs the same 11 checkboxes as a validator pass
AFTER the fact instead — against the blueprint the AI already produced —
so the person using the renderer can see the same checklist rather than
only trusting the AI ran it silently. Of the 11:
  - 7 are checked for real against the parsed blueprint: style set &
    valid (□1), category set & valid (□2), proportion declaration
    complete (□4), metallic small-parts rule considered — via whether
    any PART 50.2 warning fired this build (□5), emissive rule
    considered — via whether any PART 50.3 warning fired (□6),
    distinctive detail present & not generic filler (□9), and (□11)
    non-character categories checked against PART 53.4's structural
    signal — this last one shows "n/a" for character subjects, since
    the checkbox's own wording only applies to non-character ones.
  - 4 describe something that happens DURING generation and can't be
    verified from the finished JSON at all, so they're shown as
    reminders, not verdicts, never pass/fail: □3 (measured from image,
    not generic tables), □7 (sampled midtones specifically), □8
    (inferred occluded geometry), □10 (mental render matches the
    reference at its camera angle).
This checklist is surfaced in the val-log under a "Pre-flight" heading
and, unlike a true error, never blocks the build either way — it's
informational, matching this whole patch's general "warn, don't break
existing output" posture (see PART 48/49/50's as-built notes above).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
51.2 — UNIVERSAL POST-RENDER FIDELITY GATE (run in the validator)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
After the renderer produces the output, compare it against the
reference. The validator (or a separate script) should check on
EVERY render, regardless of subject:

  1. STYLE MATCH: Render style matches meta.style. Heuristic:
     count face count on the largest part. voxel = 6 faces
     (cube only), smooth-low-poly = 50-200, pbr = 500+.
  2. CATEGORY MATCH: Output's overall form matches the
     declared subject category. A car with a torso is wrong.
     A character with four wheels is wrong. A tree-shaped
     building is wrong (unless it's literally a tree-building).
  3. COLOR MATCH: Sample the rendered output's dominant colors
     and compare to the PART 49.1 declared colors. Tolerance:
     ΔE < 15 per channel. If any declared color is off by more
     than 25 in any RGB channel, the color sample was wrong.
  4. SILHOUETTE MATCH: Render the model at the reference's
     camera angle (PART 27.5). Compute a side-by-side silhouette
     overlap. If overlap < 70%, proportions are wrong.
  5. DETAIL COUNT: Count visible distinct parts in the rendered
     output. Compare to a hand-counted "expected" number from
     the reference. If rendered has < 60% of expected, detail
     was lost.
  6. METALLIC-PRESENCE: If the reference has visible metallic
     details, scan the rendered output for material="*" presets
     that are metallic. If zero, small-parts rule (PART 50.2)
     was not applied.
  7. EMISSIVE-PRESENCE: If the reference has visible glow /
     emissive details, check that emissiveIntensity > 0 is set
     on the relevant parts. If zero and reference has glow,
     emissive rule (PART 50.3) was not applied.
  8. STYLE-COHERENT-MATERIALS: All materials in the output
     must be consistent with the declared style (PART 48.3).
     A "voxel" style with metallic materials is wrong.

If any check fails, flag the output as FIDELITY-LOW and either
regenerate with a stronger prompt, or show the user a "this
render doesn't match the reference well" warning.

[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22]: implemented as fidelityGate(),
called after every build (including Inspector-triggered live edits),
shown as a "Fidelity: N/100" HUD chip, a val-log breakdown, and the
Inspector's Fidelity tab. Score = percentage of APPLICABLE checks that
passed; a check that's not applicable/not implementable is excluded
from the denominator entirely rather than counted as a pass or a fail.
Per check:
  1. STYLE MATCH — implemented via largest-part triangle count, using
     this section's own example bands widened somewhat (voxel ≤ 24
     tris; smooth-low-poly 20-500; pbr-realistic/photogrammetry 300+).
     Styles this section gives no concrete band for (chibi, anime-cel,
     toon, papercraft, isometric-flat, low-poly-painterly, wireframe,
     x-ray) have no check here — marked n/a rather than guessed at.
  2. CATEGORY MATCH — implemented as a structural signal check (shared
     with PART 53.4/the validator's category-coherence pass), not a
     full "matches the declared form" judgment — see PART 53.4's
     as-built note for exactly what's checked per category.
  3. COLOR MATCH — NOT IMPLEMENTED. Reading back actual rendered pixel
     colors from the live WebGL canvas to compare against PART 49.1's
     declared colors turned out to be fragile in practice (the
     renderer's drawing buffer isn't guaranteed to still hold the
     previous frame's contents by the time a comparison would run) and
     was judged not worth the added complexity/risk for this patch.
     Marked n/a, excluded from the score.
  4. SILHOUETTE MATCH — [UPDATED, PART 59] now IMPLEMENTED when a
     reference image is attached (Compare mode / the Prompt Builder's
     reference library, PART 52). Renders the current build to a 64x64
     alpha grid, samples the attached reference image to the same grid,
     and scores intersection-over-union; pass threshold is IoU ≥ 50%.
     With no reference image attached — the common case for this
     prompt-only fork — the check has nothing to sample and stays
     marked n/a, excluded from the score, same as before. See PART 59
     for the full write-up.
  5. DETAIL COUNT — implemented against a hand-set MINIMUM object count
     per category (character ≥ 8, prop ≥ 5, and reasonable
     extrapolations at the same granularity for the rest) rather than
     "60% of a hand-counted expected number from the reference" — there
     is no reference count available to the renderer to compare
     against, so a fixed per-category floor substitutes for it.
  6/7. METALLIC-PRESENCE / EMISSIVE-PRESENCE — this section's wording
     ("if the reference has visible metallic/glow details...") can't be
     evaluated without a reference the renderer can analyze, so each is
     only SCORED when something else in the same build signals metal/
     glow is expected (the PART 49.1 DISTINCTIVE_DETAIL text, meta.
     description, or — for metallic only — a category where hardware is
     typical: vehicle/weapon/furniture). Otherwise marked n/a. This is a
     deliberate softening of the section's literal wording: scoring
     these unconditionally would fail perfectly ordinary subjects that
     simply have no metal or glow parts at all (a plain wooden chair
     isn't wrong for having zero metallic materials).
  8. STYLE-COHERENT-MATERIALS — implemented for the 4 PART 50.4 styles
     (flat-low-poly/voxel/isometric-flat/anime-cel) by checking the
     ACTUAL built mesh materials for stray metalness/transmission. This
     should always pass once PART 50.4's override has run — it exists
     as an end-to-end confirmation that the override actually took
     effect, not a second independent enforcement mechanism. For every
     other style, marked n/a (no coherence rule defined for them here).


================================================================================
PART 52 — v7.7 ADDITIONS: UI / PROMPT CHANGES (applies to the HTML viewer,
          low_poly_3d.html)
================================================================================
[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22 — supersedes the tab-split note below
this line, kept struck through for history]: low_poly_3d.html is a single
unified renderer/viewer — there is no separate "Text-to-3D tab" /
"Image-to-3D tab" split; one never existed. Everything in this PART lives
behind two new toolbar buttons — "📝 Prompt" and "🖼 Compare" — plus a new
"Fidelity" tab added to the existing right-hand Inspector panel.
Reference images are attached ONCE, inside the Prompt panel, and that
same attachment is what powers Compare mode, the Fidelity tab, and the
multi-view/resolution checks in PART 53 — there is no separate per-tab
image slot to route between. This renderer makes no network or AI API
calls anywhere in its own code; reference images exist purely for
on-screen comparison and are never transmitted anywhere.

~~NOTE FOR THE TEXT-PROMPT (NO-IMAGE) TAB: low_poly_3d.html's Text-to-3D~~
~~tab has no reference-image dropzone, so 52.2 (compare overlay), 52.3~~
~~(JSON inspector fidelity tab's image-vs-render diff), and 52.5~~
~~(few-shot from saved outputs) apply as described only once the user~~
~~also attaches a reference image via the Image-to-3D tab. On the~~
~~Text-to-3D tab itself, only 52.1 (structured style-first prompt~~
~~template) and 52.4 (regenerate-with-context) are directly actionable —~~
~~apply those two now, and treat 52.2/52.3/52.5 as shared-UI features~~
~~that light up once an image is present.~~

The HTML viewer's dropzone and prompt area can be improved to
catch fidelity issues before the AI is even called. These UI
changes apply to every model, every subject.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
52.1 — UNIVERSAL STYLE-FIRST PROMPT TEMPLATE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
[AS BUILT]: implemented as a new floating "Prompt Builder" panel (📝
Prompt toolbar button), added ALONGSIDE the existing JSON dropzone/Quick-
JSON box rather than replacing it — pasting JSON directly still works
exactly as before, with no template involved, which serves the same
purpose as the "skip template" toggle this section originally called for
(the panel is opt-in, not forced; nothing about the old paste-JSON flow
changed). The panel contains:

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Category:          [dropdown — the 11 PART 49.1 values]    │
  │ Style:             [dropdown — the 13 PART 48.1 values]    │
  │ Camera angle:      [front / 3-4 / profile / back / top /  │
  │                     worm-eye / unspecified]                │
  │ Background:        [studio-dark / studio-light / sky /    │
  │                     transparent / specific-hex]            │
  │ Additional notes (optional): [text]                        │
  │ Reference image(s) (optional): [attach — see 52-note above]│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Clicking "Generate prompt" assembles one text block — any PART 52.5
few-shot examples, then the category/style/camera/background/notes
above, then the PART 53.7 reminder appended at the end — shown in a
read-only textarea with a Copy button. The user pastes this into
whichever AI chat they use (this renderer has no direct AI connection of
its own) and pastes the returned JSON/JS back into the Quick JSON box.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
52.2 — REFERENCE-VS-OUTPUT COMPARE MODE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
[AS BUILT]: implemented as the translucent-overlay option only — the
side-by-side/synchronized-orbit alternative this section also offered
was not built, since the overlay alone already addresses the "why
doesn't it look like the reference" problem this section names, and
stays correct across arbitrary camera positions with far less UI. "🖼
Compare" toggles the attached reference image over the viewport at an
adjustable opacity (0-100% slider); if more than one reference image is
attached, a pill button cycles between them. The fidelity score itself
is NOT a separate badge inside the compare overlay — it doesn't need to
be, since the persistent "Fidelity: N/100" HUD chip (PART 51.2) next to
the status pill stays visible during Compare mode too. Clicking "🖼
Compare" with no reference image attached shows a toast and opens the
Prompt panel to its attach section, rather than doing nothing silently.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
52.3 — JSON INSPECTOR FIDELITY TAB
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
[AS BUILT]: matches as specified, plus one addition. The right-hand
Inspector gained an "Object / Fidelity" tab strip; the Fidelity tab
shows, on every build:
  - meta.style and meta.category (flagged if absent/unrecognized)
  - The proportion declaration (PART 49.1 — see that section's as-built
    note on its actual field count)
  - Materials-by-object list
  - Four booleans, each sourced from the PART 51.2 fidelity gate:
    "Style-coherent materials", "Metallic parts present", "Emissive
    parts present", "Category-coherent proportions"
  - ADDED (not in the original spec): a per-category benchmark — this
    device's average fidelity score for the current meta.category over
    its last logged builds (PART 53.5), so a single build's score has
    some context instead of standing alone

Clicking a boolean jumps to the specific object named in its underlying
detail message when one is named (e.g. a style-coherence violation names
the offending object); for scene-wide booleans with no single object to
point to, it shows the detail as a toast rather than doing nothing.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
52.4 — REGENERATE-WITH-CONTEXT BUTTON
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
[AS BUILT]: same intent, a superset of the format originally given.
Clicking "🔁 Regenerate with current scene's context" pre-fills the
Prompt panel's category/style dropdowns from the current blueprint's
meta, and the generated prompt includes:

  PART 52.4 — REGENERATE WITH CONTEXT.
  Keep the overall structure/proportions/object count the same. Only
  fix what's named below plus "additional notes" — don't otherwise
  redesign it.
  Previous meta: <meta object>
  Previous object count: <n>
  Fidelity-gate issues found last time: <the specific failed PART 51.2
    checks and their detail messages, or a note that none were found>

Any reference image already attached stays attached across a regenerate
— there's a single persistent attachment, not a per-request one, so
nothing needs to explicitly restate "use the same image" the way the
original bracketed format did. Concrete PART 51.2 failure details were
used in place of a bare proportion-declaration restatement, since naming
the SPECIFIC thing to fix is more actionable than asking the AI to
re-derive it from a category/style label alone.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
52.5 — REFERENCE LIBRARY + FEW-SHOT (per-style, per-category)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
[AS BUILT]: implemented as the style×category bucket only — the
separate "style alone" and "category alone" buckets this section also
described were not built. Style×category is the most specific of the
three, and in practice the most useful; one bucket type keeps the
localStorage schema and the Prompt panel's library browser simpler.
Every successful build with BOTH meta.style and meta.category set is
saved automatically (capped at 5 most-recent entries per bucket, each
trimmed to meta + a compact per-object id/type/material/color summary —
not the full blueprint, to keep entries small). When "Generate prompt"
runs, up to 2 saved entries from the matching bucket are prepended to
the output as few-shot examples; if the bucket is empty, the prompt
proceeds without that section, exactly as originally specified.

The library is also user-browsable (list + delete, per bucket) inside
the Prompt panel — not originally specified, but a natural consequence
of the data existing at all.


================================================================================
PART 53 — v7.7 ADDITIONS: UNIVERSAL WORKFLOW RECOMMENDATIONS
================================================================================
These are not part of the LBL language itself but are best
practices for operating a system that uses LBL. All apply to
every subject, every render.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
53.1 — MULTI-VIEW INPUT WHEN POSSIBLE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A single 3-4 front view is the worst case for a 3D modeler
because ~60% of the subject is occluded. If the user can
provide:
  - Front + back, or
  - Front + side, or
  - Top-down, or
  - A turnaround sheet (front/3-4/back/side in one image),
the AI's PART 27.6 (occlusion handling) has much less to
infer, and PART 27.5 (view-angle consistency check) becomes
possible. The renderer's multi-image upload is already there
— make the UI actively recommend a second view, not just
allow it.

Subject-specific view recommendations:
  - Characters: front + side (or turnaround sheet) is ideal
  - Vehicles: top-down + side is ideal (catches 3D form best)
  - Architecture: front + oblique-elevated, or photo + plan
  - Weapons: side + top (or handle-down, blade-up)
  - Plants: side + top (catches canopy spread)
  - Furniture: front + side (or 3-4)
  - Props: any single clear view is usually sufficient
  - Environments: 2+ wide views at different angles

[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22]: "the renderer's multi-image upload
is already there" was not true when this line was written — checked
directly against low_poly_3d.html's source before this patch, there was
no image-upload mechanism anywhere in the file, single- or multi-image.
It's genuinely there now: the Prompt panel's reference-image attach
accepts multiple images (click or drag/drop), and shows an inline tip —
"attach a second view" when exactly one image is attached, or an
affirming note when 2+ are. The 8 subject-specific view recommendations
above were NOT individually implemented as separate per-category tips —
only the generic 1-image-vs-2+-images distinction is surfaced; this
list remains valid guidance for a person deciding what to photograph/
attach, just not separately wired to the category dropdown.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
53.2 — HIGH-RES REFERENCE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Below ~512px on the long edge, the AI's color sampling (PART
27.4) becomes unreliable. Recommend ≥ 1024px reference images
in the dropzone UI. If a smaller image is uploaded, upscale
it (the renderer can do this on the client side with a simple
nearest-neighbor) before sending to the AI.

This applies equally to all subjects — pixel-level color
sampling on a 256px reference is unreliable whether the
subject is a face, a car, or a tree.

[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22]: implemented at the ~512px failure
threshold specifically (any attached image with its shorter side under
512px gets flagged with an upscale offer), not as a softer "recommend
1024px+" nudge below that. "Before sending to the AI" doesn't apply
here — this renderer makes no network/AI calls at all, so nothing is
ever sent anywhere; the upscale button (real nearest-neighbor, 2×,
capped at 2048px) exists purely so the person can look at a bigger
canvas themselves, or attach the upscaled version when they paste their
own prompt into whatever AI chat they use. Nearest-neighbor was chosen
specifically because it invents no new detail, unlike a smoothing
upscale would.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
53.3 — STYLE MISMATCH DETECTION IN THE PROMPT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
If a user uploads a voxel reference but the system prompt is
configured for smooth-low-poly (or vice versa), the output will
be hopeless. Add a pre-flight check:
  - User uploads image.
  - Quick classifier (or a fast vision model call) returns a
    predicted style.
  - If predicted style ≠ system's default style, show a banner:
    "This reference looks like a [predicted style] model. The
    current system is optimized for [default style]. Output
    quality may be reduced. Switch style? [Y/N]"

This applies to all subject categories — a voxel car and a
voxel character both need the voxel pipeline.

[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22]: neither "quick classifier" nor "fast
vision model call" was implemented — this renderer makes no network or
AI API calls anywhere in its own code, so a real classifier was never
actually on the table for this artifact specifically (a genuine vision
model call would need one). What runs instead is a coarse, fully local,
offline heuristic: downsample the attached image to 32×32, quantize
each pixel to 16 levels per channel, count distinct colors, and bucket
"≤ 40 distinct colors" as flat/hard-edged-looking, otherwise as smooth/
gradient-looking. Every PART 48.1 style falls into one of those two
buckets except chibi, which is left unbucketed since it legitimately
renders either way. The banner text says plainly that this is "a local
color-diversity heuristic, not a real classifier" — it's meant as a
"double-check this" nudge a person can dismiss, not a prediction to
trust the way a real vision model's output might be.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
53.4 — CATEGORY-SPECIFIC FAILURE LIBRARY
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Build a small library of "common failure modes by category"
that the AI can be told about when generating that category.
This is meta-knowledge that doesn't change render-to-render
but dramatically improves output.

  CHARACTERS common failures:
    - Wrong head:body ratio (snap to realism)
    - Limb detachment (no skeleton integrity)
    - Loss of facial features (eyebrows, nose, mouth)
    - Generic body type regardless of reference build
    - Symmetric pose when reference is asymmetric

  VEHICLES common failures:
    - Wheels missing or as plain cylinders (no tire/wheel
      distinction)
    - Windows opaque (default to glass?)
    - Lost badges / trim / chrome details
    - Wrong wheelbase (too short, too long)
    - Generic box-shaped car regardless of reference type

  ARCHITECTURE common failures:
    - All buildings become "house with peaked roof"
    - Windows uniformly sized (real buildings have varied
      windows)
    - Lost architectural style (Gothic, modern, etc.)
    - Roof geometry wrong (flat vs. peaked vs. domed)
    - Doors / entrances too small or in wrong position

  WEAPONS common failures:
    - Sword hilt defaulted to brown wood (loses metal)
    - Gun trigger / sights missing
    - Bow string missing or wrong color
    - Wrong overall length (sword too short for the user)
    - Lost decorative details (engravings, inlays)

  PLANTS common failures:
    - Trunk uniform brown (loses bark texture)
    - Canopy single sphere (loses tree shape)
    - All leaves same size and orientation
    - Wrong leaf/needle style (deciduous vs. conifer)
    - No branches or too many branches

  FURNITURE common failures:
    - Legs too thin or too thick
    - Missing cushions / upholstery
    - Lost style (modern vs. ornate)
    - Wrong proportions (chair too tall, table too narrow)
    - Lost material (wood defaulted to plastic-looking)

  PROPS common failures:
    - Lost distinctive features (a teapot without a spout)
    - Wrong scale (a key as big as a person)
    - Generic material (every prop gets same default)
    - Missing labels, decals, text
    - Wrong aspect ratio

  ENVIRONMENTS common failures:
    - Flat background with no depth
    - Sky/ground ratio wrong
    - All detail on the foreground
    - Lost atmospheric perspective
    - Generic "grass + tree" output regardless of biome

  ABSTRACT / OTHER common failures:
    - Snap to a known subject (abstract sculpture becomes
      "a person")
    - Loss of distinctive geometric features
    - Uniform color / no surface variation
    - Wrong proportions in the abstract sense

This failure library is consulted by the AI on every render
based on meta.category, not hard-coded into the prompt.

[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22]: this full library remains valid
AI-facing guidance, consulted the way this section describes. The
renderer ADDITIONALLY runs one automated structural spot-check per
category (shared between the validator's warnings and the PART 51.2
fidelity gate's CATEGORY MATCH check), covering 7 of the 9 groups above
— vehicle, architecture, weapon, plant, furniture, prop, environment.
Characters/creatures and abstract/other have no renderer-side check
(character integrity would need real skeleton/pose analysis, and
abstract/other is inherently too open-ended for a structural marker to
mean anything). For each of the 7, only ONE representative signal from
that category's failure list is actually checked — not the full 5-item
list above — since these are meant as fast, cheap structural spot-
checks, not a complete audit:
  - vehicle: any part with a rubber/tire-sounding material name, or an
    id containing "wheel", or a spoked-wheel generator
  - architecture: at least 4 distinct objects (catches "single box +
    cone" oversimplification)
  - weapon: either an elongated part (≥ 3:1 on its longest vs. shortest
    scale axis) or at least one metallic-preset material
  - plant: no bark/wood-named material also has metalness > 0.1
  - furniture: at least 4 distinct objects
  - prop: a non-empty, non-generic DISTINCTIVE_DETAIL in the PART 49.1
    declaration
  - environment: at least 3 distinct objects
A failing signal produces a warning naming the specific common-failure
text above, not a hard error.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
53.5 — FIDELITY LOG + PER-CATEGORY BENCHMARK
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Log every render's PART 51.2 results, broken down by:
  - meta.category
  - meta.style
  - fidelity score per check (1-8)
  - overall pass/fail

Over time, this reveals which category × style combinations
produce the lowest fidelity. Use this to:
  - Prioritize few-shot examples for weak combinations
  - Identify prompt weaknesses per category
  - Spot renderer bugs (consistent low scores in a specific
    check across all renders = bug, not model weakness)

[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22]: logs {timestamp, meta.style,
meta.category, overall score, per-check name+pass/fail} to localStorage
on every build, capped at the 200 most recent entries (a cap this
section didn't specify; added so the log can't grow unbounded). The
Inspector's Fidelity tab surfaces this device's average score for the
CURRENT build's category over its logged history, giving a single
build's score some context. The three follow-on uses this section lists
— prioritizing few-shot examples, identifying prompt weaknesses,
spotting renderer bugs — remain manual-analysis use cases for whoever
reviews the log; none of the three is automated by the renderer itself
beyond making the raw log and the one per-category average visible.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
53.6 — MODEL INTEGRITY (re-state from PART 36-45)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The model integrity rules apply to every subject, not just
characters. Re-verify these fire on every render:

  - Connected forms (no floating parts) — applies to
    vehicle wheels, building roofs, weapon parts, furniture
    legs, etc., not just character limbs.
  - Pose matches the reference (not a generic default pose)
    — applies to any posed subject.
  - No missing parts (every visible mass in the reference
    has a corresponding object in the JSON) — applies to
    all subjects.
  - No hallucinated parts (no extra objects that aren't in
    the reference) — applies to all subjects; AI adding
    "spare tire" to a car reference without one in the
    image is a hallucination.
  - Scale consistency (all parts in correct proportion to
    each other) — applies to all subjects.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
53.7 — DEFAULT PROMPT ADDITIONS (paste at the end of every
        system prompt)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
[v7.8/PART 54 note, extended 2026-07-25 for PART 57: PARTs 54-57 (the
20-technique vocabulary pass, positioning-integrity audit, contradiction
audit, and pixel-perfect fidelity pass) are appended AFTER this section
in this reference document's physical layout, but their content should be
treated as already known/applied BEFORE this final reminder fires — this
reminder is meant to be the actual last thing checked before output, per
its own instruction below. In particular, treat PART 57.2's box_subdivided
+round+smoothing mandate for character cores/hero props and PART 57.6's
eyeTrack default as already-applied constraints on items 1/3/5 above, not
optional extras to layer on afterward. — Cross-file
consistency note, added 2026-07-22: this note is present in
Prompt_To_Js.txt/Image_To_Js.txt but was missing from this file and
from Image_To_JSON.txt before now; added to both for consistency, since
it's about physical document layout, not JSON-vs-JS output format.]

Add this block to the END of the system prompt, after all
specs are pasted. It serves as a final reminder before the
AI emits output:

  ============================================================
  REMINDER — APPLY ON EVERY RENDER, EVERY SUBJECT:
  
  1. Identify the style (PART 48). Lock it. Write to meta.style.
  2. Identify the subject category. Write to meta.category.
  3. Measure proportions from the image (PART 49). Write the
     proportion declaration as // comments at top.
  4. Sample midtone colors. Apply metallic small-parts rule
     (PART 50.2) and emissive rule (PART 50.3).
  5. Check against the 11-item pre-output checklist (PART 51.1).
  6. Use subject-category-specific measurement method from
     PART 49.2 (not generic character ratios for non-characters).
  7. Output ONLY valid JSON. No markdown fences. No
     explanations before or after.
  ============================================================

[AS-BUILT NOTE, applied 2026-07-22]: this exact 7-item block (verbatim,
not paraphrased) is what the renderer's Prompt Builder panel appends to
every prompt it generates — an earlier pass at wiring this up had
substituted its own paraphrase from memory, which diverged from the
real wording here and, critically, dropped item 7's "no markdown
fences" instruction; that's been corrected to use this exact text. Item
7 is templated per output format in the renderer's other 3 spec files —
see Prompt_To_Js.txt/Image_To_Js.txt for the JS-script equivalent.

PART 54 — v7.8 ADDITIONS: 20-TECHNIQUE CG-PIPELINE VOCABULARY PASS
================================================================================
A pass through 20 named CG techniques (Subdivision + Sculpting, Displacement
Mapping, Parametric Modeling, Dynamic Topology, Hand-painted Texture, UV
Mapping, Decals, Normal Mapping, MatCap Shading, Separate Mesh + Texture,
Micro-polygons / Tessellation, Vector Displacement, Nanite Virtualized
Geometry, PBR Texture Sets, Detail Normal / Detail Mask, Procedural
Textures, Triplanar Projection, Eye Shader with Refraction, Parallax
Occlusion Mapping, Eye Tracking Rig), checking each one against the actual
renderer source rather than assuming the name alone justifies a new field.
Each is tagged with its real status:
  [ALREADY LIVE]   the renderer already did this — documented properly here
                    for the first time, possibly with a small extension
  [NEW]             a genuinely new renderer capability added in v7.8
  [ASPIRATIONAL]    honestly NOT implementable as the real technique in a
                    WebGL/Three.js renderer — explained why, and pointed at
                    the closest real equivalent this renderer actually has

A NOTE ON THE ELEPHANT IN THE ROOM: several of these 20 names (Subdivision,
Tessellation, Vector Displacement, Nanite in particular) are, by their
original definition, HIGH-detail/HIGH-polygon techniques from film and
AAA-game pipelines — the opposite instinct from "low poly." That's not a
contradiction to paper over; it's addressed head-on per technique below.
The resolution is the same one real-time game art has used for two
decades: keep the actual mesh low-poly (few visible facets, cheap to
render) and fake additional richness with SHADING — normal maps, detail
normals, matcaps, triplanar texture, decals, PBR texture sets. That's why
most of this PART is shading/texturing techniques, and why the few
genuinely geometry-hungry ones (Nanite, hardware tessellation, true
adaptive dynamic topology) are marked [ASPIRATIONAL] rather than faked at
the cost of the low-poly look this whole renderer exists for. Every
opt-in field below defaults to OFF/unset — no existing blueprint's render
changes because this PART exists.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.0 — STATUS TABLE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
   1. Subdivision + Sculpting     [ALREADY LIVE, +NEW]  54.1
   2. Displacement Mapping       [ALREADY LIVE]         54.2
   3. Parametric Modeling        [ALREADY LIVE]         54.3
   4. Dynamic Topology           [ASPIRATIONAL]         54.4
   5. Hand-painted Texture       [NEW]                  54.5
   6. UV Mapping                 [ALREADY LIVE, +NEW]   54.6
   7. Decals                     [NEW]                  54.7
   8. Normal Mapping             [ALREADY LIVE]         54.8
   9. MatCap Shading             [NEW]                  54.9
  10. Separate Mesh + Texture    [ALREADY LIVE]         54.10
  11. Micro-polygons/Tessellation[ASPIRATIONAL]         54.11
  12. Vector Displacement        [NEW]                  54.12
  13. Nanite Virtualized Geometry[ASPIRATIONAL]         54.13
  14. PBR Texture Sets           [NEW extension]        54.14
  15. Detail Normal / Detail Mask[NEW]                  54.15
  16. Procedural Textures        [ALREADY LIVE, +NEW]   54.16
  17. Triplanar Projection       [ALREADY LIVE, +NEW]   54.17
  18. Eye Shader with Refraction [NEW extension]         54.18
  19. Parallax Occlusion Mapping [NEW]                  54.19
  20. Eye Tracking Rig           [NEW]                  54.20

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.1 — SUBDIVISION + SCULPTING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Subdivision (real Loop subdivision, smooths + adds geometry) already
existed: "subdivisionLevel": 1-3 on any object. "Sculpting" already existed
too, as GLOBAL parametric deformers — taper/bulge/twist/roundness directly
on an object, plus operations like noise/pinch/inflate, plus SDF blobs and
fractal displacement. All of that reshapes the WHOLE mesh from one formula.
NEW in v7.8 — a real LOCALIZED brush, closer to what "sculpting" usually
means (ZBrush/Blender push-pull/pinch/smooth at a point, not a global
formula). Add to any object's "operations" array:
  { "type": "sculptStamp", "params": {
      "mode": "pull", "center": [0, 0.3, 0.1], "radius": 0.25,
      "strength": 0.15, "falloff": "smooth" } }
  "mode": "push" (dent inward along normal) | "pull" (bump outward along
    normal) | "pinch" (draw vertices toward the stamp's center axis) |
    "smooth" (locally relax toward neighbor average — softens an
    over-aggressive stamp, or blunts a sharp CSG seam)
  "center": [x,y,z] in the object's OWN local space (same space as its
    geometry, before position/rotation are applied)
  "radius": world units, "strength": 0-1ish, "falloff": "smooth"
    (smoothstep, the natural-looking default) | "linear" (harder edge)
Stamp it several times at different centers/radii to sculpt up a feature
progressively, the same way an actual sculptor works in passes rather than
one global deformer.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.2 — DISPLACEMENT MAPPING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live: any object with "texturePattern" set gets a matching
procedural displacementMap for free; set "displacementAmount" (world
units) to actually push geometry along the vertex normal using it. This is
SCALAR displacement (always along the normal) — see 54.12 for true
arbitrary-direction vector displacement, which this is not.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.3 — PARAMETRIC MODELING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live — this is the foundation of the entire blueprint format, not
a bolt-on feature: every primitive/generator (sphere, box, chain, sweep,
lathe, superellipsoid, and 30+ others — PART 2/9-14) is defined by named
NUMERIC PARAMETERS rather than raw vertex data, which is the actual
definition of parametric modeling (a shape driven by adjustable inputs,
regenerable from those inputs rather than hand-sculpted point by point).
Nothing to add here; naming it explicitly so it isn't mistaken for a gap.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.4 — DYNAMIC TOPOLOGY  [ASPIRATIONAL — see reasoning below]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Real dynamic topology (ZBrush/Blender's Dyntopo/multires: the mesh
RE-TRIANGULATES on the fly as you sculpt, adding density only where a
brush is actively adding detail) is fundamentally an OFFLINE AUTHORING-TOOL
algorithm, not a real-time rendering technique — there's no "renderer" to
add it to; it belongs to a sculpting application this project doesn't
have or aim to be. It's also not the same shape of problem as this
renderer's build pipeline, which generates a mesh once from parameters
rather than incrementally re-meshing under an artist's cursor.
The closest HONEST equivalent already available: "subdivisionLevel" (54.1)
sets a FIXED, uniform density per object at build time, and 54.1's new
sculptStamp adds detail only where you actually stamp — which is the
practical, achievable spirit of "topology follows where detail is added,"
even though it's a fixed build-time choice rather than a live adaptive
re-mesh. If genuinely adaptive re-triangulation (subdividing only the
triangles near a stamp, leaving the rest of the mesh at its original
density) is needed later, that's a real, larger feature to design
carefully — it's not safely retrofittable as a quick addition, since naive
partial subdivision creates T-junction cracks at the boundary between
subdivided and non-subdivided regions unless handled deliberately.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.5 — HAND-PAINTED TEXTURE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
NEW procedural pattern, same pipeline as every other "texturePattern"
value: "texturePattern": "hand_painted" (aliases: "handpainted",
"painterly"). Distinct from "stone"/"noise" (small, uniform, high-
frequency speckle): this layers a few large, irregular, low-frequency
color blotches — the uneven tonal pooling a real brush leaves — with
faint directional streaking on top (the visible drag of bristles), which
reads as painted rather than computer-generated. Works with every other
texturePattern option (textureScale, textureContrast, normalStrength,
pbrTextureSet, detailPattern — 54.14/54.15).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.6 — UV MAPPING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live — every primitive gets real UVs automatically (box
projection, picking the dominant axis per vertex from its normal), a
prerequisite this renderer has always satisfied for texturePattern/decals/
etc. to work at all. NEW in v7.8: this is now an explicit, choosable field
instead of only an automatic behind-the-scenes step —
  "uvProjection": "box" (default) | "triplanar" | "spherical" | "cylindrical"
                  | "front" | "top" | "side"
"triplanar" was previously ONLY auto-applied to CSG/heavily-twisted
geometry (54.17 covers it as its own technique). "spherical" (longitude/
latitude unwrap from the object's own bbox center) suits round props —
planets, orbs, domes, eyes — where box projection pinches visibly at the
poles. "cylindrical" (angle-around-Y + height) suits columns, bottles,
limbs, trunks. "front" / "top" / "side" (added in the same v7.8 pass, per
PART 58) are flat ORTHOGRAPHIC projections from one world axis — EVERY
vertex gets the same 2D projection regardless of which face it's on
(unlike box, which picks per-vertex by dominant normal axis), so use
these for a mostly-planar shape meant to read as a single flat reference
image mapped straight on — a sign, a card, a creature/prop authored (or
photographed, on the image-input fork) from one canonical angle — not for
a fully 3D rounded form, where they'll visibly stretch/compress on any
face not facing that axis. "front"=XY plane, "top"=XZ plane, "side"=ZY
plane; each is bbox-normalized to 0-1 independently, so no extra remap is
needed even if the LBL's own texture assumes 0-1 coverage. Omit the field
entirely to keep the exact previous behavior (box, or auto-triplanar on
flagged geometry).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.7 — DECALS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
NEW. A top-level blueprint field, since a decal targets an object rather
than being one itself:
  "decals": [
    { "onId": "torso", "position": [0, 1.1, 0.31],
      "rotation": [0, 0, 0], "size": [0.3, 0.3, 0.3],
      "color": "#cc2222", "texturePattern": "noise", "opacity": 1 }
  ]
Uses THREE's real DecalGeometry, which projects onto the TARGET's actual
triangles/curvature — genuinely different from placing another primitive
near the surface, since it conforms to the target's own shape (an insignia
that follows a sphere's curve, a scorch mark that wraps a CSG-cut chunk's
real cut face) instead of floating as a flat independent shape that a
plain box/plane could never match. "onId" must match a real object id
(the validator errors if it doesn't — a decal with no target can't project
onto anything). "position"/"rotation"/"size" are WORLD-space, matching
DecalGeometry's own convention. "texturePattern"/"color" work the same as
any other material; omit "texturePattern" for a flat-colored decal.
Renders after CSG and every ref-based rebuild, so it's always projected
onto each target's FINAL shape, never a stale pre-boolean one.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.8 — NORMAL MAPPING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live: any "texturePattern" automatically generates a matching
procedural normalMap alongside its color map (both derived from the same
underlying height field, so the shading detail actually lines up with the
color variation instead of being unrelated noise). Tune with
"normalStrength" (how pronounced the bump is) and "normalScale" (THREE's
own normalScale multiplier). This is THE standard way real-time engines
fake high-poly surface detail on a low-poly mesh — see the 54.0 framing
note on why this, not more geometry, is the intended direction for most of
this PART.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.9 — MATCAP SHADING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
