  35. Neural texture synthesis / diffusion   [ASPIRATIONAL]          62.35

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.1 — PARAMETRIC / PROCEDURAL MODELING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already documented at PART 54.3 — the foundation of the whole blueprint
format (every primitive/generator is driven by named numeric parameters).
Nothing new to add; restating here only so the full 35-item list has no
silent gaps.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.2 — CONSTRUCTIVE SOLID GEOMETRY (CSG)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live — real boolean union/intersection/subtraction operations
exist on the renderer's CSG path (verified in code: 60+ references across
the CSG build/carve functions). Union, intersection, and subtraction are
all real mesh-boolean results, not faked with material tricks or
overlapping geometry.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.3 — SDF / RAY MARCHING  [ALREADY LIVE, partial]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The MODELING half of this technique is real and live: "sdf_blob" (aliases
"metaball"/"metaballs"/"blobfield") defines a shape implicitly as a field
of signed-distance "balls" and extracts a real polygon mesh from it via
marching cubes (62.4) — the same conceptual pipeline as SDF modeling in
Houdini/Blender. What is NOT live is the RENDER-time half of the name —
true per-pixel ray marching against a distance function in a fragment
shader (Shadertoy-style, geometry-free rendering). That's a fundamentally
different rendering architecture (a full-screen raymarch shader instead
of a triangle mesh) and isn't compatible with this renderer's whole
triangle-mesh/lighting/shadow/post-processing pipeline — it would mean
replacing the renderer, not extending it. The closest honest equivalent:
sdf_blob gives you the SDF-defined-shape workflow, output as a normal
mesh so it still gets real shadows, SSAO, and every material this
renderer supports.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.4 — MARCHING CUBES / MARCHING TETRAHEDRA
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live — sdf_blob's mesh extraction IS marching cubes (see the
existing PART 46 auto-enhancement note on "Techniques 7/8 (Marching
Cubes/Poisson reconstruction)": raising "resolution" on an sdf_blob
raises marching-cubes resolution directly). Marching tetrahedra
specifically is not a separate option — not needed, since marching cubes
already produces a clean manifold mesh for every sdf_blob use case this
renderer has.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.5 — METABALLS / IMPLICIT SURFACES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live — this is the same sdf_blob feature as 62.3/62.4 under its
more common name: a field of blended "balls" (position/strength/subtract
per ball) produces one smooth blobby surface. Confirmed real, not just a
naming alias, by the code review referenced above.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.6 — L-SYSTEMS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live (added v6.6) — a real string-rewriting turtle-graphics
L-system generator (genLSystem/lsystemExpand), reachable via "type":
"lsystem" (alias "grammar"). Rule-based generation of branching
structures (trees, root systems, and similar) works exactly as the
technique's name describes, not a lookalike approximation.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.7 — FRACTAL GEOMETRY (MANDELBULB/MENGER SPONGE/IFS)  [ALREADY LIVE, partial]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A real fractal generator exists ("type": "fractal", genFractal): a base
icosphere recursively displaced across several passes, each pass adding
finer, weaker detail (explicit fractal falloff — amplitude halves each
iteration) for asteroids, alien rocks, and craggy organic surfaces. That
is genuinely fractal displacement, not a single noise pass dressed up —
but it is NOT any of the three specific named fractal families
(Mandelbulb, Menger sponge, IFS/Iterated Function Systems), none of which
this renderer implements. If a literal Menger sponge or IFS look is
needed, "fractal"'s recursive-displacement approach won't produce it —
those specific structures would be a genuinely new generator, not a
relabeling of this one.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.8 — CELLULAR AUTOMATA  [ASPIRATIONAL]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Not implemented — no rule-based grid-growth generator exists (nothing
resembling Conway's-Life-style neighbor rules applied to a 3D grid). No
close equivalent either; this would be a genuinely new generator.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.9 — DIFFUSION-LIMITED AGGREGATION (DLA)  [ASPIRATIONAL]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Not implemented — no particle-accretion/random-walk-cluster generator
exists. The renderer's organic/craggy looks (62.7's "fractal", the
mountain/cliff/rock_formation generators' seeded jitter) come from
displacement math, not from a simulated accretion process, so they
aren't a hidden equivalent of DLA — they'd produce a different-looking
result if asked to stand in for it.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.10 — REACTION-DIFFUSION (STRUCTURAL GROWTH)  [ASPIRATIONAL]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Not implemented as a geometry-growth technique — no Turing-pattern solver
that shapes STRUCTURE (as opposed to surface color/texture — see 62.27
for the texturing version of this same gap) exists in this renderer.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.11 — BEZIER CURVES / B-SPLINES / NURBS  [ALREADY LIVE, partial]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Real quadratic and cubic Bezier curves are live (THREE.QuadraticBezierCurve3
/ THREE.CubicBezierCurve3), reachable directly via "type": "beziercurve"
(aliases "bezier_curve"/"spline") and used internally as the path input
for curve-following generators (tube/sweep/lathe-style builds). NURBS
specifically is not implemented — no rational-B-spline surface type
exists; Bezier curves are the closest real primitive this renderer has,
and cover the same practical need (a smooth author-defined path) for
every use case this renderer's generators actually call for.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.12 — SUBDIVISION SURFACES (CATMULL-CLARK / LOOP)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already documented at PART 54.1 — real Loop subdivision via
"subdivisionLevel": 1-3, smoothing and adding geometry. Catmull-Clark
specifically (quad-based) is not implemented — Loop (triangle-based) is
the correct algorithm for this renderer anyway, since every mesh here is
triangulated, not quad-based.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.13 — PROCEDURAL TERRAIN (NOISE HEIGHTMAPS + EROSION)  [ALREADY LIVE, partial]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Noise-driven terrain shapes are live and real: ground_plane/hill/valley/
island get seeded jitter via the existing auto-enhancement noise
operation (PART 46), and mountain/cliff/rock_formation bake their own
seeded jitter (LBL_RNG) directly into their generator functions — genuine
per-vertex procedural variation, not a flat primitive with a texture
painted on. What's NOT implemented is simulated EROSION (hydraulic/
thermal erosion passes that carve drainage channels and talus slopes into
a heightmap over simulated time) — the noise here is a single static
displacement, not an iterative physical simulation. No close equivalent
exists for the erosion half specifically.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.14 — VOXEL-BASED MODELING  [ASPIRATIONAL]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Not implemented as an actual volumetric-grid modeling technique (there is
no 3D voxel grid data structure or Minecraft-style world generator in
this renderer). "voxel" appears twice elsewhere in this codebase for
unrelated reasons, worth naming so neither is mistaken for this
technique: (1) "voxel" is a rendering STYLE name (meta.style: "voxel") —
a flat-shaded, hard-edged, no-tone-mapping look applied to ordinary
geometry, not a voxel data model; (2) genText3D (the "text3d" generator)
rasterizes a string to a grid and extrudes each inked cell into its own
small box — a real voxel-BLOCK look for typeface only, not a general
voxel modeling capability. Neither is a substitute for true voxel
world/object generation.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.15 — SHAPE GRAMMARS / CGA GRAMMAR  [ALREADY LIVE, partial]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Partially covered by 62.6: the L-system generator's "grammar" alias is a
real string-rewriting grammar engine, which is the same underlying
mechanism CGA/shape-grammar architecture tools use (rule-based, iterative
symbol expansion). What's not implemented is anything CITY/BUILDING-
specific (no procedural facade/massing/city-block generator that
interprets grammar rules as architectural operations) — the live grammar
engine is generic turtle-graphics, not pre-wired with architectural
semantics.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.16 — WAVE FUNCTION COLLAPSE  [ASPIRATIONAL]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Not implemented — no constraint-propagation tile/module solver exists.
Would be a genuinely new generator; nothing here approximates it.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.17 — POINT CLOUD RECONSTRUCTION (POISSON/DELAUNAY)  [ASPIRATIONAL]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Not implemented as real point-cloud-to-mesh reconstruction — this
renderer has no scan/point-cloud input path at all. "Delaunay" and
"Poisson" appear elsewhere in this codebase only as code-comment
shorthand naming a DIFFERENT prior technique audit's terrain/blob
generators (see PART 46's auto-enhancement notes on "Techniques 3/5" and
"Techniques 7/8") — those comments borrow the algorithm names loosely to
describe smoothing/resolution behavior on hill/valley/sdf_blob, they are
not literal point-cloud reconstruction and shouldn't be read as this
technique being live under another name.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.18 — PHYSICS-BASED SIMULATION (CLOTH/FLUID/SOFT-BODY/PARTICLE)  [ASPIRATIONAL, partial]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
No real physics solver exists (no cloth simulation, fluid solver, or
soft-body dynamics) — "cloth" in this codebase is only a material PRESET
(a color/roughness/metalness recipe that reads as fabric), not a
simulated drape. The one real partial exception: particle/trail-style VFX
generators (e.g. "smoke_trail") exist for animated effects, which is the
particle-system half of this technique in spirit, though it's an
authored animation curve rather than a physically simulated particle
solver.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.19 — NEURAL/AI-BASED GENERATION (NERF/DIFFUSION 3D)  [ASPIRATIONAL]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Not implemented and out of scope by design — this is a static, offline,
single-file renderer with no model-inference runtime. (Note this is a
different question from the AI ASSISTANT that authors a blueprint FROM a
reference image or prompt, which is what this whole document family
already does — that's a generation-time authoring aid, not a renderer
feature, and isn't what this technique name refers to.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.20 — PERLIN / SIMPLEX NOISE  [ALREADY LIVE, partial]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A real gradient/value-noise function (a custom deterministic hash-based
noise, not literally Ken Perlin's or Simplex's specific algorithms) drives
"texturePattern": "noise", the fractal displacement generator (62.7), and
several other procedural surfaces. It is functionally the same category
of technique (continuous pseudo-random surface variation from a
coordinate) and produces the same practical result, but isn't literally
Perlin or Simplex noise's specific lattice/simplex-grid math — worth
naming precisely rather than implying an exact-algorithm match that
isn't there.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.21 — WORLEY NOISE (CELLULAR/VORONOI)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live — a real Voronoi generator ("type": "voronoi") builds a
shattered/cracked-cell shell, and a jittered-Voronoi-cell-edge crackle/
fracture pattern is available as a texture effect (v7.1). This is the
real cellular-noise technique, not an approximation.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.22 — FRACTAL BROWNIAN MOTION (fBm)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live — the "fractal" generator (62.7) explicitly layers several
octaves of noise with amplitude halving each pass (fractal falloff),
which is the literal definition of fBm (summed octaves of noise at
decreasing amplitude/increasing frequency). Confirmed in code, not just
named similarly.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.23 — TURBULENCE FUNCTIONS  [ASPIRATIONAL]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Not implemented as a distinct effect — no absolute-value/distorted-noise
turbulence function exists separately from the plain noise/fBm already
covered (62.20/62.22). fBm's layered octaves give some of the same
"organic chaotic surface" visual goal, but turbulence's characteristic
sharp-ridged, absolute-value-folded look (classic for fire/smoke/marble
veining) isn't a real option here yet.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.24 — PROCEDURAL MARBLE/WOOD/STONE SHADERS  [ALREADY LIVE, partial]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Wood and stone are real, live texturePattern values ("wood"/"woodgrain"/
"wood_grain" and "stone", plus "brick"), each with its own procedural
generation function, not a shared generic pattern reused under different
names. MARBLE is the gap: "marble" only exists as a flat material-color
PRESET (a pale, low-roughness base color tuned to read as polished
marble) — there is no procedural marble-vein texturePattern (no turbulent
streak/vein pattern the way wood has grain rings or stone has speckle).
Anyone reaching for "marble" expecting the same procedural-vein treatment
wood/stone get will only see a flat tinted surface.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.25 — PROCEDURAL BUMP / NORMAL MAPPING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already documented at PART 54.8 — see there for the full field list;
restating only to close out this list without a silent gap.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.26 — PROCEDURAL DISPLACEMENT MAPPING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already documented at PART 54.2 — see there for the full field list.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.27 — REACTION-DIFFUSION TEXTURING (TURING PATTERNS)  [ASPIRATIONAL]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Not implemented — no Turing-pattern (activator/inhibitor) texture
simulation exists. "scales"/"speckle" texturePattern values produce a
designed repeating pattern, not a simulated reaction-diffusion result, so
they aren't a hidden equivalent — a genuine Turing-pattern generator
would look organically irregular in a way a designed repeat pattern
doesn't.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.28 — UV UNWRAPPING ALGORITHMS (LSCM/ABF++)  [ASPIRATIONAL]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Not implemented and not needed for how this renderer actually works:
every primitive gets automatic UVs (box projection, or one of
"uvProjection"'s triplanar/spherical/cylindrical/front/top/side choices —
PART 54.6) computed directly from the procedural geometry, with no seams
to solve for. Seam-minimizing unwrap ALGORITHMS (LSCM, ABF++) exist to
solve distortion on ARBITRARY hand-modeled meshes with no natural
axis-aligned structure — a problem this renderer's parametrically-defined
primitives don't have, so this is a genuine scope mismatch rather than a
missing feature.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.29 — PROJECTION-BASED TEXTURING (PLANAR/CYLINDRICAL/SPHERICAL/TRIPLANAR)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already documented at PART 54.6 ("uvProjection" field) and PART 54.17
(triplanar specifically) — see there for the full field list.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.30 — ENVIRONMENT / REFLECTION MAPPING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live — a real cubemap-based environment map exists and is
auto-enabled for the "pbr-realistic" style (documented at PART 59, part
of the v7.8 texturing/fidelity/lighting guarantees pass). Confirmed a
genuine reflection-mapping feature, not a flat reflective-looking color.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.31 — EXAMPLE-BASED TEXTURE SYNTHESIS  [ASPIRATIONAL]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Not implemented — no algorithm exists that grows a large texture from a
small authored sample patch. Out of scope for a parametric-pattern
renderer; every texturePattern here is a formula, not a sample-driven
synthesis.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.32 — PROCEDURAL WEATHERING / AGING  [ALREADY LIVE, partial]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Rust/dirt/grime-READING materials are real and live (e.g. "rust",
"rustedmetal" material presets — a genuine corroded-iron color/roughness/
metalness recipe, not just a brown tint). What's NOT implemented is
SIMULATED weathering — an operation that procedurally accumulates more
grime in crevices/edges/low-flow areas the way a real weathering-sim
would, rather than a uniform hand-tuned material applied everywhere on
the object. Cavity shading (62.33) is the closest live mechanism that
reacts to actual geometry (it darkens creases), but it's a shading effect
for readability, not a weathering simulation that alters the perceived
material itself.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.33 — AMBIENT OCCLUSION BAKING  [ALREADY LIVE, partial]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Two real, live mechanisms cover this technique's practical goal, though
neither is literally an offline ray-traced BAKE: (1) a real-time
screen-space ambient occlusion (SSAO) post-process pass, toggle-able in
the UI; (2) curvature-based fake ambient occlusion baked directly into
VERTEX COLORS at build time via "cavityShading": true (PART 33 B4) — this
one genuinely is computed once and stored per-vertex the way a bake is,
just from curvature rather than ray casting. True per-texel ray-traced AO
baking (sampling hemisphere rays into a lightmap) isn't implemented, but
between the two live options the same visual goal (darkened creases/
crevices) is already achieved.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.34 — NODE-BASED PROCEDURAL MATERIAL GRAPHS  [ASPIRATIONAL]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Not implemented and a deliberate scope mismatch, not a gap: this
renderer's material system is a flat set of named JSON fields
(material/texturePattern/textureScale/pbrTextureSet/etc.), which a
node-based graph (Substance Designer/Blender shader nodes) would compile
DOWN TO — building an actual node-graph authoring layer on top of a
single-file blueprint-JSON renderer would be redundant complexity, not a
missing capability; every real effect a node graph could produce here is
already reachable directly as a field.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.35 — NEURAL TEXTURE SYNTHESIS / DIFFUSION-BASED TEXTURING  [ASPIRATIONAL]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Not implemented and out of scope — no model-inference runtime exists in
this renderer (same reasoning as 62.19). Generating a texture from a
prompt/sample via GAN or diffusion model would need external inference
infrastructure this single-file offline renderer doesn't have and isn't
built to have.

================================================================================
PART 63 — v8.1 ADDITIONS: MULTI-PART MESH EXPORT FOR EXTERNAL RIG-TEST VIEWERS
================================================================================
Source: field report against an external GLB viewer's rig-test mode. This
renderer has no rig-test mode of its own — external viewers (glTF
inspectors, animation/rigging tools) load the exported .glb independently
and enumerate its mesh nodes to decide what's individually pickable/
riggable. If the whole model collapsed to one mesh/group during
generation (whole-model sdf_blob, whole-model CSG boolean union — see the
CAVEAT at the end of PART 61.2 — or any other full-body merge), that
enumeration finds exactly ONE part regardless of how much internal detail
the mesh actually has, and the external tool reports something like
"Using 1 mesh/group part(s) as rig-test targets" even though animation
clips (PART 18/19's tracks) still play back fine — clip playback only
needs named nodes to exist, not multiple meshes, so a single-mesh export
can look completely broken in a rig-test viewer while looking completely
fine in this renderer and in plain clip playback. This PART is the fix.

NOTE (v8.3, informational — no change needed in this path): a companion
investigation on the TS authoring path (Prompt_To_Ts.txt / Image_To_Ts.txt
PART 30.7) found that path's external-viewer "1 part" fallback was
actually gated on a MISSING glTF `skins` section, not on mesh count.
That does not apply here — this blueprint path's export
(buildSkinnedHierarchyForExport in the renderer) has always
auto-generated a real skinned rig for every model, unconditionally, so
the skeleton itself was never the blocker for JSON/JS-authored models.
The multi-part-mesh + isPickable/partName rules in this PART are the
complete fix for this authoring path.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
63.1 — RULE: KEEP BODY PARTS AS SEPARATE TOP-LEVEL MESH ENTRIES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Every distinct body part or component (head, torso, each arm — whether
authored as one "chain" per PART 40 or as separate upper/lower segments,
each hand, each leg, each foot, and any prop) MUST remain its own object
entry in the "objects" array, producing its own separate THREE.Mesh at
render time. Never let PART 61's Q1 (sdf_blob) or Q2 (CSG boolean union)
branch swallow the WHOLE model into one continuous result — those
techniques stay scoped to the smallest region that genuinely needs to
read as fused (PART 61.2's CAVEAT). A model is NOT required to hit any
specific triangle-level fusion; it IS required to still contain multiple
independent mesh entries after every operation/CSG/decimation pass runs.

Target for a character-scale model: roughly 10-15 separate mesh nodes in
the exported .glb — head, torso, upper-arm/forearm or whole-arm-chain ×2,
hand ×2, upper-leg/lower-leg or whole-leg-chain ×2, foot ×2, plus any
held props or accessories. A simple prop/vehicle/furniture model can
legitimately have fewer — the requirement is "genuinely more than one",
not a hard minimum that forces artificial part-splitting on a subject
that doesn't call for it.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
63.2 — RULE: MARK EVERY PART MESH FOR EXTERNAL PICKABILITY
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The renderer already copies each object's full literal onto its mesh —
`mesh.name = obj.id; mesh.userData = obj;` (buildScene()) — so any extra
fields you add directly to an object's top-level literal ride along into
`userData` automatically, with no renderer code change needed. Add these
two fields to EVERY visible part-level object (skip pure CSG tool objects
that carry no geometry of their own — same exception PART 21 already
makes for "baseColor"):

  {
    "id": "head", "type": "sphere", ...,
    "isPickable": true,
    "partName": "head"
  }

`"partName"` should equal the object's own `"id"` (or a clearer
human-readable label if the id is a generated/opaque string) — this is
exactly what an external glTF-based rig-test tool reads to enumerate and
label separately-pickable parts. These two fields are inert to THIS
renderer (nothing here currently reads `isPickable`/`partName`) — they
exist purely for external-viewer compatibility and never change how the
model looks or behaves in-app.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
63.3 — RULE: PARTS STAY REAL SIBLINGS/DESCENDANTS OF THE SCENE ROOT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Ordinary top-level "objects" entries already land as direct children of
`sceneGroup` (or of their `attachTo` parent, which is itself in that same
tree) — no extra step is needed for the flat-vs-nested requirement here,
UNLESS an object is deliberately routed through a whole-model merge
technique (63.1). Do not invent a wrapper/grouping object purely to
"organize" parts in the JSON — every part must be independently reachable
by GLTFExporter's node walk, which flat/attachTo placement already
guarantees.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
63.4 — VALIDATION ADDITIONS (append to PART 21's checklist)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  [ ] [v8.1] No single sdf_blob or CSG boolean-union chain spans the
      ENTIRE model — each is scoped to one limb/panel-cluster at most
      (PART 61.2 CAVEAT, PART 63.1). The final object set still contains
      multiple genuinely separate mesh entries, not one.
  [ ] [v8.1] Every visible part-level object carries "isPickable": true
      and "partName" (matching its "id") in its top-level literal (PART
      63.2) — skip only CSG tool-only objects with no geometry of their
      own.
  [ ] [v8.1] A character-scale model has roughly 10-15 separate exportable
      mesh parts (fewer is fine for a genuinely simple prop) — not 1
      (PART 63.1).

================================================================================
PART 64 — v8.2 ADDITIONS: CHARACTER / MASCOT SCULPT ENRICHMENT
================================================================================
Source: Character_Sculpt_Enrichment_v1.txt (compiled from object-sculpt
schema 2.1, character-v1.5 track). Compatible with LBL v8.0+ (JSON path).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PURPOSE & SCOPE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
This PART is an ADD-ON. It applies ONLY when the subject is a stylized
character, mascot, creature, humanoid, or anthropomorphic figure. It does
NOT replace any of the core LBL rules in PARTs 1-63 — it adds hierarchy
depth, attachment contracts, action-ready pivots, material frequency
bands, recessed-eye rules, build-pass discipline, and anti-shallow
quality gates so the AI stops emitting flat under-detailed characters.

APPLY automatically when:
  - Subject is character / mascot / creature / humanoid / anthropomorphic
  - Reference image shows a posed or standing figure with limbs, head, face
  - User says "stylized", "mascot", "electric mouse", "chibi", "cartoon
    character", "anthropomorphic", "person", "man", "woman", "kid", etc.
  - Pipeline routing or meta.category is "character"

DO NOT force this structure on pure props, vehicles, architecture, food,
or abstract shapes. A pet rock with eyes is NOT a character; an animal
with bilateral limbs, head, and facial features IS.

For the image-input fork (Image_To_Json.txt / Image_To_Js.txt) this PART
applies IN ADDITION to the image analysis protocol in PART 27. The image
analysis protocol determines the proportions and pose; this PART
determines the component hierarchy and attachment contract.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
64.1 — MANDATORY COMPONENT HIERARCHY (MACRO → MESO → MICRO)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Every character blueprint MUST contain at least these named parts (use
these exact ids or clearly scoped equivalents). Missing any macro/meso
part is a quality failure.

MACRO (required — these are the 6 root-level body masses):
  root            — overall character container / origin
  pelvis          — hip / lower torso mass
  abdomen         — mid torso
  chest           — upper torso / ribcage mass
  neck            — neck cylinder/capsule
  head            — head volume

MESO (required for full-body characters):
  hair            — hair volume (may be omitted only if the character is bald)
  clavicle-l / clavicle-r
  upper-arm-l / upper-arm-r
  forearm-l / forearm-r
  hand-l / hand-r
  thigh-l / thigh-r
  shin-l / shin-r
  foot-l / foot-r

MICRO (required for face readability):
  brow-l / brow-r
  ear-l / ear-r
  nose
  mouth
  eye-l / eye-r
  eye-cavity-l / eye-cavity-r   (recessed sockets — see 64.6, never flat decals)

Optional but recommended when visible:
  fingers (thumb/index/middle/ring/little × 2-3 phalanges per hand)
  tail, ears (animal character), horns, accessories, clothing layers,
  held props

RULE: Never collapse independently movable parts into one mesh. Each
limb segment is its own object with its own pivot. The PART 40 "chain"
generator is the preferred single-mesh implementation when the limb
needs per-segment continuity, but the limb must STILL ride on its own
named id ("arm_l", not folded into "chest").

EXAMPLE — minimal character hierarchy (flat format, skeleton only — no
materials yet, see 64.5 for that pass):

  {
    "id": "root", "type": "group", "children": [
      { "id": "pelvis", "type": "ellipsoid", "material": "cloth", "baseColor": "#1a3a8a" },
      { "id": "abdomen", "type": "ellipsoid", "attachTo": "pelvis", "placement": "above", "material": "cloth", "baseColor": "#1a3a8a" },
      { "id": "chest", "type": "ellipsoid", "attachTo": "abdomen", "placement": "above", "material": "cloth", "baseColor": "#cc2222" },
      { "id": "neck", "type": "cylinder", "attachTo": "chest", "placement": "above", "material": "skin", "baseColor": "#e8b87c", "scale": [0.18, 0.18, 0.18] },
      { "id": "head", "type": "sphere", "attachTo": "neck", "placement": "above", "material": "skin", "baseColor": "#e8b87c" },
      { "id": "hair", "type": "blob", "attachTo": "head", "placement": "above", "offset": [0, 0.18, -0.05], "material": "fur", "baseColor": "#3a2418" },
      { "id": "eye-cavity-l", "type": "sphere", "attachTo": "head", "placement": "front", "offset": [-0.10, 0.05, 0.18], "scale": [0.06, 0.06, 0.04], "material": "skin", "baseColor": "#7a4a2a" },
      { "id": "eye-l", "type": "sphere", "attachTo": "eye-cavity-l", "placement": "front", "offset": [0, 0, 0.01], "scale": [0.045, 0.045, 0.03], "material": "eye", "baseColor": "#1a1a1a" },
      // ... mirror eye-cavity-r / eye-r, ear-l / ear-r, nose, mouth, brow-l / brow-r ...
      { "id": "clavicle-l", "type": "sphere", "attachTo": "chest", "placement": "left", "material": "cloth", "baseColor": "#cc2222" },
      { "id": "arm_l", "type": "generated", "generator": "chain", "attachTo": "clavicle-l", "placement": "below", "params": { "baseRadius": 0.07, "segs": 6, "segments": [ { "length": 0.32, "tipRatio": 0.85, "aim": [0.06, -1, 0.05] }, { "length": 0.30, "tipRatio": 0.65, "aim": [0.08, -1, 0.20] } ] }, "material": "skin", "baseColor": "#e8b87c" },
      { "id": "hand_l", "type": "generated", "generator": "capsule", "attachTo": "arm_l", "placement": "below", "params": { "radius": 0.07, "length": 0.02 }, "material": "skin", "baseColor": "#e8b87c" }
      // ... mirror arm_r, hand_r, thigh_l, shin_l, foot_l, thigh_r, shin_r, foot_r ...
    ]
  }

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
64.2 — ATTACHMENT CONTRACT (required for every child limb/appendage)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Every child part that is not the root MUST declare an "attachment"
object (the LBL equivalent of "attachTo" with extra structural detail
about the seam). The renderer's current buildScene() ignores this field
(it only reads attachTo/placement) — it exists so the AI is forced to
think about the joint instead of guessing floating positions, and so
future renderer versions + the TS factory path can consume it.

PREFERRED SHAPE (all fields optional but strongly recommended):

  "attachment": {
    "parentSocket": "chest-clavicle-l",   // semantic socket name on parent
    "localStart":   [x, y, z],            // attach point in parent local space
    "localEnd":     [x, y, z],            // far end of this segment
    "contactType":  "rigid-weld" | "socket-joint" | "hinge-joint",
    "baseRadius":   number,               // thickness at root
    "endRadius":    number,               // thickness at tip
    "embedDepth":   0.02-0.05,            // how far it sinks into parent
    "gapTolerance": 0.01                  // max allowed air gap
  }

CONTACT TYPE GUIDANCE (semantic, not enforced by the renderer):
  rigid-weld    — torso segments, neck, hair, clothing panels
  socket-joint  — shoulder, hip
  hinge-joint   — elbow, knee, finger joints, jaw

Minimum overlap at seams: 0.02 world units (do not leave floating
parts). The existing PART 41 attachTo + placement system is what the
renderer actually USES to position the child — the attachment object
above is the contract that the rest of the model needs to satisfy.

EXAMPLE — arm_l with a full attachment contract (chain mesh, see 64.1
for the rest):

  {
    "id": "arm_l",
    "type": "generated",
    "generator": "chain",
    "params": { "baseRadius": 0.07, "segs": 6, "segments": [...] },
    "attachTo": "clavicle-l",
    "placement": "below",
    "attachment": {
      "parentSocket": "chest-clavicle-l",
      "localStart":   [0.18, -0.05, 0.00],
      "localEnd":     [0.22, -0.65, 0.05],
      "contactType":  "socket-joint",
      "baseRadius":   0.085,
      "endRadius":    0.055,
      "embedDepth":   0.04,
      "gapTolerance": 0.01
    },
    "material": "skin",
    "baseColor": "#e8b87c"
  }

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
64.3 — ACTION PROFILE & PIVOTS (action-ready static rig)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Every macro and meso part should carry an "actionProfile" object. The
renderer ignores this field today; it exists so a future rig/anim layer
(the TS factory path's userData.sculptRuntime already uses the same
shape — see Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 6 / PART 30) can drive the part
without re-deriving it.

PREFERRED SHAPE:

  "actionProfile": {
    "animationRole": "root" | "static" | "limb" | "digit" | "face",
    "pivot": {
      "mode": "center" | "root" | "custom",
      "localPosition": [0, 0, 0],
      "axis":           [0, 1, 0]
    },
    "transformChannels": {
      "translate":     true,
      "rotate":        true,
      "scale":         true,
      "bend":          false,
      "twist":         false,
      "detach":        false,
      "visibility":    true,
      "materialState": false
    },
    "collider": {
      "type":   "box" | "sphere" | "capsule",
      "offset": [0, 0, 0],
      "scale":  [1, 1, 1]
    },
    "destruction": {
      "breakable":     false,
      "fractureGroup": "<id>"
    }
  }

PIVOT PLACEMENT RULES (read these BEFORE writing pivot.localPosition):
  root        — at the model's logical center (usually 0,0,0)
  pelvis      — at the hip center
  abdomen     — at the lower torso center
  chest       — at the upper torso center
  neck        — at the base of the neck (jaw line)
  head        — at the head center (NOT at the chin)
  hair        — at the scalp root
  upper-arm   — at the shoulder (pivot at the join, not the segment middle)
  forearm     — at the elbow
  hand        — at the wrist
  thigh       — at the hip
  shin        — at the knee
  foot        — at the ankle
  eye         — at the eye center
  jaw/mouth   — at the lower-lip center

Place the pivot at the semantic joint, then build geometry outward from
localStart (root end) to localEnd (tip). The renderer's existing
"position" + "attachTo + placement" system does the world placement;
the "actionProfile.pivot" is the LOCAL-FRAME hint that future rig code
needs.

EXAMPLE — head with actionProfile:

  {
    "id": "head",
    "type": "sphere",
    "attachTo": "neck",
    "placement": "above",
    "material": "skin",
    "baseColor": "#e8b87c",
    "actionProfile": {
      "animationRole": "face",
      "pivot": {
        "mode": "center",
        "localPosition": [0, 0, 0],
        "axis":           [0, 1, 0]
      },
      "transformChannels": {
        "translate": true, "rotate": true, "scale": false,
        "bend": false, "twist": false, "detach": false,
        "visibility": true, "materialState": false
      },
      "collider": { "type": "sphere", "offset": [0, 0, 0], "scale": [1, 1, 1] },
      "destruction": { "breakable": false, "fractureGroup": "head" }
    }
  }

For chain-generator limbs the pivot is implicit in the segment aim
direction; you can still fill in actionProfile with the joint pivot
position (the elbow) for the forearm segment specifically.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
64.4 — MATERIALS (character palette + frequency bands)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Minimum material set for a clothed character:
  skin, hair, shirt (or torso cloth), pants, shoes, eye, lips

A creature/mascot may substitute "fur"/"feathers"/"scales" for skin +
hair, but keep the eye + a "mouth interior" material distinct.

Each material should declare (LBL already supports baseColor / roughness
/ metalness; the rest below are OPTIONAL extension fields the renderer
ignores, mirroring the surface-frequency-band concept from the
enrichment file):

  - albedo.dominant        — baseColor (already supported)
  - albedo.secondary       — optional "secondary baseColor" for hand-painted
                              trim panels (renderer-ignored; document intent)
  - roughness.base         — already supported (top-level roughness)
  - roughness.variation    — optional 0.0-0.1 jitter amount
  - surfaceFrequencyBands: — object with three optional sub-fields
      macro  — broad color/height breakup (sampled region span)
      meso   — pores, fabric weave, fur clumps
      micro  — highlight breakup under grazing light

DO NOT use a single flat color for the whole character. DO NOT paint
lighting into albedo. If you want a lit-vs-shadow region, use one of:
  - "toneVariant": "dark" | "light" (PART 26.7) on a separate sub-mesh
  - "colorVariation": 0.04 (PART 26.4) for a painted-shading look
  - A second material with darker color on a genuinely recessed panel

ROUGHNESS GUIDANCE (when you set it explicitly):
  skin   0.45 - 0.60
  cloth  0.70 - 0.90
  hair   0.55 - 0.75
  eye    0.15 - 0.25
  lips   0.30 - 0.50
  shoe   0.50 - 0.80

EXAMPLE — character palette (drop into the blueprint-level "palette" map):

  "palette": {
    "skin":  "#e8b87c",
    "skin_d": "#b5773a",   // darker recess / shadow panel
    "hair":  "#3a2418",
    "shirt": "#cc2222",
    "pants": "#1a3a8a",
    "shoe":  "#1a1a1a",
    "eye":   "#1a1a1a",
    "lip":   "#cc6644"
  }

  // in objects:
  { "id": "chest", "material": "$shirt", "baseColor": "$shirt" },
  { "id": "shirt_shadow", "attachTo": "chest", "placement": "below",
    "offset": [0, -0.02, 0.001], "material": "$shirt", "baseColor": "$skin_d",
    "toneVariant": "dark" }

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
64.5 — FACE LANDMARKS & RECESSED EYES (critical)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Eyes MUST read as recessed cavities, not flat stickers. PREFERRED
techniques, in order of preference (all are optional — the renderer
just renders whatever geometry you give it, so the choice is on the
author):

  1. Smaller sphere for the cavity, then a slightly smaller eye sphere
     inset into it (most reliable across every renderer version).
  2. Inset sphere with a slight negative Z offset (chibi style).
  3. booleanSubtract a small sphere from the head, then place a smaller
     eye sphere inside the resulting hole. (Use booleanSubtract from
     PART 10; remember CSG timing caveat from PART 38 — boolean the
     cavity BEFORE the head is attached to anything else.)

LANDMARK PLACEMENT (relative to head height, where head height = 1.0):
  eye line          — at   0.55 of head height from the chin
  eye spacing       — 0.30-0.35 of head width apart (center to center)
  nose base         — at   0.40 of head height from the chin
  mouth line        — at   0.25 of head height from the chin
  brow line         — at   0.68 of head height (above the eye line)
  ear line          — at   0.55 of head height (≈ on the eye line)

EXAMPLE — recessed eye cluster (chibi, head height 0.42):

  {
    "id": "head", "type": "sphere", "scale": [0.22, 0.21, 0.22], "material": "skin", "baseColor": "#e8b87c",
    "attachTo": "neck", "placement": "above",
    "actionProfile": { "animationRole": "face", "pivot": { "mode": "center", "localPosition": [0,0,0], "axis": [0,1,0] }, "transformChannels": {"translate":true,"rotate":true,"scale":false,"bend":false,"twist":false,"detach":false,"visibility":true,"materialState":false}, "collider": {"type":"sphere","offset":[0,0,0],"scale":[1,1,1]}, "destruction": {"breakable":false,"fractureGroup":"head"} }
  },
  { "id": "eye-cavity-l", "type": "sphere", "scale": [0.045, 0.045, 0.025],
    "attachTo": "head", "placement": "front", "offset": [-0.06, 0.04, 0.18],
    "material": "skin", "baseColor": "#7a4a2a" },
  { "id": "eye-l", "type": "sphere", "scale": [0.034, 0.034, 0.020],
    "attachTo": "eye-cavity-l", "placement": "front", "offset": [0, 0, 0.005],
    "material": "eye", "baseColor": "#1a1a1a" },
  { "id": "eye-cavity-r", "type": "sphere", "scale": [0.045, 0.045, 0.025],
    "attachTo": "head", "placement": "front", "offset": [ 0.06, 0.04, 0.18],
    "material": "skin", "baseColor": "#7a4a2a" },
  { "id": "eye-r", "type": "sphere", "scale": [0.034, 0.034, 0.020],
    "attachTo": "eye-cavity-r", "placement": "front", "offset": [0, 0, 0.005],
    "material": "eye", "baseColor": "#1a1a1a" }

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
64.6 — BUILD PASSES (ordered, do not skip)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
When generating a character, mentally (or actually) follow this order.
Emit the full blueprint only after the last required pass.

  1. blockout
     - root + pelvis + abdomen + chest + neck + head + major limb capsules
     - Correct head-unit proportions and overall silhouette
     - Acceptance: humanoid proportions readable with zero materials

  2. proportion-lock
     - Lock head/torso/limb ratios to measured reference (or style table)
     - Fix pose angles (elbow/knee bend, weight shift)

  3. feature-placement
     - hair, brows, ears, nose, mouth, eyes + cavities
     - fingers if visible
     - All attachment contracts filled (64.2)

  4. material-pass
     - Assign skin/hair/cloth/shoe/eye/lips materials
     - Sample real mid-tone colors from reference if image is present
     - Add roughness variation and basic frequency bands (64.4)

  5. lighting-pass
     - Optional in the blueprint, required in the post-render review
     - Note key / fill / rim intent (which way does the main light come
       from on the reference?)

  6. interaction-pass
     - Confirm every limb has a named pivot in actionProfile (64.3)
     - Confirm every held prop is attachTo:"hand_l" or "hand_r"
       (PART 43), not floating

  7. optimization-pass
     - Stay inside the performance budget (prefer < 80-120k tris for
       full characters)
     - Fold per-frame colorVariation / smoothing into "autoEnhance":true
       (PART 46) where possible

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
64.7 — QUALITY CONTRACT (anti-shallow rules — REJECT/REWRITE if any fire)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
REJECT the blueprint and rewrite if ANY of these are true:

  - Only a root + 1-2 body parts for a full character (PART 64.1 lists
    the minimum set; a 3-part character is NEVER acceptable)
  - Limbs are single capsules with no elbow/knee break (use the chain
    generator, PART 40, or two stacked capsule segments per limb)
  - Eyes are flat disks or decals with no cavity (64.5)
  - Hands are undifferentiated boxes with no fingers (when fingers
    are visible at the reference's zoom level)
  - Materials are one generic color with uniform roughness (64.4)
  - Child parts float with no attachment / embedDepth (64.2)
  - Adjacent parts have seam gap > 0.02 (PART 45 rule)
  - Face landmarks ignore measured eye-line / mouth-line (64.5)
  - Pose is forced to perfect T-pose when the reference shows a
    different pose (PART 42)

DEFINITION OF DONE (character):
  Silhouette matches the reference, primary proportions are correct,
  component hierarchy depth ≥ 3 levels (macro + meso + micro from 64.1),
  most recognizable local features are present, materials respond to
  light (not flat plastic).

Append these to PART 21's validation checklist (character-only items):

  [ ] [v8.2] Character has all 6 MACRO parts (root, pelvis, abdomen,
      chest, neck, head) — 64.1.
  [ ] [v8.2] Full-body character has all 14 MESO parts (hair, 2× clavicle,
      2× upper-arm, 2× forearm, 2× hand, 2× thigh, 2× shin, 2× foot) —
      omit only the parts genuinely not visible — 64.1.
  [ ] [v8.2] Face has eye-cavity-l, eye-l, eye-cavity-r, eye-r as 4
      separate objects (NOT one merged "eyes" object) — 64.5.
  [ ] [v8.2] Every child limb/appendage has either an "attachment"
      object (64.2) OR a complete "attachTo" + "placement" pair with
      "overlap": 0.02+ (PART 26.5).
  [ ] [v8.2] Every macro and meso part has an "actionProfile" object
      with at least animationRole + pivot (64.3) — even if the renderer
      ignores it today.
  [ ] [v8.2] Minimum material set is present (skin, hair, torso cloth,
      pants/legs, shoes, eye) — 64.4. A mascot/creature may substitute
      fur/feathers/scales for skin + hair but still needs eye +
      mouth-interior distinct.
  [ ] [v8.2] Eye material is set to "eye" (or a metalness < 0.1,
      roughness < 0.3 MeshStandardMaterial equivalent) — not a generic
      "default" preset.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
64.8 — OUTPUT RULES (still pure LBL)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  - Prefer the flat "objects" array (PART 1, FORMAT A). The nested
    FORMAT B also works — child ids are scoped by the converter
    automatically (PART 19).
  - Use string material names that exist in LBL presets (PART 3), or
    omit "material" and set baseColor + roughness directly.
  - The new fields (attachment, actionProfile, surfaceFrequencyBands,
    collider, destruction, transformChannels, etc.) are EXTRA keys on
    the object. The current renderer ignores unknown keys safely. Future
    renderer versions and the TS factory path consume them.
  - Keep all existing LBL constraints (no trailing commas, valid
    primitives, sdf_blob must have "balls", extrude/lathe must have
    "profile", booleanX "with" must reference an existing id, etc.).
  - When an image is supplied, MEASURE proportions and SAMPLE colors
    from the image before writing any numbers (see Image_To_Json.txt
    PART 27 for the full protocol).
  - Every limb segment is its own object with its own id and pivot —
    the chain generator (PART 40) is the preferred single-mesh
    implementation but the limb id still exists.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
64.9 — QUICK CHARACTER SCAFFOLD (copy-paste starting point)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Use this mental skeleton, then fill real scales/positions from the
reference (or from the style table in PART 20 for a prompt-only build):

  root (type: group)
  ├── pelvis
  │   ├── thigh-l → shin-l → foot-l
  │   └── thigh-r → shin-r → foot-r
  ├── abdomen
  │   └── chest
  │       ├── neck → head
  │       │         ├── hair
  │       │         ├── brow-l / brow-r
  │       │         ├── ear-l / ear-r
  │       │         ├── nose
  │       │         ├── mouth
  │       │         ├── eye-cavity-l → eye-l
  │       │         └── eye-cavity-r → eye-r
  │       ├── clavicle-l → upper-arm-l → forearm-l → hand-l (+ fingers)
  │       └── clavicle-r → upper-arm-r → forearm-r → hand-r (+ fingers)

For a chibi mascot, fold thighs and shins into one stubby cylinder per
leg, omit fingers, exaggerate the head, and keep all 6 MACRO parts
(64.1 — never fold chest+abdomen+pelvis into one "body" even on a chibi).

For a quadruped (dog, cat, horse, dragon), replace thigh-r/l + shin-r/l
with front-leg-r/l + back-leg-r/l, add a tail attached to pelvis, and
keep the same eye/brow/ear/nose/mouth micro face structure (move ears
forward to the eye line per 64.5).

For a creature with no arms (slime, octopus with a humanoid face), drop
clavicle/upper-arm/forearm/hand per side but keep the rest of the
hierarchy intact.

================================================================================
END OF PART 64 — CHARACTER / MASCOT SCULPT ENRICHMENT
Output remains valid LBL JSON. Unknown extra fields are kept as
optional and ignored by the current renderer; they exist so future
renderer versions and the TS factory path can consume them, and so the
AI is forced to author a proper component hierarchy instead of a flat
1-2-part character. The rules in this PART apply ONLY when the subject
is a character, mascot, creature, or humanoid.
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 65 — v8.1 ADDITIONS: STRUCTURAL ERROR CORRECTIONS
(JSON / JS blueprint → Three.js output pipeline)
================================================================================
Source: SYSTEM_DIAGNOSTIC_AND_ARCHITECTURAL_ERROR_REPORT_THREEJS.txt
(captured errors 1-10 from prompt-to-ts, img2threejs, WebGL sandboxes,
and glTF 2.0 exporter pipelines). For the JSON/JS blueprint path, only
the renderer-level failures (errors 5, 6, 7, 8) apply — the AI does not
emit raw Three.js code here, so the TS-specific errors (1, 2, 3, 4, 9,
10) are already structurally impossible. The renderer's buildScene()
honors the rules below, and the AI is required to author blueprints that
do not force the renderer to violate them.

This PART formalizes the 4 JSON/JS-relevant fixes as hard rules so the
AI never re-emits any of the structural failures below. Where existing
PARTs already cover a concept in depth, this PART is the single-line
"do not violate" reminder with the canonical anti-pattern vs compliant-
pattern example; cross-reference the parent PART for full treatment.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
65.1 — ANTI-PATTERN CATALOG (JSON/JS-relevant subset)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[5] PROCEDURAL CANVAS DECAL CONTEXT LIFECYCLE & MISSING FLAGS
    The blueprint path does not let the AI write canvas code directly,
    but two blueprint fields can still trigger the underlying error:
    `texturePattern` (procedural) and `textureImage` (data URL / file).
    The renderer wraps both into a CanvasTexture / Texture on the AI's
    behalf. Hard rules the AI must observe so the renderer never trips
    the error:

    ❌ // "textureImageColorSpace" missing or wrong casing:
       { "id":"decal", "type":"plane",
         "textureImage": "logo.png" }                  // no color space
       { "id":"decal", "type":"plane",
         "textureImage": "logo.png",
         "textureImageColorSpace": "RGB" }             // WRONG value

    ✅ { "id":"decal", "type":"plane",
         "material":"glossy",
         "textureImage": "textures/logo.png",
         "textureImageRepeat":   [1, 1],
         "textureImageOffset":   [0, 0],
         "textureImageRotation": 0,
         "textureImageColorSpace": "srgb" }            // required for color
       // or for normal/roughness/metalness/AO maps:
       { "id":"panel", "type":"cube", "material":"brushed-metal",
         "textureImage": "textures/panel-normal.png",
         "textureImageColorSpace": "no-color" }        // required for data

    RULE: every `textureImage` field MUST be paired with an explicit
    `textureImageColorSpace` — `"srgb"` for color/albedo textures,
    `"no-color"` for normal/roughness/metalness/AO/data maps. The
    renderer uses this to set the correct THREE colorSpace flag; a
    missing field is auto-defaulted to "srgb" but the AI MUST state it
    explicitly so the blueprint is self-documenting and survives
    external GLB round-trips. (See PART 8 for the full texture spec.)
    Reinforces PART 8 (procedural patterns) and the
    `textureImageColorSpace` companion fields described around PART 3.

[6] GLTF 2.0 / GLTFEXPORTER SERIALIZATION INCOMPATIBILITIES
    The renderer's buildScene() copies each object's `userData`
    sub-object verbatim onto the resulting mesh's `userData`. The AI's
    job is to keep `userData` JSON-serializable so the export-to-GLB
    path never throws "Converting circular structure to JSON".

    ❌ // functions and circular refs break the GLB exporter
       { "id":"leg", "type":"capsule", "material":"metal",
         "userData": {
           "onPick":  "() => focusCamera()",     // ❌ FUNCTION
           "sibling": "<<ref:other-leg>>"        // ❌ non-JSON ref
         } }

    ✅ { "id":"leg-l", "type":"capsule", "material":"metal",
         "userData": {
           "partName":    "leg-left",              // pure scalar
           "isPickable":  true,                    // used by inspector
           "mirrorOf":    "leg-r",                 // sibling NAME, not ref
           "jointParent": "thigh-l"                // string id only
         } }
       { "id":"leg-r", "type":"capsule", "material":"metal",
         "userData": {
           "partName":   "leg-right",
           "isPickable": true,
           "mirrorOf":   "leg-l",
           "jointParent":"thigh-r"
         } }

    RULE: `userData` is STRICT JSON (plain strings, numbers, booleans,
    arrays, plain objects). No functions. No EventListeners. No
    cross-references to other object ids expressed as anything but
    a plain string — store the sibling's id, not a JS reference.
    Companion rules in PART 63 (`isPickable`, `partName`) and
    PART 30.7 (TS path) reinforce this on the factory path.

[7] DEPRECATED THREE.JS LEGACY APIs (r150+ COMPATIBILITY)
    The renderer targets Three.js r150+. Blueprint-side, this surfaces
    as: (a) the `material` field must reference a preset that compiles
    to MeshStandardMaterial / MeshPhysicalMaterial — NOT Lambert /
    Phong / Basic; and (b) `textureImageColorSpace: "srgb"` must use
    the modern SRGBColorSpace token, not the legacy sRGBEncoding string.

    ❌ // legacy material names or legacy color-space tokens
       { "id":"panel", "material":"lambert" }               // REMOVED
       { "id":"decal", "textureImageColorSpace":
                    "sRGBEncoding" }                         // DEPRECATED

    ✅ { "id":"panel", "material":"brushed-metal" }         // PBR preset
       { "id":"decal", "textureImageColorSpace": "srgb" }   // modern token
       // Custom PBR via direct numeric material:
       { "id":"panel", "material": {
           "baseColor":  "#c8763a",
           "metalness":  0.0,
           "roughness":  0.7
         } }

    RULE: prefer the named preset list in PART 3 (`mat` values resolve
    to MeshStandardMaterial in the renderer). For custom PBR, use the
    `material: { baseColor, metalness, roughness, ... }` object form
    with numeric PBR fields — never emit `material: "lambert"`,
    `material: "phong"`, or `material: "basic"`. Color-space tokens
    are exactly `"srgb"` or `"no-color"` — never the legacy
    `sRGBEncoding` / `LinearEncoding` strings. Reinforces PART 3 and
    PART 7.

[8] MATERIAL/GEOMETRY SHARED MUTATION & MEMORY LEAKAGE
    Blueprint-side, this surfaces when the AI references a single
    material name across many objects that should be visually distinct,
    or re-uses one `material: { ... }` object literal across multiple
    parts. The renderer's preset system already de-duplicates material
    names (so 200 objects sharing `"material":"fur"` share one
    MeshStandardMaterial instance — good), but object-literal
    materials are instantiated per-object, and intentional visual
    variation between two surfaces requires DISTINCT material names
    or DISTINCT baseColor/metalness/roughness numeric values.

    ❌ // same custom material on visually-different panels
       { "id":"panel-warm",  "material": { "baseColor":"#cc6633" } }
       { "id":"panel-cool",  "material": { "baseColor":"#cc6633" } }  // dup
       { "id":"panel-warm2", "material": { "baseColor":"#cc6633" } }  // dup

    ✅ // distinct material per visual role
       { "id":"panel-warm",  "material":"copper",  "baseColor":"#cc6633" }
       { "id":"panel-cool",  "material":"steel",   "baseColor":"#7a8a99" }
       { "id":"panel-trim",  "material":"rubber",  "baseColor":"#1a1a1a" }
       // or, when no preset fits, use distinct numeric values:
       { "id":"panel-a", "material": { "baseColor":"#cc6633",
                                       "metalness":0.0, "roughness":0.7 } }
       { "id":"panel-b", "material": { "baseColor":"#cc6633",
                                       "metalness":0.0, "roughness":0.4 } }

    RULE: two visually-different surfaces = two distinct material
    references (different preset names, OR identical preset name with
    different `baseColor` / `metalness` / `roughness` numeric values,
    OR one preset + one object-literal). Never reuse the SAME
    `material: { ... }` object-literal across multiple parts expecting
    different future PBR edits. Reinforces PART 7's "one preset per
    visual role" guidance and PART 15's "don't repeat the same flat
    material everywhere" anti-pattern.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
65.2 — JSON/JS BLUEPRINT REFERENCE CHECKLIST (silent pre-output pass)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Before emitting any blueprint, mentally walk this list. Any unchecked
item is a renderer-side failure that will surface in the inspector or
the external GLB export.

  [ ] 1. Every `textureImage` is paired with an explicit
        `textureImageColorSpace` of exactly `"srgb"` or `"no-color"`.
  [ ] 2. Every `userData` is JSON-serializable — no functions, no
        EventListeners, no live JS references; sibling references are
        string ids only.
  [ ] 3. Every multi-part body uses `userData.isPickable: true` +
        `userData.partName: "<semantic-name>"` (PART 63.1 contract)
        so external GLB rig-test viewers can address each part.
  [ ] 4. No `material: "lambert" | "phong" | "basic"` — all materials
        are PBR presets or numeric PBR object-literal forms.
  [ ] 5. `textureImageColorSpace` never uses the legacy
        `"sRGBEncoding"` / `"LinearEncoding"` strings — exactly
        `"srgb"` or `"no-color"`.
  [ ] 6. Visually-distinct surfaces use distinct material references
        (different preset names OR distinct numeric PBR values) —
        not the same `material: { ... }` literal copy-pasted across
        parts.
  [ ] 7. Materials that need transparency (`opacity < 1`) explicitly
        set `"transparent": true` so the renderer enables alpha
        blending (PART 15 pitfall).
  [ ] 8. Materials that need double-sided rendering explicitly set
        `"side": "double"` (or `"back"`) — the default is single-sided.
  [ ] 9. Procedural `texturePattern` and image `textureImage` are
        never combined on the same part (PART 8 override rule).
  [ ] 10. Each rig-relevant articulation (where present) names its
         parent joint and child segment in `userData.jointParent` /
         `userData.jointChild` as plain strings (mirrors PART 30.7's
         TS-path discipline on the JSON/JS side).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
65.3 — WHY ONLY 4 OF 10 ERRORS APPLY HERE (cross-reference table)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
The full diagnostic catalog has 10 errors. For the JSON/JS blueprint
path, the AI does not emit raw Three.js code, so the TS-only errors
are already structurally impossible to produce from a valid blueprint:

  [1] relative local imports     → N/A (no imports in blueprint)
  [2] KeyframeTrack binding      → N/A (no keyframes in blueprint;
                                     animation is a TS-path concern)
  [3] un-normalized quaternions  → N/A (no quaternion math in blueprint)
  [4] bone pivot misalignment    → N/A (no THREE.Bone in blueprint;
                                     rig is a TS-path concern)
  [9] TypeScript type contracts  → N/A (no TS types in blueprint)
  [10] factory entrypoint name   → N/A (no factory function in
                                     blueprint; buildScene() is the
                                     fixed entry point)

The remaining 4 (5, 6, 7, 8) ARE achievable from a valid blueprint and
are the ones the catalog above locks down. The TS-path equivalent is
in PART 32 (Prompt_To_Ts.txt / Image_To_Ts.txt) and addresses the full
10.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
65.4 — INTERSECTION WITH EXISTING PARTS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
This PART does NOT replace any existing rule — it consolidates and
references them so a new AI session that hasn't fully absorbed the
parent PARTs still cannot emit broken blueprints. The 4 errors above
are addressed in depth at the following existing locations;
cross-reference is in [brackets]:

  [5] textureImageColorSpace + procedural pattern lifecycle
        → PART 8 (procedural canvas), PART 3 (textureImage companion
          fields around line 572-594), PART 15 (common mistakes)
  [6] userData hygiene + GLB export
        → PART 1.5, PART 63.1 (isPickable/partName), PART 30.7 (TS-side
          mirror)
  [7] r150+ API compatibility (material + color-space tokens)
        → PART 3 (material preset list), PART 7 (PBR recipes), PART 15
  [8] material instance split
        → PART 7, PART 15, PART 30 (TS-side mirror on the factory
          path)

PART 65 is the JSON/JS-path safety net. If you remember ONLY one thing
from this PART, remember the 4-line catalog in 65.1 — the rest is depth.

Output remains a valid LBL JSON/JS blueprint. The 4 anti-pattern

every emission. The cross-reference table in 65.3 documents why the
