================================================================================
PART 34 — v8.3 ADDITIONS: PRE-BUNDLED RIG HELPERS (OPT-IN)
(Runtime contract for JSON/JS blueprints that need a true SkinnedMesh)
================================================================================
Source: weights.ts + skeleton.ts attached by the maintainer. This PART
documents the 3 data shapes, the 5 public functions the renderer uses
internally, the runtime contract a blueprint can opt into via
userData, and the same WHEN / WHEN-NOT decision table that the TS-path
PART 34 codifies. The JSON/JS blueprint path does not author code, so
the AI cannot import 'three-rig-helpers' directly — the renderer is
the only consumer. What the AI CAN do is declare the rig data on
userData (mirroring the TS contract) and let the renderer compile it
at load time.

This is the ONLY sanctioned exception to PART 4.1 / 4.2's single-file
rule, and only applies when the model genuinely needs a
THREE.SkinnedMesh with vertex skin weights. For every other case, the
pivot-hierarchy approach in PART 30 is still the default and you do
not touch this PART.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
34.1 — THE ESCAPE HATCH (the one and only, framed for JSON/JS)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 4.1 / 4.2 (the JSON/JS equivalents on the master spec, see PART
1's "no relative imports" rule) say: every blueprint is a single
self-contained data object. The only allowed behavior is data
declaration — no code emission, no module imports, no fetch.

v8.3 sanctions a single, narrow exception for the JSON/JS path:
the renderer will read `userData.rigGraph` (a RigGraph-shaped object
on the root) and `userData.skeletonSource === 'three-rig-helpers'`
(marker), and at load time it will use the pre-bundled helpers
declared at ./public/rig/three-rig-helpers.js (3.9 KB, ES2020,
minified, served by the page's own static directory, ZERO external
deps beyond the page's own 'three' build) to compile a real
THREE.Skeleton for the model.

The AI does not import the helpers — the renderer does. The AI's only
job is to declare the rig data correctly. The exception, restated
from the AI's perspective on the JSON/JS path, is: you may add two
new fields to the root userData, `rigGraph` and `skeletonSource`,
and the renderer will take it from there.

Why this exception exists: PART 30's auto Bone-promotion + single-
bone-per-part rigid skin at GLB export time is sufficient for the 99%
case where each mesh deforms as a rigid unit bound to a single bone.
The remaining 1% — smooth-skinned characters, faces with proper
blendshape-style vertex weighting, rig graphs with > 255 bones that
need a real multi-bone-per-vertex bind — needs the renderer to
build a real THREE.SkinnedMesh with `skinIndex` + `skinWeight`
attributes. The pre-bundled helpers exist so the renderer can do
that work without the AI emitting any code.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
34.2 — THE 5 PUBLIC FUNCTIONS (renderer-internal, for reference)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The renderer uses these 5 functions internally. The AI does not call
them; it just declares the data they consume. The functions are listed
here so the spec is self-contained — a future maintainer reading
PART 34 alone (without the bundle) understands the full contract.

  // 1. Compile a RigGraph into a THREE.Skeleton + bone map.
  //    Validates exactly one root joint, parent-before-child ordering,
  //    and updates matrixWorld BEFORE calculateInverses() (otherwise
  //    every inverse is identity and vertices stretch from the origin
  //    when SkinnedMesh applies the bind pose).
  function buildSkeleton(graph: RigGraph): SkeletonBuildResult;

  // 2. Build smooth skin weights (4-influence per vertex, nearest joints
  //    by squared-distance falloff). Returns a Uint16BufferAttribute for
  //    skinIndex and a Float32BufferAttribute for skinWeight, plus
  //    maxWeightError (the worst |1 - sum| across the geometry).
  function buildSemanticWeights(
    geometry: THREE.BufferGeometry,
    skeleton: SkeletonBuildResult,
    joints: RigJoint[],
    maxInfluences?: number,        // default 4
  ): WeightBuildResult;

  // 3. Build COMPACT skin weights (uint8 indices + uint8 normalized
  //    weights, 1 influence per vertex, weight = 255). For reconstructed
  //    multipart surfaces where smoothing across an unknown source seam
  //    would be less truthful than a rigid semantic region. Throws if
  //    the skeleton has > 255 bones (uint8 index cap).
  function buildRigidSemanticWeights(
    geometry: THREE.BufferGeometry,
    skeleton: SkeletonBuildResult,
    resolveJoint: (x: number, y: number, z: number, vertex: number) => string,
  ): CompactWeightBuildResult;

  // 4. Validate a RigGraph BEFORE compiling it. Returns string[] of
  //    human-readable errors (empty array = clean). Checks for:
  //    duplicate joint ids, self-parenting, NaN / Infinity rest
  //    positions, missing parent joints, and root count != 1. The
  //    renderer's Rig panel calls this on every open.
  function validateRigGraph(graph: RigGraph): string[];

  // 5. Validate a geometry's skinIndex + skinWeight attributes. Returns
  //    string[] of human-readable errors. Checks for: skinWeight vec4
  //    presence, skinIndex vec4 presence, and per-vertex |sum - 1|
  //    within the supplied tolerance (default 0.001). The renderer
  //    calls this on every model load that declares userData.rigGraph.
  function validateWeights(geometry: THREE.BufferGeometry, tolerance?: number): string[];

  // Bonus (not part of the "5 public" but documented for completeness):
  //   resetSkeleton(result):       re-pose a built skeleton to bind
  //   jointWorldPosition(result, id): query a joint's world position
  //   asVector([x,y,z]):           convert a Vec3 literal to THREE.Vector3

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
34.3 — THE 3 DATA SHAPES (re-declared for spec self-containment)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The bundle ships the source-of-truth types INLINE — no external type
imports. The spec must be self-contained, so the canonical shapes are
re-declared here verbatim. On the JSON/JS path, the AI declares these
in the blueprint's userData; on the TS path, the AI uses them in code.

  // [number, number, number] — XYZ literal. THREE.Vector3-compatible.
  type Vec3 = [number, number, number];

  // [x, y, z, w] — quaternion in XYZW order. The bundle's
  // bone.quaternion.set(...restRotation) consumes this order directly.
  type Quat4 = [number, number, number, number];

  // One joint in the rig graph. parentId is OMITTED for the root.
  // restPosition is WORLD-space (the build pass subtracts the parent's
  // rest position to derive a local offset). restRotation is WORLD-
  // space quaternion in XYZW order. Bone NAMES are the same .name
  // strings, so they must follow PART 32 error [2]'s alphanumeric-
  // with-underscores rule and (for any humanoid / biped / mech) reuse
  // STANDARD_BONES from PART 33.1[10] (or the JSON/JS equivalent
  // bone-name catalog).
  interface RigJoint {
    id: string;                    // unique, alphanumeric + underscore
    parentId?: string;             // OMITTED on the single root
    restPosition: Vec3;            // [x, y, z] world-space
    restRotation: Quat4;           // [x, y, z, w] world-space, unit length
  }

  // The full rig graph. joints[] does NOT need to be topologically
  // sorted — buildSkeleton re-orders via a pending-queue walk that
  // defers a joint until its parent has been added. But sorting it
  // parent-before-child is fine and helps the AI's own debugging.
  interface RigGraph {
    joints: RigJoint[];
  }

The bundle ALSO exports two return-type shapes (free, not part of the
"3 data shapes" but useful for editor / .d.ts consumers):

  interface SkeletonBuildResult {
    root: THREE.Bone;
    bones: Map<string, THREE.Bone>;
    skeleton: THREE.Skeleton;
    restWorldPositions: Map<string, THREE.Vector3>;
  }
  interface WeightBuildResult {
    indices: THREE.Uint16BufferAttribute;
    weights: THREE.Float32BufferAttribute;
    maxWeightError: number;
  }
  interface CompactWeightBuildResult {
    indices: THREE.Uint8BufferAttribute;
    weights: THREE.Uint8BufferAttribute;
  }

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
34.4 — WHEN TO USE RIG HELPERS VS. THE PLAIN PIVOT HIERARCHY (PART 30)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 30 (pivot hierarchy + auto-promotion + single-bone-per-part rigid
skin at export) is still the DEFAULT. PART 34 is the explicit escape
hatch for the cases PART 30 cannot cover. Use the decision table below:

  ┌────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │ If the model needs...          │ Use PART 30          │ Use PART 34       │
  │                                │ (pivot hierarchy,    │ (three-rig-helpers│
  │                                │  default)            │  escape hatch)    │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Mesh deforms with bones        │ NO (mesh is rigidly │ YES               │
  │ (vertex skin weights)          │ bound, 1 bone/part)  │                   │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Joint rotates but mesh doesn't │ YES (default)        │ NO (overkill)     │
  │ deform (low-poly, static pose) │                      │                   │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Smooth skin (4-influence per   │ NO                   │ YES               │
  │ vertex, distance falloff)      │                      │ (buildSemantic    │
  │                                │                      │  Weights)         │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Multi-bone-per-vertex (> 1     │ NO                   │ YES               │
  │ influence per vertex)          │                      │                   │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ > 255 bones in the rig graph   │ NO                   │ YES (but only     │
  │                                │                      │ with the float    │
  │                                │                      │ path —            │
  │                                │                      │ buildRigid-       │
  │                                │                      │ SemanticWeights   │
  │                                │                      │ caps at 255)      │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Simple human / biped / mech    │ YES (use STANDARD_   │ NO                │
  │ (< 64 bones, rigid per-part)   │ BONES, no helpers)   │                   │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Style-locked cel-toon /        │ YES                  │ NO                │
  │ retro-psx / hologram mode      │                      │                   │
  │ (PART 66.1[4])                 │                      │                   │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ External GLB-viewer rig-test   │ YES (PART 30.7's     │ YES (already      │
  │ mode wants a `skins` section   │ auto-promotion       │  has a real       │
  │                                │  covers it)          │  SkinnedMesh —    │
  │                                │                      │  export is a      │
  │                                │                      │  no-op identity   │
  │                                │                      │  in this case)    │
  └────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴───────────────────┘

Quick rule of thumb: if the model has fewer than 64 bones, no vertex
skin weights, and each part is a rigid mesh that follows its parent
Group's transform, you DO NOT need this PART — use PART 30 and forget
34 exists. Reach for 34 only when the table above says YES.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
34.5 — WORKED EXAMPLE: BLUEPRINT THAT DECLARES A RIG GRAPH
        (data-only — the renderer does the rest)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The end-to-end pattern for a JSON/JS blueprint that wants the renderer
to compile a real SkinnedMesh. The AI declares the data; the renderer
imports the helpers, validates, builds, attaches skin weights, and
exposes a Rig button — all client-side, no code emitted by the AI.

  // Inside the blueprint root's userData (JSON/JS path):
  {
    "userData": {
      "rigGraph": {
        "joints": [
          { "id": "Bone_Pelvis",      "restPosition": [0, 1.0, 0],  "restRotation": [0, 0, 0, 1] },
          { "id": "Bone_Spine",       "parentId": "Bone_Pelvis", "restPosition": [0, 1.25, 0], "restRotation": [0, 0, 0, 1] },
          { "id": "Bone_Chest",       "parentId": "Bone_Spine",  "restPosition": [0, 1.55, 0], "restRotation": [0, 0, 0, 1] },
          { "id": "Bone_Head",        "parentId": "Bone_Chest",  "restPosition": [0, 1.85, 0], "restRotation": [0, 0, 0, 1] },
          { "id": "Bone_Shoulder_L",  "parentId": "Bone_Chest",  "restPosition": [ 0.25, 1.65, 0], "restRotation": [0, 0, 0, 1] },
          { "id": "Bone_Arm_L",       "parentId": "Bone_Shoulder_L", "restPosition": [ 0.50, 1.55, 0], "restRotation": [0, 0, 0, 1] },
          { "id": "Bone_Shoulder_R",  "parentId": "Bone_Chest",  "restPosition": [-0.25, 1.65, 0], "restRotation": [0, 0, 0, 1] },
          { "id": "Bone_Arm_R",       "parentId": "Bone_Shoulder_R", "restPosition": [-0.50, 1.55, 0], "restRotation": [0, 0, 0, 1] }
        ]
      },
      "skeletonSource": "three-rig-helpers",
      "rigOptions": {
        "skin": "semantic",          // "semantic" (4-influence) or "rigid" (uint8, ≤ 255)
        "maxInfluences": 4           // optional, default 4
      }
    }
  }

What the renderer does on load (no AI action required):

  1. Sees `root.userData.rigGraph` + `skeletonSource === 'three-rig-helpers'`.
  2. Calls `validateRigGraph(rigGraph)`. If errors → spec chip warning,
     Rig button disabled, no SkinnedMesh built.
  3. Calls `buildSkeleton(rigGraph)` → THREE.Skeleton + bone map.
  4. For each mesh part (every part where `userData.rigOptions?.target`
     is unset or matches that part), the renderer binds the closest
     `buildSemanticWeights(...)` output (4-influence, distance falloff)
     or `buildRigidSemanticWeights(...)` (uint8, 1-influence) — driven
     by `rigOptions.skin`.
  5. Calls `validateWeights(geometry)` on each bound mesh. If errors →
     spec chip warning, the offending mesh falls back to the PART 30
     rigid-skin path.
  6. Enables the Rig button in the topbar; the panel lists every joint
     + re-validates on every open.

The AI's only job is the data above. No code, no imports, no fetch.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
34.6 — PRE-EMISSION CHECKLIST (the 6 items — MANDATORY for any
        blueprint that declares userData.rigGraph; a no-op for every
        other blueprint)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Append these to PART 65.4's structural reference checklist. Any
unchecked item is a load-time or first-render failure that the
renderer will surface as a red spec chip.

  [ ] 1. [PART 33.5.1] The blueprint's root userData contains BOTH
         `rigGraph` (a RigGraph-shaped object) AND
         `skeletonSource === 'three-rig-helpers'`. The renderer
         treats the absence of either as "no rig helpers opted in"
         and skips PART 34 entirely.
  [ ] 2. [PART 33.5.3] The rig graph has been authored so that
         `validateRigGraph()` (run by the renderer client-side) will
         return an empty string[]. The AI cannot run the validator
         itself (no JS execution in the blueprint), but the spec
         contract is "if you author it, the renderer will validate
         it"; the AI should mentally walk the 5 checks (duplicate ids,
         self-parenting, finite rest positions, missing parents, root
         count == 1) before emitting.
  [ ] 3. [PART 33.5.3] The graph declares exactly one root joint (the
         one with parentId omitted).
  [ ] 4. [PART 32 error [2]] Every joint's `id` is alphanumeric +
         underscore, non-empty, and (for any humanoid / biped / mech)
         matches the STANDARD_BONES catalog from PART 33.1[10]. No
         hard-coded alternates like `Bone_Thigh_Left` or
         `bone-thigh-l` — the case and the underscore matter because
         external rig-test viewers and the renderer's Rig panel both
         match by exact string.
  [ ] 5. [PART 33.5.5] Each mesh part that the AI wants bound to the
         rig has a clear `userData.partName` (already PART 30.7's
         contract); the renderer uses partName to decide which meshes
         to skin. Without a partName, the renderer falls back to
         "skin every mesh" — usually what you want, but explicit is
         safer.
  [ ] 6. [PART 33.5.5] The blueprint is loadable on the JSON/JS path
         without errors (PART 65's existing 18-item checklist still
         applies — this checklist is layered on top, not a
         replacement). The renderer surfaces a spec-chip warning if
         `validateWeights()` returns non-empty after build, so the
         AI should expect to see "skinning max error" warnings in
         the chip's tooltip if the geometry doesn't fit the rig
         cleanly.

If a blueprint does not declare userData.rigGraph at all, this
checklist is a no-op — don't add fake entries to satisfy the form.
Blueprints that don't need it (i.e. most low-poly cases) skip 34
entirely.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
34.7 — RUNTIME CONTRACT (what the renderer does when it sees
        userData.rigGraph)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The renderer's `window.lblSpec.refreshOnLoad()` (in index.html, the
RJS UPDATE 3 block) now performs three rig-aware behaviors on every
load — these are observable by any blueprint that exports PART 34's
contract, and inert for every other blueprint:

  (a) VALIDATOR E11 (rig helpers marker check)
      If `root.userData.rigGraph` is present but
      `root.userData.skeletonSource !== 'three-rig-helpers'`, the
      renderer surfaces a non-blocking warning via the spec chip —
      "Rig graph declared but skeletonSource is missing or not
      'three-rig-helpers'". This is a STRICT check: the only
      sanctioned source is the pre-bundled helpers. A blueprint that
      wants a SkinnedMesh via PART 30.7's auto-promotion + per-part
      skin does NOT also declare userData.rigGraph — pick one path
      or the other.

  (b) RIG BUTTON (topbar, next to the Sockets button)
      If `root.userData.rigGraph` is present AND
      `root.userData.skeletonSource === 'three-rig-helpers'`, the
      renderer enables a new Rig button in the topbar (right after
      the Sockets button, before Recipes). Clicking it opens a panel
      listing every joint: id, parent id, and the rest position
      (read from the rigGraph on every open). The panel re-validates
      the graph with `validateRigGraph()` on every open and surfaces
      any errors as a red banner above the joint list. The validator
      runs CLIENT-SIDE — the bundle is loaded once at page load via
      the import map and the helpers are available globally.

  (c) GLB EXPORT OPTIMIZATION (optional, buildSkinnedHierarchyForExport)
      The existing `buildSkinnedHierarchyForExport` (index.html line
      ~14069, see PART 30.7) is the renderer's auto Bone-promotion +
      per-part skin pass. If the loaded model ALREADY has a real
      SkinnedMesh with skinIndex + skinWeight from PART 34, the
      export pass detects that and is a no-op for those meshes (no
      need to re-bind what the renderer already bound correctly). The
      optimization is internal — the export button is unchanged, no
      new button, no new workflow.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
34.8 — CROSS-REFERENCES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 34 layers on top of, never replaces:

  - PART 4.1 / 4.2 (the master spec's no-relative-import rule, the
    single-file rule). PART 34 is the one explicit exception; the
    rest of PART 4 still applies.
  - PART 30 (rigging & animation authoring standard) — the default
    pivot-hierarchy approach is still the right answer for ~99% of
    cases. Reach for 34 only when 30's auto-promotion + single-bone-
    per-part rigid skin is insufficient.
  - PART 30.2 — bone naming rule. Every joint id must be alphanumeric
    + underscore and (for humanoids / bipeds / mechs) reuse
    STANDARD_BONES from PART 33.1[10].
  - PART 30.7 — the auto-promotion + per-part skin pass is the
    renderer's existing skeleton export path. PART 34 is a strictly
    more powerful alternative for the 1% of cases that need it.
  - PART 32 error [2] — track binding. Every animated bone still
    needs an explicit, alphanumeric .name. The Rig panel's joint
    list reads the same .name strings.
  - PART 65.4 (the JSON/JS structural reference checklist) — the 6
    items in 34.6 are an APPEND to that list, not a replacement.
  - PART 64 (character / mascot sculpt enrichment) — orthogonal. A
    blueprint that declares userData.rigGraph can still use any of
    PART 64's character fields.
  - PART 66.1[10] / 66.5 — the JSON/JS universal rigging standard
    + the STANDARD_BONES catalog. Reuse the canonical names; don't
    invent alternates.
  - PART 66.4 (the JSON/JS 8-item architectural checklist) —
    orthogonal. None of 66.4's items change.

PART 34 does NOT introduce:
  - Any new field that PART 30 / 64 / 66 doesn't already accept
  - Any new renderer behavior beyond (a) the E11 validator, (b) the
    Rig button, and (c) the export-path no-op described in 34.7
  - Any new dependency in the blueprint beyond the two new userData
    keys (rigGraph + skeletonSource)
  - Any change to the GLB export's user-visible behavior
  - Any requirement that the AI author code or import modules

================================================================================
END OF PART 34 — v8.3 ADDITIONS: PRE-BUNDLED RIG HELPERS (OPT-IN)
The escape hatch is opt-in and narrow: use PART 30 (pivot hierarchy +
auto-promotion) for every blueprint that doesn't need true SkinnedMesh
vertex skin weights; reach for PART 34 only when the decision table in
34.4 says YES. The 6-item checklist in 34.6 is MANDATORY for any
blueprint that declares userData.rigGraph (no-op for every other
blueprint). Nothing in this PART removes or weakens any rule from
PARTs 1-33 (TS) / 1-66 (JSON/JS).
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 35 — v1.12 / v8.4 ADDITIONS: TRIANGLE-FIRST GEOMETRY (TRIS)
The "everything is a triangle" mental model for the JSON/JS
blueprint DSL. Source: the attached WORKING_WITH_TRIANGLES guide.
Layers on top of PART 1 (blueprint schema), PART 2 (typed values),
PART 4 (no relative imports), PART 30 (rigging), PART 32 / PART 65
(canonical helpers — already shipped an `inspectModelTriangles` stub;
PART 35 EXTENDS that stub with a per-geometry breakdown), and PART
34 / PART 34.R (rig helpers). The renderer gains a new `🔺 Tris`
topbar button + E12 validator check that surfaces the triangle /
vertex / draw-call counts of the currently-loaded blueprint.
================================================================================

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.1 — EXECUTIVE OVERVIEW: HOW TRIS WORK IN GPU RASTERIZATION
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
In WebGL, WebGPU, OpenGL, and DirectX, the fundamental unit of
every 3D surface is a TRIANGLE (a "tri"). Every Three.js geometry
(`BoxGeometry`, `CylinderGeometry`, `SphereGeometry`, ...) is just
a collection of triangles stored inside a `THREE.BufferGeometry`.

A single triangle requires:
  - 3 VERTICES — points in 3D space [X, Y, Z].
  - 1 NORMAL   — surface-facing direction [NX, NY, NZ], for lighting.
  - 3 UVs      — 2D texture-mapping coordinates [U, V].

When building procedural blueprints, the renderer already does the
COMPOSE & MERGE workflow automatically — each `Box` / `Cylinder` /
`Sphere` primitive in the blueprint is converted to triangles at
build time, and the entire model is rendered as a single Group of
one or more `THREE.Mesh` objects.

PART 35 adds a fifth blueprint-level tool — `meta.trisBudget` — that
lets a blueprint declare its intended triangle budget upfront, plus
runtime hooks for blueprints that want raw triangle-level control
(armor plates, decals, sci-fi cockpits) via the existing
`blueprint.parts[].customGeometry` field, now extended to accept
either an indexed or a non-indexed raw triangle buffer.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.2 — METHOD 1: MERGING COMPOSED SHAPES INTO UNIFIED TRIANGLE BUFFERS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The renderer ALREADY merges every part's underlying triangles into
one BufferGeometry at build time when the part contains 2+ primitive
shapes (PART 30.7's "auto-collapse small parts" rule). PART 35 makes
this an explicit, AI-controllable behavior via the new
`mergeGeometries` blueprint directive.

New blueprint DSL (additive — no existing field changes):
  {
    "id": "MechLeg_v2",
    "mergeGeometries": true,        // PART 35.2: collapse this part
                                    // into ONE BufferGeometry.
    "shapes": [
      { "type": "box",    "w": 0.18, "h": 0.22, "d": 1.20, "offset": [0, 0, 0.60] },
      { "type": "box",    "w": 0.20, "h": 0.05, "d": 1.15, "offset": [0, 0.12, 0.60] },
      { "type": "cylinder","r": 0.04, "h": 0.80, "segments": 8, "offset": [0, -0.08, 0.50], "rotateX": 1.5708 },
      { "type": "cylinder","r": 0.12, "h": 0.22, "segments": 12, "offset": [0, 0, 1.20], "rotateZ": 1.5708 }
    ],
    "material": "SteelOrangeFlat"
  }

Default behavior (no `mergeGeometries` key): the renderer preserves
the existing per-shape sub-mesh structure. PART 35.2 only forces
collapse when the blueprint EXPLICITLY says so.

Benefits:
  - Performance: reduces N draw calls per part to 1.
  - Single transform, single material slot per merged part.
  - Cleaner GLB / JSON export (one mesh per part instead of N).

RULE: prefer `mergeGeometries: true` whenever a single body part has
2+ primitive shapes. The renderer's PART 30.7 auto-collapse pass
detects that a part has multiple children and emits a soft warning
otherwise (E12 below).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.3 — METHOD 2: CONSTRUCTING CUSTOM GEOMETRY TRIANGLE-BY-TRIANGLE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Use this for armor plates, blades, sci-fi cockpits, mechanical
claws, or any shape whose topology you want exact control over.
The existing `blueprint.parts[].customGeometry` field (introduced in
PART 22) is extended in PART 35 to accept both indexed and non-
indexed raw triangle buffers.

----- 35.3.A — Non-Indexed Triangles (3 verts per triangle) -----

Each triangle is 3 sequential points in the position array. Triangle
0 = vertices [0,1,2]; triangle 1 = [3,4,5]; etc.

  {
    "id": "KneeBlade",
    "customGeometry": {
      "type": "nonIndexed",
      "positions": [
        // Triangle 1: Upper Right Face
         0.0,  0.75,  0.0,   // Apex tip
         0.18, 0.0,   0.08,  // Mid right
         0.0,  0.0,   0.25,  // Mid center ridge

        // Triangle 2: Upper Left Face
         0.0,  0.75,  0.0,
         0.0,  0.0,   0.25,
        -0.18, 0.0,   0.08,

        // Triangle 3: Lower Right Bevel
         0.0,  0.0,   0.25,
         0.18, 0.0,   0.08,
         0.0, -0.45,  0.05,

        // Triangle 4: Lower Left Bevel
         0.0,  0.0,   0.25,
         0.0, -0.45,  0.05,
        -0.18, 0.0,   0.08,

        // Triangle 5: Back Base Top
         0.0,  0.75,  0.0,
        -0.18, 0.0,   0.08,
         0.18, 0.0,   0.08,

        // Triangle 6: Back Base Bottom
        -0.18, 0.0,   0.08,
         0.0, -0.45,  0.05,
         0.18, 0.0,   0.08
      ],
      "computeNormals": true   // renderer calls geom.computeVertexNormals()
    },
    "material": "SteelOrangeFlat"
  }

----- 35.3.B — Indexed Triangles (shared vertices + index array) -----

Indexed geometry stores unique vertices ONCE and defines triangles
via an index array. Use this when the same vertex position is shared
by multiple triangles (5 verts → 6 triangles in the example below
vs 18 verts for the same shape non-indexed).

  {
    "id": "Pyramid",
    "customGeometry": {
      "type": "indexed",
      "positions": [
         0.0,  0.5,  0.0,   // 0: Top Tip
        -0.5,  0.0, -0.5,   // 1: Back Left
         0.5,  0.0, -0.5,   // 2: Back Right
         0.5,  0.0,  0.5,   // 3: Front Right
        -0.5,  0.0,  0.5    // 4: Front Left
      ],
      "indices": [
        0, 4, 3,   // Front
        0, 3, 2,   // Right
        0, 2, 1,   // Back
        0, 1, 4,   // Left
        1, 2, 3,
        1, 3, 4
      ],
      "computeNormals": true
    },
    "material": "Sandstone"
  }

DECISION TABLE (35.3.A vs 35.3.B):
  - 35.3.A (non-indexed): use when each triangle is its own
    independent face (decals, splinters, floating armor panels,
    anything with hard edges + flatShading: true).
  - 35.3.B (indexed):     use when vertices are shared (spheres,
    cylinders, organic curves, any surface that smooth-shades).

The renderer auto-detects the type from the presence of the `indices`
array. The default `customGeometry.type` (when omitted) is
`"nonIndexed"` for back-compat with PART 22.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.4 — METHOD 3: LOW-POLY FLAT SHADING & SEGMENT OPTIMIZATION
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

----- 35.4.A — Flat Shading Material -----

To render distinct, faceted low-poly planes with crisp edge
highlights, set `flatShading: true` on the material:

  "materials": {
    "SteelOrangeFlat": {
      "type":       "MeshStandardMaterial",
      "color":      0xe65c00,
      "metalness":  0.25,
      "roughness":  0.40,
      "flatShading": true        // sharp, visible triangle facets
    }
  }

----- 35.4.B — Controlling Triangle Density on Primitives -----

When using procedural Three.js shapes, adjust radial + height
segments to target specific triangle budgets. The blueprint's
`segments` field on cylinders / spheres / cones takes the same
value the underlying Three.js constructor does:

  Low-Poly Barrel / Piston:
    { "type": "cylinder", "r": 0.05, "h": 1.2, "segments": 6 }
    -> 6 radial = 12 side tris + 8 cap tris = 20 tris.

  Low-Poly Cockpit / Torso Sphere:
    { "type": "sphere", "r": 0.6, "widthSeg": 8, "heightSeg": 6 }
    -> 8 × 6 = 48 tris.

  Low-Poly Quadruped Foot Talon / Spike:
    { "type": "cone", "r": 0.12, "h": 0.35, "segments": 4 }
    -> 4 radial = 4 side tris + 2 base tris = 6 tris.

  Low-Poly Turret Base:
    { "type": "cylinder", "rTop": 0.6, "rBot": 0.7, "h": 0.25, "segments": 8 }
    -> 8 radial = 16 side + 12 cap (top) + 12 cap (bottom) = 40 tris.

----- 35.4.C — The `meta.trisBudget` Declarative Soft-Cap -----

A blueprint can declare an intended triangle budget. The renderer's
E12 validator reads this and surfaces a soft warning if the actual
count exceeds the declared budget by more than 20%:

  {
    "meta": {
      "trisBudget": 12000,       // intended triangle count
      "trisBudgetTolerance": 0.2 // 20% over is OK (default)
    }
  }

The renderer's E12 ALSO surfaces a warning if `totalTriangles >
50,000` regardless of the blueprint's declared budget (PART 35's
own global soft cap — 60 FPS on mid-range hardware).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.5 — METHOD 4: TRIANGLE COUNT CALCULATOR & REAL-TIME MESH INSPECTOR
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
This is the EXTENSION of PART 65.2's `inspectModelTriangles` stub.
PART 65 shipped a small helper that returned { triangles, meshes,
materials, bones, clips }. PART 35 extends it with a richer per-
geometry breakdown that the 🔺 Tris panel uses.

  {
    "triangles":    number,    // PART 65 (back-compat)
    "meshes":       number,    // PART 65 (back-compat)
    "materials":    number,    // PART 65 (back-compat)
    "bones":        number,    // PART 65 (back-compat)
    "clips":        number,    // PART 65 (back-compat)
    "totalTriangles": number,  // PART 35 (new)
    "totalVertices":  number,  // PART 35 (new)
    "drawCalls":      number,  // PART 35 (new) = mesh count for non-instanced
    "geometries": [             // PART 35 (new) — per-mesh breakdown
      {
        "name":      "string",
        "triangles": "number",
        "vertices":  "number",
        "indexed":   "boolean"
      }
    ]
  }

The function is available at runtime via
`window.lblSpec.helpers.inspectModelTriangles(rootGroup)`. The
function signature is unchanged from PART 65; the return shape is
extended (additive — the 5 original fields are preserved for any
existing PART 65 reader).

Blueprints that need metrics on a per-frame basis (e.g. an LOD
auto-switcher) read `window.lblSpec.lastTriMetrics` after every
`buildScene()`.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.6 — METHOD 5: RIGGING & GLB / JSON EXPORT WITH TRIANGLE MESHES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
When using merged or custom triangle meshes with an animated bone
armature:

  1. BONE PARENTING. The existing `attachTo` field on each part
     (PART 30.2) handles this — every merged / raw-triangle part
     declares which `Bone_*` pivot it belongs to. The renderer
     auto-collapses each bone's children into a single Mesh when
     the `mergeGeometries: true` flag (35.2) is set on at least
     one child.

  2. WORLD MATRIX UPDATE. The renderer's `buildScene()` already
     calls `rootGroup.updateMatrixWorld(true)` after the build
     completes. No blueprint-side action needed.

  3. GLTF / GLB / JSON EXPORT. When exported via the renderer's
     existing export pipeline (GLB / OBJ / STL / USDZ buttons),
     all triangle indices, vertex attributes (position + normal +
     uv), and parent node transforms are baked into the output.
     The `inspectModelTriangles` count is the LAST line of the
     renderer's export toast, so the user can confirm what
     shipped.

PART 35 does NOT introduce a new export path. It documents the
correct order of operations for the EXISTING path: build tris →
parent to bone → renderer auto-updates world matrix → export.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.7 — REFERENCE: DROP-IN LOW-POLY TURRET (JSON/JS blueprint form)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A drop-in blueprint part that builds a low-poly turret using ALL of
Methods 1-3 from above. Useful as a starting point for mech /
vehicle / sci-fi props. Ships with both the merged-triangle path
(35.2) and the raw-triangle blade path (35.3).

  {
    "id": "Turret_v2",
    "mergeGeometries": true,        // PART 35.2
    "shapes": [
      { "type": "cylinder", "rTop": 0.6, "rBot": 0.7, "h": 0.25,
        "segments": 8, "material": "SteelOrangeFlat" },
      { "type": "sphere",   "r": 0.45, "widthSeg": 8, "heightSeg": 6,
        "offset": [0, 0.20, 0], "material": "SteelOrangeFlat" },
      { "type": "box",      "w": 0.12, "h": 0.14, "d": 0.90,
        "offset": [0, 0.22, 0.45], "material": "GunmetalDark" }
    ],
    "attachTo": "Bone_TorsoTop"
  }

  "materials": {
    "SteelOrangeFlat": {
      "type":        "MeshStandardMaterial",
      "color":       0xe65c00,
      "metalness":   0.25,
      "roughness":   0.40,
      "flatShading": true
    },
    "GunmetalDark": {
      "type":        "MeshStandardMaterial",
      "color":       0x222222,
      "metalness":   0.85,
      "roughness":   0.30
    }
  }

This pattern is exposed as a starter recipe via the renderer's
existing "📚 Recipes" button (PART 53.2's starter library) so
users can drop it in with one click.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.8 — PRE-EMISSION CHECKLIST (append to PART 65.3's 16-item list)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Any unchecked item is a soft warning (E12 below) surfaced via the
🔺 Tris button.

  [ ] 17. [PART 35.2] Every body part with 2+ primitive shapes has
         `mergeGeometries: true` set. (The renderer's E12 surfaces a
         warning otherwise.)
  [ ] 18. [PART 35.3] `customGeometry` either uses 35.3.A
         (non-indexed, no `indices` field) OR 35.3.B (indexed, with
         an `indices` array). Mixed approaches in the same model are
         an anti-pattern.
  [ ] 19. [PART 35.4] Low-poly materials declare `"flatShading": true`
         when the intent is faceted; smooth-shaded organic models
         leave it off and use indexed geometry from 35.3.B.
  [ ] 20. [PART 35.4] Either `meta.trisBudget` is set AND actual
         `totalTriangles` is within `meta.trisBudgetTolerance` (default
         20%) of that value, OR `totalTriangles <= 50,000` (PART 35's
         own global soft cap).
  [ ] 21. [PART 35.6] Each animated part's `attachTo` field references
         an alphanumeric+underscore bone id matching the
         STANDARD_BONES catalog (PART 33.1[10] / 66.1[10]).
  [ ] 22. [PART 35.5] The renderer-side metrics on the 🔺 Tris panel
         match the AI's stated budget. (The user can click
         "Re-measure" to confirm; the panel is the source of truth.)

If a blueprint uses none of PART 35's methods (purely primitive
geometry, no merging, no raw tris, no `trisBudget`), this checklist
is a no-op — don't add fake entries to satisfy the form.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.9 — RUNTIME CONTRACT (what the renderer does)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The renderer's `window.lblSpec` object gains three PART 35-specific
behaviors on every load — these are observable by any blueprint
that uses PART 35's tools, and INERT for every other blueprint.

  (a) VALIDATOR E12 (triangle density + un-merged-part check)
      Runs on every `window.lblSpec.refreshOnLoad()` call. Reads
      `inspectModelTriangles(currentRoot)`. Surfaces a non-blocking
      warning via the spec chip when ANY of the following is true:
        - `totalTriangles > 50_000` ("Model uses N triangles
          (PART 35 soft cap: 50,000). Consider merging or
          decimating.")
        - A part has 2+ primitive shapes but `mergeGeometries` is
          not set / is false ("Part '<name>' has N sub-shapes
          (PART 35.2 prefers mergeGeometries: true).")
        - `meta.trisBudget` is set AND actual count exceeds
          `meta.trisBudget * (1 + meta.trisBudgetTolerance)`
          ("Triangle count N exceeds meta.trisBudget B (T% over).")
      Multiple warnings stack on the chip.

  (b) 🔺 TRIS BUTTON (topbar, between "Rig" and "Recipes")
      Enabled for EVERY loaded blueprint — there is no
      `userData.rigGraph` / `userData.skeletonSource` gate here,
      the metrics exist for every blueprint that has at least one
      Mesh. Clicking it opens a panel showing:
        - Total triangles, total vertices, mesh count (= draw calls)
        - Per-geometry breakdown table (name, triangles, vertices,
          indexed?) — sorted by triangle count descending
        - A "Copy JSON" button that puts the full metrics object on
          the clipboard (so the user can paste it back into a prompt
          when iterating on a blueprint)
        - A "Re-measure" button that re-runs inspectModelTriangles
          on the current scene (useful after live edits)
      The button is ALWAYS enabled (never `disabled`). Blueprints
      with zero meshes (an empty scene) get a "No meshes to inspect"
      placeholder.

  (c) lblSpec.helpers RE-EXPORTS
      The renderer exposes the following PART 35 functions on
      `window.lblSpec.helpers` so .ts factories (or any external
      runtime script) can use them without copying the body:
        - inspectModelTriangles(root): MeshTriangleMetrics
        - createUnifiedLimbSegment(bone, mat): THREE.Mesh
        - createLowPolyKneeBlade(): THREE.BufferGeometry
        - createIndexedPyramid():    THREE.BufferGeometry
        - buildLowPolyTurretWithTris(material): THREE.Mesh
      These are the SAME functions the TS-side spec (PART 35.5 /
      35.2 / 35.3.A / 35.3.B / 35.7) defines. The renderer's copy
      is the same one the 🔺 Tris panel uses.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.10 — CROSS-REFERENCES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 35 layers on top of, never replaces:

  - PART 1 (blueprint schema). 35.2's `mergeGeometries`, 35.3's
    extended `customGeometry.type`, and 35.4.C's `meta.trisBudget`
    are all NEW fields — no existing field changes meaning.
  - PART 2 (typed values). The `MeshTriangleMetrics` extension in
    35.5 keeps the original 5 fields from PART 65.2 and adds 4
    new ones — a back-compat additive change.
  - PART 4 (no relative imports). Blueprints never imported code;
    this rule is unaffected. The renderer-side `lblSpec.helpers`
    re-exports in 35.9(c) are the bridge for any .ts factory.
  - PART 22 (customGeometry). 35.3 EXTENDS the existing field with
    a `type` discriminator — the default is `"nonIndexed"` for back-
    compat.
  - PART 30 (rigging & animation). 35.6 documents the correct
    bone-parenting order for merged triangle meshes; reinforces —
    never replaces — PART 30.7's auto-collapse rule.
  - PART 33.1[10] / 66.1[10] (STANDARD_BONES catalog). 35.8 item
    21 references the canonical bone names.
  - PART 32 / PART 65 (canonical helpers). 35.5 EXTENDS PART 65.2's
    `inspectModelTriangles` with a per-geometry breakdown; the
    original 5-field shape is preserved.
  - PART 32.3 / PART 65.3 (16-item checklist). 35.8's 6 items
    APPEND to that list (17-22), not a replacement.
  - PART 33 / PART 66 (architectural features). 35.4.B's 50,000-tri
    soft cap is consistent with PART 33.1 / 66.1's existing
    complexity budgets.
  - PART 34 / PART 34.R (rig helpers). 35.6's "updateMatrixWorld"
    is the same call the renderer makes for the Rig panel to read
    correct rest positions.
  - PART 34.6 (6-item checklist). Orthogonal — none of PART 35's
    items overlap with PART 34's items.

PART 35 does NOT introduce:
  - Any new required field (every PART 35 field is OPT-IN with a
    safe default)
  - Any new export path (35.6 documents the EXISTING path, doesn't
    add a new path)
  - Any new render-time behavior beyond (a) the E12 validator,
    (b) the 🔺 Tris button, and (c) the `lblSpec.helpers` re-exports
    described in 35.9
  - Any removal or weakening of rules from PARTs 1-34 (TS) /
    1-66 (JSON/JS)

================================================================================
END OF PART 35 — v1.12 / v8.4 ADDITIONS: TRIANGLE-FIRST GEOMETRY (TRIS)
The "everything is a triangle" mental model is now first-class in
the LBL spec: 5 documented methods, 6-item pre-emission checklist,
1 new topbar button, 1 new validator, 5 renderer-side helpers on
`window.lblSpec.helpers`. Layers on top of PART 32 / PART 65's
existing `inspectModelTriangles` (extended, not replaced) and PART
30's rigging pipeline (reinforced, not replaced). Nothing in this
PART removes or weakens any rule from PARTs 1-34 (TS) / 1-66
(JSON/JS).
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 36 — v1.13 / v8.5 ADDITIONS: MULTI-FILE DROP + MODEL + RIG PAIRING
The renderer now accepts a FileList (or a multi-select file picker, or
a multi-file drag-and-drop) instead of only ever processing the first
file. The new behavior is opt-in by virtue of the user dropping more
than one file at a time — every single-file drop is unchanged. This
PART layers on top of PART 1 (single-file factory output contract),
PART 2 (types), PART 4 (imports), PART 32 / PART 65 (canonical helpers
+ validators), PART 34 (pre-bundled rig helpers), and PART 35
(triangle-first geometry). The renderer gains:
  - `multiple` attribute on the 4 file inputs (file-input, file3d-input,
    dz-input, mob-dz-input) so the browser's file picker accepts a batch
  - a `lblRouteFiles(fileList)` dispatcher that buckets each File by
    extension / MIME and routes to the right loader
  - a `__lblLoaded3DModels[]` array that holds every uploaded 3D model
    in the current session (was a single `loaded3dGroup` before)
  - a new "rig" file type — a .ts/.mts/.cts file that doesn't define a
    `create*Model` factory but DOES expose a `RigGraph` data structure
================================================================================

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.1 — EXECUTIVE OVERVIEW: WHAT CHANGES FOR AI-AUTHORED FACTORIES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
NOTHING. A single model.ts file dropped by the user behaves exactly as
it always has. A single blueprint.json file is unchanged. A single
GLB/OBJ/STL is unchanged. A single image-texture drop is unchanged.

What changes is ONLY when the user drops more than one file at once:

  1. 3D models (.glb / .gltf / .fbx / .obj / .stl / .dae / .usdz)
     - The first one anchors at the origin (matches the old behavior).
     - Every subsequent one is APPENDED to the right of the previously
       loaded 3D models, with a 1.0-unit gap, and the camera is
       refit to the combined bounding box of all loaded models.
     - The scene tree shows one row per loaded model.
     - Each row is independently selectable; the gizmo / inspector /
       sub-mesh highlight all honor the per-model selection.

  2. Images (.png / .jpg / .jpeg / .gif / .webp / .svg / .bmp / .avif)
     - Each image is applied as a texture to the currently selected
       part (or spawns a new textured plane if nothing is selected).
     - All images in the batch are processed.

  3. Blueprints + rigs (.json / .js / .ts / .mts / .cts)
     - Loaded sequentially (one finishes before the next starts) so
       a rig file in the batch can attach to a model file that loaded
       earlier in the same drop.
     - The first blueprint replaces the scene (matches the old
       behavior). Subsequent blueprints trigger a toast: "Multiple
       blueprint files detected — drop them one at a time to load
       them sequentially." (Merging two full scenes is intentionally
       out of scope for the drop path; the editor paste path is the
       supported workflow for that.)
     - A .ts file that fails the regular `create*Model` factory check
       is automatically retried as a RIG (see 36.2). If it also
       fails as a rig, the user gets a clear error toast.

The 6-item pre-emission checklist in 35.6 and the 34.6 PART 34
checklist are unchanged. No new contract is imposed on .ts factories
— every model you wrote for v1.12 / v8.4 still loads, still exports,
still renders. The new behavior is purely additive on the dropper.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.2 — THE "RIG" FILE TYPE: 4 SUPPORTED EXPORT SHAPES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A .ts file is treated as a RIG (instead of a model) when:

  (a) it has no `create*Model` factory (so the regular pipeline throws
      "No create*Model() export found in <file>"), AND
  (b) it exposes a `RigGraph` data structure in one of these 4 shapes:

  SHAPE 1 — default export is a RigGraph OBJECT:
    ```ts
    export default {
      joints: [
        { id: 'hips',  parentId: null,     restPosition: [0, 1, 0], restRotation: [0, 0, 0, 1] },
        { id: 'spine', parentId: 'hips',  restPosition: [0, 0.6, 0], restRotation: [0, 0, 0, 1] },
        { id: 'head',  parentId: 'spine', restPosition: [0, 0.5, 0], restRotation: [0, 0, 0, 1] }
      ]
    };
    ```

  SHAPE 2 — default export is a FUNCTION that returns a RigGraph:
    ```ts
    export default function makeRig() {
      return { joints: [/* ... */] };
    }
    ```

  SHAPE 3 — named export `rigGraph` is a RigGraph:
    ```ts
    export const rigGraph = { joints: [/* ... */] };
    ```

  SHAPE 4 — named getter function returns a RigGraph:
    ```ts
    export function getRigGraph() { return { joints: [/* ... */] }; }
    // also accepted: createRigGraph, buildRigGraph, getRig, createRig
    ```

A RigGraph is `{ joints: Array<{ id: string, parentId: string|null,
restPosition: [number, number, number], restRotation: [number, number,
number, number] }> }`. The exact same shape the bundled
`three-rig-helpers` library consumes (see PART 34 for the full type
definitions and the validation rules).

When a rig is detected, the renderer:

  1. compiles the .ts file through the same TS pipeline as models
  2. dynamically imports the compiled module
  3. extracts the RigGraph via one of the 4 shapes above
  4. validates it with `three-rig-helpers.validateRigGraph(rigGraph)`
     (throws with a clear error message on duplicate joint ids,
     dangling parents, non-root trees, etc.)
  5. calls `three-rig-helpers.buildSkeleton(rigGraph)` to get
     `{ root: Bone, bones: Map, skeleton: Skeleton, restWorldPositions }`
  6. attaches `root` to the most recently loaded TS model group
  7. sets `userData.rigGraph` and `userData.skeletonSource = 'three-rig-helpers'`
     (the existing 🦴 Rig button + PART 34 spec checks then enable
     automatically — no separate "rig was applied" UI to wire)
  8. toasts: `✓ Rig applied — N joints on "<model name>"`

If no TS model is currently loaded, the rig is QUEUED in
`window.__lblPendingRigs` and a toast says
`"Rig "<file>" queued — drop a model .ts next and it will attach
automatically"`. The queue is drained in `addTsModelToScene()` right
after `CURRENT_TS_MODEL` is assigned, so a rig dropped first + a model
dropped second still ends up with the rig attached.

The renderer re-applies a rig to a model that already has one (drops
the old rig root before adding the new one), so re-dropping a rig
file replaces the previous skeleton — no duplicate Bones.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.3 — WHAT A RIG FILE MUST NOT DO
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The PART 4 single-file / no-relative-imports rules apply to rig files
the same as they apply to model files. Specifically:

  - Imports of `'three'` and `'three/addons/...'` are allowed (the
    import map resolves them). No relative imports to other files in
    the same drop.
  - No `fetch()` / `XMLHttpRequest` / `import()` of any URL outside
    the import map. The rig file must be self-contained.
  - No browser globals beyond what a model file is allowed to touch.
  - No `document.createElement` / direct DOM manipulation — the
    renderer's UI updates are driven by the userData markers set
    by the attach step, not by the rig file itself.

If the rig file does any of these, the user gets a clear error toast.
The spec does not enforce this with a separate linter — the renderer's
existing PART 4 validators handle it on the model side, and the
dynamic-import failure surfaces naturally on the rig side.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.4 — MINIMAL WORKED EXAMPLE: a model.ts + rig.ts pair
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Drop these two files together (any order — model first or rig first
both work) and the rig attaches to the model automatically:

  // model.ts
  import * as THREE from 'three';

  export default function createCubeCharacter(opts = {}) {
    const g = new THREE.Group();
    g.name = 'CubeCharacter';

    const body = new THREE.Mesh(
      new THREE.BoxGeometry(1, 2, 0.6),
      new THREE.MeshStandardMaterial({ color: 0x88aaff, flatShading: true })
    );
    body.position.y = 1.0;
    body.castShadow = true; body.receiveShadow = true;
    g.add(body);

    const head = new THREE.Mesh(
      new THREE.BoxGeometry(0.7, 0.7, 0.7),
      new THREE.MeshStandardMaterial({ color: 0xffcc88, flatShading: true })
    );
    head.position.y = 2.35;
    head.castShadow = true; head.receiveShadow = true;
    g.add(head);

    g.userData.sculptRuntime = { tick: () => {} };
    return g;
  }

  // rig.ts — note: NO create*Model factory, so the dropper treats it
  //          as a rig and attaches it to the just-loaded model.
  export default {
    joints: [
      { id: 'hips', parentId: null,
        restPosition: [0, 1.0, 0], restRotation: [0, 0, 0, 1] },
      { id: 'spine', parentId: 'hips',
        restPosition: [0, 0.6, 0], restRotation: [0, 0, 0, 1] },
      { id: 'head', parentId: 'spine',
        restPosition: [0, 0.5, 0], restRotation: [0, 0, 0, 1] }
    ]
  };

What the user sees when they drop both:

  1. Toast: "Loading CubeCharacter.ts…"
  2. Model appears, centered on the origin, auto-camera-fit.
  3. Toast: "✓ Rig applied — 3 joints on CubeCharacter"
  4. The 🦴 Rig button (PART 34) becomes enabled — clicking it
     lists the 3-joint hierarchy.
  5. The skeleton is visible in the viewport (when "Show Skeleton"
     is on in the topbar).

Drop rig.ts alone first:

  1. Toast: 'Rig "rig.ts" queued — drop a model .ts next and it
     will attach automatically'
  2. Then drop model.ts:
  3. Toast: "Loading CubeCharacter.ts…"
  4. Toast: '✓ Queued rig "rig.ts" applied — 3 joints'

Drop model.ts + rig.ts + a 3D model (e.g. a GLB) in one batch:

  1. Model.ts loads → CURRENT_TS_MODEL set, rig attaches immediately.
  2. The GLB is APPENDED in a row to the right of the character
     (because the scene is non-empty when it drops). The camera
     refits to the combined bounding box.
  3. The rig only attaches to the TS model — the GLB is unaffected
     (PART 34 was the contract that introduces the rig pipeline,
     and it only applies to TS models with `userData.rigGraph`).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.5 — BACKWARDS COMPATIBILITY: WHAT THE SINGLE-FILE PATH DOES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The single-file path is the SAME dispatch as before — `lblRouteFiles`
short-circuits to `lblRouteDroppedFile` which is the original
"image → texture, anything else → readFile" branch. So:

  - 1 model.ts   → unchanged (loads, sets CURRENT_TS_MODEL)
  - 1 rig.ts     → NEW: tries model first, fails, tries rig,
                   succeeds, queues (or applies if a model is
                   already in the scene)
  - 1 GLB/OBJ/etc → unchanged
  - 1 image      → unchanged (textures the selection)
  - 1 JSON/JS blueprint → unchanged
  - N of anything → NEW: bucketed + dispatched as documented above

The `multiple` attribute on the 4 file inputs is a no-op for the
single-file case (browsers just behave the same), so the change is
fully backwards-compatible at the file-picker level too.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.6 — PRE-EMISSION CHECKLIST (extends the 35.6 + 34.6 lists)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The 6-item pre-emission checklist in 35.6 and the 6-item PART 34
checklist are unchanged. If you write a .ts file that exposes a
RigGraph (PART 36), add these 2 items to your personal mental
checklist (not part of the official gate — the renderer doesn't
enforce them, they're here so you don't ship a broken rig file):

  [PART-36.1] If your .ts file has NO `create*Model` factory, the
              dropper will try to load it as a RIG. Make sure one of
              the 4 SHAPES in 36.2 is present, otherwise the user
              gets a "no RigGraph found" error.
  [PART-36.2] Every joint in the `joints` array needs a UNIQUE `id`,
              exactly ONE joint with `parentId: null` (the root), and
              every non-root joint's `parentId` must point at an id
              that appears EARLIER in the array (topological order).
              `three-rig-helpers.validateRigGraph()` enforces these.

PART 36 does NOT introduce a new export path, a new mandatory contract,
or a new validator on .ts factories. The new behavior is purely the
dropper's — a .ts file either IS a model (and goes through the existing
PART 1 pipeline) or IS a rig (and goes through 36.2). No file is
forced to be one or the other; the user picks by which file they
choose to drop.

PART 36 layers on top of, never replaces:
  - PART 1 (TS output contract) — every model still has a factory
  - PART 2 (type safety) — rig exports are untyped or self-typed
  - PART 4 (no relative imports) — same rule for rig files
  - PART 30 (rigging) — auto-promotion still happens for blueprints
  - PART 32 / PART 65 (canonical helpers) — extended, not replaced
  - PART 34 (rig helpers) — rig files use the same `three-rig-helpers`
  - PART 35 (tris inspector) — multi-model scenes still report their
    combined triangle budget via the 🔺 Tris button

PART 36 does NOT introduce:
  - A new mandatory field on any existing userData namespace
  - A new top-level API surface (no `lblAttachRig` global — the
    queue + drain pattern is the only public hook)
  - A new pre-emission gate or validator item
  - A change to the GLB / OBJ / STL / FBX / DAE / USDZ loader
  - A change to the editor paste path (still single-file at a time)
  - A change to the Demo / Model Store (still one .ts per click)

================================================================================
END OF PART 36 — v1.13 / v8.5 ADDITIONS: MULTI-FILE DROP + MODEL + RIG PAIRING
Multi-file drop is now first-class: the user can pick N files at once
or drop N files in one drag, the dropper buckets them by extension /
MIME, and 3D models get arranged in a row + camera-refit while
blueprints + rigs process sequentially. A new "rig" file type lets a
model.ts + rig.ts pair render together with zero changes to either
file (just write the rig as `export default { joints: [...] }` and
drop them in any order). Every existing single-file workflow is
preserved. The new behavior is purely additive on the dropper and
the renderer state — no new contract, no new validator, no new
mandatory field. Layers on top of PART 1, PART 2, PART 4, PART 30,
PART 32 / PART 65, PART 34, PART 35. Nothing in this PART removes or
weakens any rule from PARTs 1-35.
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 37 — v1.14 / v8.6 ADDITIONS: ZIP BUNDLE SUPPORT
The renderer accepts a `.zip` file (single file, drop, drag, or
served from the demo / model-store URL) as a "bundle" of model +
rig + any other files, and extracts it in-memory before routing
each entry through the same multi-file drop pipeline from PART 36.
The user can ship one zip per model (e.g. `Model_1.zip`) instead
of juggling separate files. This PART layers on top of PART 1
(single-file factory output contract), PART 2 (types), PART 4
(imports), PART 30 (rigging), PART 32 / PART 65 (canonical
helpers + validators), PART 34 (pre-bundled rig helpers), PART 35
(triangle-first geometry), and PART 36 (multi-file drop + model +
rig pairing). The renderer gains:
  - `fflate` in the import map (10 KB minified, no deps, MIT)
  - `multiple` attribute on the 4 file inputs extended with `.zip`
  - a new "zip" file-type bucket in the dispatcher
  - `__lblExtractZip(file)` that turns a zip into a list of File
    objects ready for the existing routing pipeline
  - `__lblLooksLikeZip(bytes)` magic-byte check (`PK\x03\x04`)
  - `__lblFetchBytes(url)` for the demo / model-store path
  - `.zip` accepted in the demo manifest + the legacy probe
  - the model-store "Download Model Code" button re-serves the
    original zip bytes verbatim (no inner-model extraction)
================================================================================

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.1 — EXECUTIVE OVERVIEW: WHAT A BUNDLE LOOKS LIKE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A bundle is just a regular `.zip` archive. The renderer does not
impose a particular layout, but the conventional / recommended shape
matches the demo / model-store naming and the PART 36 model + rig
pairing contract:

  Model_1.zip                  (or any *.zip — the file's basename is
                                irrelevant, only the contents matter)
  ├── model.ts                 (REQUIRED — the main model factory.
                                Can also be named model.js / model.json
                                if you prefer the JSON/JS blueprint
                                path. Whichever file the parser
                                accepts as a model goes first.)
  ├── rig.ts                   (OPTIONAL — PART 36's 4 export shapes.
                                Auto-attaches to the just-loaded
                                model exactly as if you'd dropped
                                it standalone.)
  ├── extra.glb                (OPTIONAL — appended in a row the
                                same way a multi-file GLB drop would.)
  ├── texture.png              (OPTIONAL — applied as a texture to
                                the selection, same as a regular
                                image drop.)
  ├── README.md                (OPTIONAL — informational only; the
                                renderer doesn't try to render it
                                but lists it in the extraction toast
                                so the user knows it's there.)
  └── <any other asset>        (OPTIONAL — anything the import map
                                can route is processed; unknown
                                extensions are passed through as
                                blueprint text last-resort, same
                                as a regular drop.)

The renderer filters out macOS resource-fork junk (`__MACOSX/.*`),
hidden dotfiles (`.DS_Store`, `._*`), and directory markers. Every
other entry becomes a File object and goes through `lblRouteFiles`.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.2 — DROP, FILE-PICKER, AND DRAG-AND-DROP BEHAVIOR
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A `.zip` is now a first-class file type in the dropper. All 4 file
inputs (`file-input`, `file3d-input`, `dz-input`, `mob-dz-input`)
include `.zip` in their `accept` attribute, and the dropzone
sub-text reads "drop a .zip with model.ts + rig.ts" so the user
knows the feature exists. Drop / pick / drag behavior:

  1. Single .zip dropped   → `__lblExtractZip` runs, the extracted
                              files re-enter `lblRouteFiles`, which
                              fans them out as if they'd been dropped
                              individually. The user sees:
                              "✓ Extracted N files from Model_1.zip"
  2. .zip + other files     → Zips are processed FIRST (so the
                              extraction completes before the other
                              files start), then the non-zip files
                              go through the existing bucket dispatch.
  3. Multiple .zips         → Extracted sequentially (the second zip
                              waits for the first to finish) so a
                              model.ts in zip A + a rig.ts in zip B
                              still wire up via `__lblPendingRigs`.

The full extraction → routing path is:

  ┌─ drop / pick zip
  │
  ├─ __lblClassifyFile → 'zip' bucket
  │
  ├─ async __lblExtractZip(file):
  │     unzip() via fflate
  │     filter out __MACOSX, .DS_Store, dotfiles
  │     text files → File(text); binary files → File(uint8)
  │     return { files, entries, skipped, zipName }
  │
  ├─ toast "Extracted N files from <zipName>"
  │
  └─ lblRouteFiles(extracted) — recurse with the FileList
        ├─ model.ts   → validateAndBuild →  CURRENT_TS_MODEL set
        ├─ rig.ts     → __lblReadBlueprintOrRig → rig attaches
        ├─ extra.glb  → load3DFile (append if scene non-empty)
        ├─ texture    → lblApplyImageFileToSelected
        └─ README.md  → classified 'blueprint' last-resort;
                        try-compile fails with a parse error that
                        shows up as a non-blocking toast (the
                        model + rig still load — README.md is the
                        user's documentation, not source code)

If `__lblExtractZip` itself throws (corrupt zip, password-protected,
unsupported compression method), the user sees a clear error toast
with the underlying message and the rest of the batch is unaffected.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.3 — DEMO / MODEL-STORE BEHAVIOR
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The demo system (Demo / Model Store) is also zip-aware. A demo
author can ship a single `public/models/Model_1.zip` and the
renderer will:

  1. Discover it via the manifest:
     { "id": "Model_1", "file": "Model_1.zip" }
     (the loader accepts .zip alongside .ts / .js / .json)

  2. Or discover it via the legacy probe (which now tries
     `Model_1.zip` after `Model_1.ts` / `.js` / `.json`).

  3. On click, fetch the zip, check the `PK\x03\x04` magic bytes
     (so a stale 404 HTML page isn't mistaken for a zip), then
     extract + route through the same multi-file dispatch.

  4. The "Download Model Code" button re-serves the **original
     zip bytes verbatim** — what you download is what you
     uploaded, not a reconstructed model.ts. This is important
     for a demo workflow: if the user finds a bug in a model
     bundle, they can fix the source files in the zip, re-upload
     to the repo, and the next demo load serves back exactly
     the same archive. The download goes through the existing
     `lblLastDemoSource.bytes` path, no new button needed.

The legacy probe prefers .ts over .zip when both exist for the
same Model_N, so a repo with `Model_1.ts` keeps using the source
editor flow and a repo with only `Model_1.zip` (no source) still
loads. To FORCE zip mode, list the .zip in the manifest.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.4 — WHAT A BUNDLE FILE MUST NOT DO
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The PART 4 single-file / no-relative-imports rules apply to every
.ts / .js / .json file inside a bundle, exactly as if they'd been
dropped individually:

  - No relative imports to OTHER FILES IN THE SAME BUNDLE. The
    extraction produces flat File objects, and the TS pipeline
    rewrites imports through the import map — `./rig` inside a
    bundle does NOT resolve to the bundle's rig.ts.
  - Imports of `'three'`, `'three/addons/...'`, `'three-bvh-csg'`,
    `'three-rig-helpers'`, `'fflate'` (and any future import-map
    entries) are allowed.

If you need to share helpers between model.ts and rig.ts inside
a bundle, the supported workaround is:

  1. Inline the helper functions into each file (the spec already
     encourages this — the LBL spec is a self-contained-output
     format, see PART 4.1 "no relative imports").
  2. OR factor the helper into a third file, write a tiny build
     step that concatenates everything into one model.ts at
     deploy time, and ship the .ts in the demo store instead
     of the .zip.

The renderer does NOT enforce this with a separate linter — the
existing PART 4 validators handle it on the model side, and the
dynamic-import failure surfaces naturally on the rig side.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.5 — BACKWARDS COMPATIBILITY
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A bundle is purely additive. Every existing workflow is preserved:

  - Single .ts / .js / .json / .glb / .obj / .stl / .fbx / .dae /
    image drop → unchanged.
  - Multi-file drop with model + rig → unchanged (PART 36 still
    works exactly as before, zips are just one more file type
    that the bucket dispatch recognizes).
  - Demo / model-store with .ts / .js / .json → unchanged.
  - Editor paste path → unchanged (still single-file at a time).
  - "Download Model Code" button → serves .ts / .js / .json as
    text (unchanged) AND zip as `application/zip` (new — only
    when the source was a zip).

The `accept` attribute change on the 4 file inputs (added `.zip`)
is a no-op for users who don't have zips — the browser's file
picker shows zip files alongside the other supported extensions
but the user can still pick a non-zip file and the existing flow
takes over.

The `__lblClassifyFile` change (added the 'zip' bucket) is also
a no-op for non-zip files. The bucket only triggers when the file
extension is `.zip`, and the extracted File objects are re-classified
in the recursive `lblRouteFiles` call with their original
extensions.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.6 — PRE-EMISSION CHECKLIST (extends the 36.6 / 35.6 / 34.6 lists)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The 6-item pre-emission checklist in 35.6, the 6-item PART 34
checklist, and the 2-item PART 36 voluntary checklist are all
unchanged. If you ship a bundle (PART 37), add these 2 voluntary
items to your personal mental checklist (not enforced by any
validator — the renderer doesn't introspect zip layouts):

  [PART-37.1] The bundle contains at least one .ts / .js / .json
              file that defines a renderable scene. An empty zip
              gets a "contained no renderable files" error.
  [PART-37.2] The bundle's .ts / .js / .json files do NOT use
              relative imports to other files in the same zip.
              The import map doesn't know about zip-internal
              paths. (See 37.4 for the supported workarounds.)

PART 37 does NOT introduce a new mandatory field, a new export
path, a new validator, or a new API surface. The new behavior is
purely a new input format on the dropper + a new recognized
extension in the demo / model-store. No .ts factory is required
to know about zips — the renderer handles extraction transparently.

PART 37 layers on top of, never replaces:
  - PART 1 (TS output contract) — every bundle's model still has a factory
  - PART 2 (type safety) — same TypeScript rules
  - PART 4 (no relative imports) — applies to zip contents
  - PART 30 (rigging) — auto-promotion still happens for blueprints
  - PART 32 / PART 65 (canonical helpers) — extended, not replaced
  - PART 34 (rig helpers) — bundles can include rig.ts that uses
    'three-rig-helpers' exactly like a standalone rig file
  - PART 35 (tris inspector) — bundle-loaded models still report
    their triangle budget via the 🔺 Tris button
  - PART 36 (multi-file drop) — the zip extraction feeds into the
    same `lblRouteFiles` dispatcher

PART 37 does NOT introduce:
  - A new mandatory field on any existing userData namespace
  - A new top-level API surface (no global `lblLoadZipFromUrl` —
    the demo flow uses the existing `lblModelStoreLoadByNumber`)
  - A change to the GLB / OBJ / STL / FBX / DAE loader
  - A change to the editor paste path
  - A change to the file-picker UI (just an `accept` attr extension)
  - A change to the scene tree or the Rig button
  - A dependency on a non-import-mapped module (fflate IS in
    the import map, same access pattern as the other deps)

================================================================================
END OF PART 37 — v1.14 / v8.6 ADDITIONS: ZIP BUNDLE SUPPORT
The renderer now accepts a .zip anywhere a file can enter the
system: drop, file-picker, drag, or the demo / model-store URL.
The bundle is extracted in-memory (no disk write), filtered for
junk files, and every entry is routed through the same multi-
file dispatcher from PART 36 — model.ts loads, rig.ts attaches,
extra GLBs append, images texture, READMEs surface as non-blocking
toast. The raw zip bytes are preserved so "Download Model Code"
re-serves the exact same archive the user loaded. Single-file
workflows are unchanged. Layers on top of PART 1, PART 2, PART
4, PART 30, PART 32 / PART 65, PART 34, PART 35, PART 36. Nothing
in this PART removes or weakens any rule from PARTs 1-36.
================================================================================
